Index and grouping: the index protocol and factory, key factorizing, the float rules | Core, NumPy | `LabelIndex` on each label array, built lazily and cached; `labels.py`; `Groups` with cached codes, counts, and blocks; `grouped.py` | The protocol and factory, the regular-grid index, key transforms as records (below) |
| 3. Planner and executor: records an expression graph and runs it, optionally chunk by chunk | 1–2 | Expression trees (`expr.py`, formulas), executed eagerly through the backend methods; lazy values on the xarray backend through Dask | The planner (decision 10) |
| 4. Verbs: names and labels only, never axis positions | 1–3 | The verbs; `slice` takes 1-based positions because positions are the user's concept there | None |
| 5. Public API | 4 | `tidy3a` | None |

Who touches NumPy: the notes say layers 1 and 2 only. The executor's kernels must as well (ufuncs, `take`, the grouped kernels); verbs, planner, and API stay NumPy-free. Today the expression evaluator and `verbs.py` still call NumPy in places (`np.result_type` for write-back promotion, `np.where`, the rounding hint in `align.py`); A5 moves those behind the backend.

The index protocol: labels to positions, ranges to positions, and the `unique` and sorted flags, with a factory that inspects a label array once and picks the implementation; indexes built lazily and cached on the label array, as now. Implementations: sorted labels through `searchsorted` (exists); unsorted labels through one sort at build time (exists); a regular-grid index for evenly spaced labels such as gridded latitude, longitude, and time, which resolves labels and ranges arithmetically with no sort and no index memory (new, cheap, and the common case for the sample datasets); a multi-key index for composite labels (later; tidy3a keeps multi-level labels on xarray until then). Not in the protocol's NumPy-only implementations: a hash map for unique unsorted labels. A Python `dict` of 1M labels is slow to build and cannot be looked up vectorised; the fast hash is pandas (26 ms to factorize 10M keys, against 443 ms for `np.unique`), so hashing comes with pandas or a compiled kernel (track B), and the sort-once index is the unsorted case otherwise. Custom index classes can be registered for other label types, but the protocol carries the exactness obligations (a label `kind`, lookups through `exact_cast`), so a custom index cannot reintroduce `2**53 + 1 == 2**53`; that rule cost priority 1.

Key transforms: grouping in the core is on exact equality only, and domain meaning lives in transforms applied to the key before factorizing: binning (`bins()` with edges or a duration exists), rounding, spatial grid cells (the voxel workload), time windows. Built-in transforms are small records holding their settings (bin width and origin, cell size), so they print in the plan and in `groups:`, compare equal when their settings do, serve as cache keys, and give readable group labels, in the sorted order the output uses; plain functions and lambdas are accepted for quick use and are never equal to anything else. Tolerance-based rounding can split nearby values that straddle a bucket edge, so bucket boundaries are user-controllable and documented.

Corrections to the notes, from what was measured and built:

- `bincount` with weights for float sums is the "simple float64" kernel below: it gives 0 for `[2**53, 1, -2**53]`, whose sum is 1, and `reduceat` adds floats in a different order from `np.sum`. The grouping module needs the certified or a compensated kernel and the floating-point contract, not `bincount` alone; `bincount` is right for counts and for integer and boolean sums.
- A running key-to-code dictionary across chunks is a per-row Python loop, too slow for 90,000-group data, and assigns codes in arrival order while the output labels are sorted; chunked factorizing needs a vectorised hash per chunk, a merge, and a final relabelling (what Dask and flox do), and tree-combined float reductions are not bit-identical across chunk sizes (flox differed from per-group means in 14–15 of 32 groups).
- NaN keys: `group_by` on a value key with NaN is an error today ("filter them out first"); the notes' rule (all NaN one group, `dropna` choosing whether it is kept) is pandas' with `dropna=False`. It is a behaviour change (decision 11). Signed zero already groups as one value in pandas and NumPy; NumPy 2's `np.unique` also groups NaN together.
- The grouping bottleneck is measured, not suspected: at 10M points and 32 groups, finding groups 26 ms (pandas' hash), sorting 14, gathering the strided field 40, the 32 means 1; the many-small-groups case went from 4.8 s to 12 ms in priorities 2–3. What remains is memory traffic and hashing, which a planner does not change and columnar fields and compiled kernels do (track B).
- Not in the notes, learned the hard way: the exact label rule applies to lookups, alignment, and joins, not only grouping (kind and exact equality, exact casts, join type checks, repeated labels), and lives in layer 2; xarray stays the reference implementation in tests (an optional test dependency) even when it is no longer the foundation, since the property tests against it, the pandas oracle for labels, and `fsum` and exact rationals for reductions found nearly every bug in milestones 9–10; and the reproducibility contract says what is bit-identical and what is only repeatable, and across what.

#### Track A — the library

**Layers and API** (the package is `labelarray`, decision 2; class and function names are placeholders until A4–A5):

| Layer | From | Public surface |
|---|---|---|
| `labels` (layer 2) | `labels.py` and `LabelIndex` from `numpy_backend.py` | `kind`, `equal`, `compare`, `strict_subset`, `lossy_join_type`, `exact_cast`; the index protocol (`get_indexer(wanted)`, `slice_indexer(start, stop)`, `unique`, `sorted`), the `index_for(labels)` factory, `SortedIndex`, `SortOnceIndex`, `GridIndex`, `register_index(kind, cls)`; `LabelError` |
| `grouped` (layer 2) | `grouped.py`, `Groups` and `_factorize` from `expr.py`, plus the kernels from track B | `Grouping(codes, labels)` with `counts()`, `blocks()`; `factorize(values, transform=None, dropna=…)`; transforms `Bins(edges)`, `Duration(text)`, `Round(step, origin)`, `GridCells(size, origin)` as records; `reduce(values, op, grouping, axis, na_rm, min_count)`; `broadcast(stats, grouping)` |
| `array` (layer 1, with the operations of layer 3's eager executor) | `NumpyArray`, `NumpyData`, `_Labels`, the helpers | `Array(values, dims, labels, attrs, name)` with `__array_ufunc__` and `__array_function__` broadcasting by axis name, `sel`, `isel`, `transpose`, `rename`, `reduce`, `dot`, `concat`; `Collection` with `assign`, `select`, `keep`, `join(how)`, `nearest`, `to_dict`; `align(a, b)` returning the arrays ready for exact arithmetic and what was reordered; `AlignError`; `to_xarray`/`from_xarray` when xarray is installed |

tidy3a keeps the verbs, expressions, formulas, inspection output, the messages and hints, and a thin `NumpyBackend` adapter implementing its backend protocol with the library; its `Groups` wraps `Grouping`; its `LabelError` and `AlignError` inherit from both `TidyError` and the library's errors, so existing `except` clauses keep working.

**Steps**, each with a gate:

1. Write the backend interface down as a `typing.Protocol` in `backend.py` and check both backends against it in a test. Gate: the suite unchanged. This makes the boundary explicit before anything moves.
2. Move the grouping object out of `expr.py` into `grouped.py` (`Grouping`); `expr.Groups` becomes a subclass holding tidy3a's `axis` and `name`. Gate: the suite and the 65-case corpus unchanged.
3. Give the library its own errors; tidy3a's inherit from both. Gate: every test that matches an error message still passes.
4. Add the index protocol and factory with the regular-grid index, and key transforms as records with `bins()` as the first; add `__array_ufunc__` and `__array_function__` to the array, with broadcasting by axis name and the alignment rule applied (decision 3 says whether labels must match exactly or the library offers a looser mode). Gate: `np.sqrt(array)`, `array + other`, and `np.where` give the same values as `backend.apply`, checked on both backends by the property tests.
5. Move the verb-shaped methods into the adapter and name the public API; document the array, the collection, and the rules in the library's own README. Gate: `numpy_backend.py` imports nothing from tidy3a but the adapter does.
6. Split the tests: the pandas-oracle label tests, the bit-identity and certified-kernel tests, and `test_numpy_labels.py` go with the library; the xarray-equivalence, corpus, and contract tests stay in tidy3a as its integration suite. Gate: both suites pass on current and minimum dependencies.
7. Separate distribution, with tidy3a depending on it at a pinned minimum version; tidy3a's `pip install tidy3a` then installs NumPy and the library. Gate: the wheel checks in CI (plain, and with the xarray extra) pass.

Steps 1–3 are small (a day), change no behaviour, and are worth doing whether or not the library ships: they remove the hidden coupling. Steps 4–7 are the library proper, with the grid index and the transforms adding perhaps a day: a few days of work, an API design pass, and from then on two packages to version and release. The risk is API churn in the library while tidy3a's verbs still change; tidy3a's suite, run against the library at every change, is what catches it.

Step A1 status (2026-10-25): done. `Backend` in `backend.py` is a `runtime_checkable` protocol of 64 methods in eight sections, each with a one-line contract, generated from the xarray backend's signatures and then written up. `tests/test_backend.py` checks that each backend implements exactly the protocol (the same method names, parameter names, kinds, and defaults, and no public method outside it) and that every protocol method is called somewhere in the shared modules. Writing it down found two methods no shared code called, `is_lazy` and `named`, now removed (`named` stays private to the NumPy backend's `field`); `unsupported` became part of the protocol, with the xarray backend answering None, so `backend_for` no longer special-cases it; the NumPy backend's `from_xarray` became private, since only its `wrap` uses it. Gates: the suite unchanged on both backends on current and minimum dependencies (1,799 and 1,755 with the two new tests), the corpus identical on both, lint unchanged.

Step A2 status (2026-10-25): done. `grouped.Grouping(codes, labels)` holds one integer code per position as a plain NumPy array and the labels in code order, with `counts()` and `blocks()` computed once and kept; it needs nothing from tidy3a, which a test checks by reading the module's imports. `expr.Groups` is a subclass adding tidy3a's `name`, `axis`, and `codes_array`, the codes as the backend's array along the axis with its labels, which the backends use only to broadcast a per-group result back. Both backends read `groups.codes` as the plain array now. Gates: the suite on both backends on current and minimum dependencies (1,805 and 1,761 with the three new tests), the corpus identical on both, lint unchanged.

Step A3 status (2026-10-25): done. The subpackage exists (decision 2: a subpackage now), first as `tidy3a.core`, renamed `tidy3a.labelarray` once the name was chosen, holding `labelarray/errors.py`: `LabelArrayError(ValueError)` with `LabelError`, `AlignError`, and `ShapeError`, importing nothing from the rest of tidy3a, which a test checks. `labels.py` and `numpy_backend.py` raise them (`ShapeError` for axis names, sizes, and ranks that do not fit; `LabelError` for labels that cannot be ordered, compared, or looked up); the two messages in `numpy_backend.py` that speak of tidy3a features (`collect()` without xarray, a lazy outside array) stay `TidyError` until A5 moves them to the adapter. tidy3a's `LabelError` and `AlignError` inherit from both `TidyError` and the library's classes, and `errors.translating` wraps every boundary a library error can cross (the readers, `tidy()`, `Verb.__call__`, `collect()`, `to_xarray()`, `to_dict()`, and `repr`), raising it again as tidy3a's class with the same message and traceback and no chained cause, so `except TidyError` and `except labelarray.LabelError` both catch it; a test lists the boundaries. Found on the way, not fixed here: `read_array` given labels of mixed Python types raises a raw `TypeError` from `np.unique` in `_parse_labels`, before the library sees them. Fixed later the same day (`_label_array` in `io.py`), and found worse than reported: a plain list was not refused but converted by NumPy, so `[1, "a"]` became the text labels "1" and "a", `[True, 2]` the numbers 1 and 2, and `["a", b"b"]` text, breaking the exact label rule without a word; only object arrays and lists with `None` reached the `TypeError`. Both readers now refuse labels mixing kinds (booleans, numbers, times, durations, text, bytes, None) with one `ReadError` naming the kinds and types, and join integers with floats only when float64 holds every label exactly (`[2**53 + 1, 0.5]` is refused). Tests first in `tests/test_io.py` and `tests/test_binary.py`. Gates: the suite on both backends on current and minimum dependencies (1,819 and 1,775 with the seven new tests in `tests/test_errors.py`), the corpus identical on both, the demo unchanged, lint unchanged, and `tidy3a.labelarray`, `labels`, and `grouped` import with xarray and pandas blocked.

Step A4, first part, status (2026-10-25): done in its own worktree session (branch `label-index`, commit `4acc1e2`), merged into `main`. `tidy3a.labelarray.index` holds the `LabelIndex` protocol (`get_indexer`, `slice_indexer` for inclusive label ranges, `kind`, `size`, `unique`, `sorted`) and `index_for(labels)`, which inspects labels once and picks `GridIndex`, `SortedIndex`, or `SortOnceIndex` (the last two the old index, unchanged); the backend still caches the choice on each label array. `GridIndex` covers exactly evenly spaced numbers, times, and durations, increasing or decreasing, held as integers (floats scaled by a power of two), so lookups and ranges are integer arithmetic with no sort and no arrays held; `np.arange(0, 1, 0.1)` is not a grid. `register_index(kind, cls, types=())` registers classes, with the exact-rule obligations in its docstring. The label rule moved to `tidy3a.labelarray.labels` (`tidy3a.labels` re-exports it), and the alignment of a reordered axis to `tidy3a.labelarray.align`, which is more of A5 than the brief asked for. A bug found there and fixed: on ARM, a float-to-int64 cast saturates, so `exact_cast` called `2.0**63` an exact int64; floats outside an integer type's range are never exact now. `NumpyArray` has `__array_ufunc__`, `__array_function__` for `np.where`, and Python's operators, broadcasting by axis name under the exact alignment rule (a reorder matched by label, anything else an `AlignError`); operators are quiet as on xarray, NumPy functions warn; reductions, gufuncs, `out=`, and plain arrays are refused; arrays are no longer hashable, since `==` compares values. Tests: `tests/test_label_index.py`, `tests/test_array_ufunc.py`. The rest of A4 (key transforms) waited on decision 11.

Decision 11 status (2026-10-25): done in its own worktree session (branch `nan-group-keys`, commit `2a35e6e`), merged into `main`. Every NaN, whatever its sign or payload, is one group, and every NaT; signed zeros stay one value. `group_by(..., dropna=True)`, the default, leaves those positions out and notes it ("changed: group_by(ring) dropped 2 positions with a missing ring; dropna=False keeps them as a group"); `dropna=False` keeps them as a group labelled NaN (NaT for times), sorted last. The dropped positions sit in a last group both backends reduce as any other, so neither backend changed: `summarise` leaves it out, and a grouped `mutate` gives those positions a missing value, as pandas' `transform` does. `bins()` follows `dropna` too (a missing value is a missing key; a value outside the bins is still an error), with the kept group labelled "NaN" or "NaT" so bin labels stay text. NaT keys formed a group before; the default now drops them. Tests: `tests/test_nan_groups.py`.

Step A4, second part, status (2026-10-25): done in its own worktree (branch `wip/key-transforms`, commits `c05c295` and `d714436`), merged into `main` as `8b0ed53`. `tidy3a.labelarray.transforms` holds the key transforms as records, applied before grouping on exact equality: `Bins(edges)`, `Duration(width, origin)`, `Round(step, origin)`, and `GridCells(size, origin)`, equal when their settings are (so they can key a cache), hashable, and printed with their settings; plain functions become transforms through `as_transform` and are equal only to themselves; `factorize(values, transform, dropna=…)` gives codes and labels with every NaN or NaT one missing key, dropped or kept last. Steps and origins are read as the decimals they print as, so a value is in a bucket exactly when it lies between the edges as printed (with size 0.1, 0.3 is in [0.3, 0.4), although `0.3 / 0.1` is 2.9999999999999996); integer keys with whole-number settings are placed in integer arithmetic; `Round` sends halves up; `Bins` and `Duration` list every bin or window from the first to the last, `Round` and `GridCells` only those holding values. Values either side of an edge fall into different groups by design, and the origin moves the edges. Infinite values and zoned times are refused; on xarray 2024.6 zoned times arrive as an object array of integer nanoseconds, so the zone is read from the pandas index behind the values (found by the minimum-dependency run, fixed in `d714436`). `bins()` is built on `Bins` and `Duration` with the same results, labels, and messages (1,452 outcomes compared with the previous code on both backends) and takes `origin=` for durations; the duration reader and edge formatting moved into the library. New for `group_by`: `rounded(field, step, origin=0)` and `cells(field, size, origin=0)`, printed as `bins` prints (`group_by(rounded(distance))`, axes `distance_rounded` and `x_cell`). `labelarray.errors.GroupError` joins the library's errors. Grouping by several transformed keys at once (x, y, z cells as one voxel key) is still refused, as any second value key is: lifting that is the next step for voxel grouping. Merged `main`: the suite (2,747 passed) and minimum dependencies (2,710, the usual 4 warnings), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo, lint one finding fewer; the branch was renamed from the app's default prefix to `wip/` before merging.

Merged `main` (`ebf2576`): the suite on both backends (2,605 passed) and on minimum dependencies (2,528), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo, lint two findings fewer. The NaN tests' one second-precision time array became nanoseconds, since older xarray warns converting it.

Step A5 status (2026-10-25): done, staged for review. The NumPy backend's array code moved into the library: `labelarray.array` holds `Array` (the former `NumpyArray`, with the read-only `_Labels` that keep their index, the NumPy protocols, and the reductions), now with the methods `isel`, `sel`, `transpose`, `rename`, `reduce`, `dot`, and `labels`, and the functions `concat`, `align`, `broadcast`, `labels_from`, `label_index`, and `positions_of`; `labelarray.collection` holds `Collection` (the former `NumpyData`) with `isel`, `sel`, `select`, `assign`, `rename`, `join(how)`, `nearest`, `to_dict`, `to_xarray`, and `from_xarray`; `tidy3a.grouped` became `labelarray.grouped`, adding `reduce`, `counts`, and `broadcast` over an `Array` and `FEW_GROUPS`, which the xarray backend shares. `tidy3a.labelarray` names the public API in `__all__`, and the library documents the array, the collection, grouping, and the rules in its own README (`src/tidy3a/labelarray/README.md`), whose examples `tests/test_labelarray.py` runs. `numpy_backend.py` is now the adapter, 360 lines from 1,018: tidy3a's backend protocol over the library, with tidy3a's own messages (`collect()` without xarray, a lazy outside array) and what only tidy3a needs (which xarray objects it can hold, the label listing for messages through pandas, the readers' constructors, positions as labels for `keep`); it defines no functions outside the class, which a test checks, and `NumpyArray` and `NumpyData` remain as names for `Array` and `Collection`. The visible change: `where(...)` predicates receive an object whose type name is `Array` (CHANGELOG, Unreleased). Not done, and left as it is: the NumPy calls that remain in `expr.py` and `verbs.py` are type checks, literals, and the factorizing of keys before the backend is involved, not array computation; moving them gains nothing until a second library backend exists, and `expr._factorize` (pandas' hash when installed) and `labelarray.factorize` (NumPy only) stay separate for that reason. Gates: the suite on both backends (2,858 passed, with 66 new tests in `tests/test_labelarray.py` checking `Array`, `Collection`, joins, `nearest`, `concat`, and the grouped reductions against xarray and against each group reduced alone, the library without xarray and pandas, and the README) and on minimum dependencies (2,781 passed), the 65-case corpus identical on both backends against the commit before, the demo unchanged but for the type name it prints, and lint three findings fewer.

Step A6 status (2026-10-25): done, staged for review. The library's tests are a suite of their own, `labelarray_tests/`, which imports nothing of tidy3a but `tidy3a.labelarray` (a test reads every file's imports, so the library and its tests can leave together in step A7) and needs no tidy3a backend, so each test runs once: `test_array.py` (arrays, against xarray, and the NumPy protocols moved from `test_array_ufunc.py`), `test_collection.py`, `test_grouping.py` (the grouping object, and grouped reductions bit-identical to each group alone, with the one-pass kernel on and off, in 200 random cases ported from tidy3a's verb-level test), `test_sums.py` (the exact-rational reference, the correct-rounding tests in library terms, and the compiled-kernel tests), `test_label_lookups.py` (the pandas-oracle lookups, from `test_numpy_labels.py`), `test_label_indexes.py` (from `test_label_index.py`), `test_transforms.py`, `test_contraction.py`, `test_library_errors.py`, `test_library_readme.py`, and `test_standalone.py`. `tests/` stays tidy3a's integration suite on both backends: verbs on both backends against the corpus and xarray, alignment through `tidy3a.align` with tidy3a's notes (what remains of `test_numpy_labels.py`), the error translation at the boundaries, the verb-level float32 tests (keeping their own copy of the exact reference, so tidy3a's results are still checked independently of the library), `rounded()`, `cells()`, and `bins()` on the records, the property tests comparing `Array`'s ufuncs with each backend's `apply`, and the adapter test (now in `test_numpy_backend.py`). `pyproject.toml` lists both suites in `testpaths`, so CI and `scripts/check.sh` run both unchanged; `MANIFEST.in` adds the library suite and README to the source distribution. Nothing was lost: every test name collected before is collected after, five under new names (`test_numpy_arrays_have_a_shape` is `test_arrays_have_a_shape`, the adapter test `test_the_backend_is_an_adapter_over_the_library`, the two import checks one in `test_standalone.py`, and the library-errors check `test_arrays_raise_the_library_errors`), and 207 are new, 200 of them the ported random cases. Gates: the library suite (694 passed; 661 on minimum dependencies, the 33 compiled-kernel tests skipped without Numba) and tidy3a's (2,000 passed; 1,996 on minimum dependencies) pass on their own and together (2,734; fewer than 2,858 because library tests no longer run once per backend), and lint two findings fewer. No source file changed.

Step A7 status (2026-10-25): done, the tidy3a side staged for review. labelarray is a repository and a PyPI package of its own: [rleyvasal/labelarray](https://github.com/rleyvasal/labelarray), commit `fbbdd2c` on the owner's initial license commit, released as [0.1.0](https://pypi.org/project/labelarray/0.1.0/) through trusted publishing from its `pypi` environment (every release job passed; a clean install from PyPI imports with NumPy alone). It holds `src/labelarray/` (the library from `tidy3a.labelarray` at `4287eea`, importing `labelarray.*`, docstrings no longer citing this plan), the 694 tests from `labelarray_tests/`, its README with tested examples, a changelog, CI and a release workflow mirroring tidy3a's, `scripts/check.sh`, and `docs/releasing.md`; its check passed on Python 3.11–3.14, on the minimum NumPy, pandas, and xarray, and on the wheel plain and with each extra. One fix found on the way, in labelarray only, since tidy3a's copy is gone: `factorize` labelled a kept missing-time group with a NaT of NumPy's deprecated generic unit, a warning tidy3a's `filterwarnings` entry for flox had hidden; it now has the values' unit. tidy3a depends on `labelarray>=0.1.0` (the `fast` extra on `labelarray[fast]`), imports `labelarray` directly, and no longer carries `src/tidy3a/labelarray/` or `labelarray_tests/`; `tidy3a/labelarray.py` keeps the old name as an alias for the package and each of its modules (a test checks `tidy3a.labelarray is labelarray` and `tidy3a.labelarray.sums is labelarray.sums`), so code written against 0.3.0 keeps working. Gates: the suite (2,041 passed with the prototypes) and minimum dependencies (2,037) against the published package, the corpus identical on both backends against the commit before, the demo unchanged, lint one finding fewer (the library's `v != v` left with it), and `scripts/check.sh` with labelarray installed from PyPI in fresh environments (Python 3.11–3.14 each 2,037 passed and 8 skipped; minimum NumPy, pandas, and xarray 2,002 passed and 18 skipped; the source distribution, metadata, and the wheel plain, with xarray, and with fast). Track A is complete; the library now changes in its own repository, and tidy3a picks up its releases through the version floor.

Decision 10, stage (a), status (2026-10-25): done, staged for review, with the owner's choices on the API: `explain()` shows each step with its effect, and pipelines are reused both ways. `tidy3a.plan` records a step on each verb's result at the frame's verb boundary (`TidyArray._apply_verb`): the verb, and a snapshot of the result's variables (names, axes, sizes, types), axes, field axis, groups (with their count when `group_by` found them), and notes, never values, so recording computes nothing; the verbs still run at once through the backend methods. `explain()` gives a `Plan`, printing the source with its variables and then each step with its effect, compared from consecutive snapshots: axes removed, added, or resized; variables removed, added, or given new axes or a new type (a size change shows once, on the axis); field axis; groups; and the notes the step added; a step changing nothing says so. `verb >> verb` composes a `Pipeline` before any data, which `data >> steps` runs verb by verb (each recorded as written separately), which joins flat with other pipelines, and which may end in a verb giving a value (`pull`, `as_table`), shown as what it gave; `steps.explain(data, head=n)` runs it on the first `n` positions of each observation axis and lists only its own steps, saying it was a preview. `result.plan()` gives the recorded verbs as a pipeline, so `other >> result.plan()` repeats them; the record survives `tidy(frame, backend=...)`. A step keeps its verb, so a result keeps alive what its verbs captured, such as a join's other frame, which is what lets its plan run again. Cost: about 12 µs per verb (the snapshot 6.5 µs), 4% of a four-verb pipeline on 34,688 points (1.30 ms against 1.26 ms, median of 12 alternating rounds), within the 10% criterion. Corrected by the quiet-machine benchmark below (2026-10-26): on the benchmark's 34,688-point workloads recording costs 20–36 µs per pipeline on the NumPy backend (5–8%) and 57–119 µs on the xarray backend (6–9%), where asking for the variables builds a DataArray each; still within 10% of 0.3.0, but only just. Made cheaper the same day: a backend method, `layout(data)`, gives each variable's name, axes, shape, and type straight from storage (xarray `Variable`s, not `DataArray`s), axis order comes from the same pass, and a source frame keeps its first snapshot instead of taking one per pipeline; a snapshot fell from 4.2 to 1.8 µs (NumPy) and from 13.4 to 3.0 µs (xarray), and the rerun below puts `main` within 3% of 0.3.0. Not done in this stage: rewriting each verb as a plan node executed by one interpreter ("each verb written once"); the recorded node is the verb as it is, which suffices for `explain()`, previews, and replay, and the rewrite belongs with stage (b) if a workload ever needs chunked execution. Tests: `tests/test_plan.py` (16, on both backends), and the README's example. Gates: the suite (2,133 passed) and minimum dependencies (2,074), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo unchanged, lint unchanged, and `scripts/check.sh` (Python 3.11–3.14 each 2,127 passed; minimum dependencies 2,039; the distributions).

#### Track B — speed-ups

Measured in this session (Apple M4, 10 threads, 10M points of five float32 fields unless stated; milliseconds):

| Lever | Measured | Changes results |
|---|---|---|
| Columnar storage for field holders (each field a contiguous 1-D array) | Field mean 2.9 → 1.1; field write-back 3.4 → 0.9; gathering a field in group order 40 → 19; rotation 14 → 6.7 with `matmul`. One transpose per read: 17.7 (copying the same 200 MB: 4.4) | No |
| Compiled one-pass grouping (Numba, in a scratch environment) | Compensated (Neumaier) float64 grouped mean in one pass: 10.0 on one thread, 3.4 on ten, against 55 + 14 (sort) today. Error against `math.fsum`: zero (one thread), 2.2 × 10⁻¹⁶ relative (ten threads); the two are not bit-identical in float64 but round to the same float32 | Last bits; gated by the floating-point contract (decision 4) |
| `matmul` instead of `einsum` in `dot` | 24 against 66 on the same contiguous data; 6.7 on columnar data. Bit-identical for a permutation rotation (entries 0, ±1); for a general 3 × 3 matrix 35% of results differ, by about 2 float32 steps (largest absolute difference 3 × 10⁻⁵ on values up to 243) | Last bits; same gate |
| Compiled factorize for integer-valued keys | 24.4 against pandas' 26: finding groups is memory-bound | No, and no gain |
| Reusing buffers in point-cloud expressions | 6.8 → 6.5 for `sqrt(x² + y² + z²)` | No, and no gain |
| Multi-threading beyond grouping (numexpr for formulas, Numba `prange`) | Not measured; NumPy's ufuncs use one of the ten cores | numexpr no; otherwise as above |

Where the time goes today on the NumPy backend: the 10M-point §7 pipeline (55 ms) spends about 8 copying `[x, y, z]` out of the holder, 14 in the rotation (`einsum`; fancy indexing on the feature axis happens to leave the selection column-major), about 10 in arithmetic, and 17 in the filter (which already gathers by positions, 3.6, not by mask, 19). The grouped mean (102 ms) spends 26 finding groups (pandas), 14 sorting, 40 gathering the strided field, and 1 in the 32 means. The grouped mutate adds broadcasting (4) and the subtraction (5).

**The floating-point contract**, measured first because it gates two levers. Four kernels for grouped float32 sums and means were compared on 12 cases, 5 broad random ones against `math.fsum` and 7 adversarial ones against exact rational sums (cancellation such as `[2**53, 1, -2**53]`, `[1e30, 1, -1e30]`, alternating ±1e8 with 1s, values and their negatives, a sum beyond float32 whose mean is within it), and on the full 10M-point pipelines:

| Kernel | Worst error (float32 steps) | 10M grouped mean | 10M grouped mutate |
|---|---|---|---|
| Today: gather in group order, NumPy's float32 reduction per group | 39 on ordinary cancelling data; 46 million on adversarial cases; `inf` for the overflowing sum's mean | 99 ms, 238 MB | 107 ms, 238 MB |
| Simple float64 accumulation (`np.bincount` with weights) | gives 0 for `[2**53, 1, -2**53]`, whose sum is 1 | 67 ms, 153 MB | 66 ms, 153 MB |
| Exact: significands summed per exponent, totals combined with compensation; independent of input order and chunking | 0 | 159 ms, 372 MB | 157 ms, 372 MB |
| Certified float64: one float64 pass plus a pass for the sum of magnitudes, which gives an error bound per group; where both ends of the bound round to the same float32 the result is correctly rounded, and the other groups go to the exact kernel | 0 | 74 ms, 191 MB | 83 ms, 191 MB |

The certified kernel's weakness is heavy cancellation: on 10M values centred on zero, 26 of 32 groups fail the check and it takes 171 ms against 106 for the exact kernel alone. Three untested fixes: certify only the result asked for (sum or mean), switch to the exact kernel when most groups fail, and read significands and exponents from the float32 bits. A compiled compensated kernel (the Numba row above) is what pandas and Polars do, and resolves this at 10 ms without the bound.

The contract the measurements support, pending decision 4: float32 sums and means accumulate in float64 and return float32; float64 inputs keep today's path until a kernel is shown better on the same references (sequential float64 is worse than NumPy's pairwise sums for them); labels, shapes, output types, and missing-value rules stay exact; results are repeatable for a fixed input, kernel, and environment, and exact between the two eager backends where they call the same kernel; grouped and ungrouped reductions may use different algorithms with their numerical agreement tested, not bit identity; documented rounding differences are allowed across algorithms, layouts, Dask chunking, thread counts, and devices. Tests use `math.fsum` for broad coverage and exact rational arithmetic for the adversarial corpus, compare against the stored float32 inputs, and measure error as absolute near zero, in float32 steps where meaningful, and scaled by the sum of magnitudes under cancellation.

**Steps**, in the order that pays soonest:

1. Columnar storage for field holders in the NumPy backend only: `read_array` and `from_xarray` store a holder with the field axis as separate contiguous fields (one transpose, 17.7 ms per 200 MB), `values` returns the user's layout as a view, and dims order as the user sees it never changes. NumPy-only, no numerical change. Gate: the suite, the corpus, and bit-identical results; the 10M-point §7 and grouped workloads faster end to end by the amounts above.
2. The compiled kernels as an optional extra (`tidy3a[fast]`, Numba first: no build matrix, about 100 MB installed, 1–2 s first-call compilation cached on disk), used for grouped float32 sums and means and for finding groups when it wins; the pure-NumPy path (the certified kernel) stays the required fallback. Gate: both paths give the same float32 results on the accuracy corpus and the property tests; full-pipeline time and peak memory recorded. Projected 10M-point grouped mean about 40 ms (26 finding groups, 10 reducing, the rest overhead) and grouped mutate about 50, from 102 and 110.
3. `matmul` in `dot` once the contract allows last-bit changes, measured on the whole §7 pipeline (rotation, offset, distance, filter together), since `opt_einsum` once made the rotation faster and the pipeline slower by changing the result's layout.
4. Rerun `scripts/bench.sh` and the milestone 9 criteria, with the speed criterion as revised in decision 9.

A Rust extension (PyO3 and maturin, built with `cibuildwheel`, as Polars is) is the long-run form of step 2 if the library stands alone and Numba's weight is unwanted; it needs a wheel build matrix and is not the first step.

Track B status (2026-10-25): steps done in the recommended order (kernel, then layout, then compiled path, then `matmul`, then the benchmark); not committed.

- Order changed before starting: a check showed field-major storage changes results (a whole holder's mean over points, the `einsum` rotation), so the shared kernel came first, making sums and means independent of layout.
- B2, one float32 sum/mean kernel (`tidy3a.labelarray.sums`), used by both backends for in-memory data, grouped and ungrouped. Every grouped result, and every ungrouped one adding 8 or more values, is correctly rounded: a float64 estimate in about sqrt(n) chunks of sqrt(n) values gives an error bound per result; where both ends of the bound round to the same float32 the result is proven, and the rest are summed exactly (float32 significands summed per exponent with `np.bincount`, combined in Python integers). Ungrouped reductions of fewer than 8 values per result (`sum(p**2, over="i")`) add in float64 in a fixed order without copying: certifying 10M of those cost 32 ms against 4 ms for the sum, since float64 sums of a few float32 values are often exact and exactly halfway between two float32 values. Tested against exact rational arithmetic and an independent rounding routine on 246 groups (broad random, cancellation, wide range, subnormals, adversarial cases including `[2**53, 1, -2**53]` and halfway points, NaN, infinities, overflow, signed zero), with and without `na_rm`, grouped, ungrouped, through `mutate`, on both backends, and for layout independence. Two bugs found and fixed while testing: a shift by a NumPy integer overflowing past 63 bits in the exact sums, and the bound's effective term count used as a mean's divisor. One existing test that asserted bit identity with xarray's per-group float32 means now checks them against the exact reference.
- B1, columnar fields: measured after B2 and not built. The vector selection `[x, y, z]` already comes out field-major, so the rotation gains without it; what global field-major storage would still save (about 10 ms per pipeline: the selection copy, the filter, the write-back) costs 18 ms and a 200 MB copy at every read and breaks the rule that wrapping in-memory data shares it.
- B2', the compiled kernel (`tidy3a.labelarray._compiled`, the `fast` extra, `numba>=0.60`): one parallel pass of compensated (Neumaier) float64 sums, magnitudes, and counts per group; the same certification and exact tier follow, so the results are the same bits as the NumPy path (tested on the corpus). 10M values in 32 groups: 4.8 ms whatever the data, against 41–90 ms for the NumPy path. Numba is imported on the first grouped float32 reduction; the first call in a process compiles in about 1.3 s, later processes load the cache in about 0.5 s. Since results are correctly rounded, the thread count cannot change them: decision 14's `threads=1` option is not needed. Numba is in the dev extra, so CI tests the compiled path; the minimum-dependency job tests the NumPy path.
- B3, `matmul` in `dot` (`tidy3a.labelarray.contract`, both backends in memory): operands arranged the same way for every layout, so results do not depend on layout; 6.5 against 13.3 ms for a 3 x 3 rotation of 10M field-major points (14.2 against 68.7 row-major). On the whole §7 pipeline: 64.4 → 57.9 ms, and the formula form 44.5 → 40.8, so it is adopted. Floating-point warnings are off for the multiplication, since Accelerate under NumPy 2.0 sets the flags on ordinary finite data. Lazy xarray data keeps `xr.dot`.
- B4, the pinned benchmark, run twice on the uncommitted tree (`benchmarks/results/2026-10-25-trackB-numpy-path.json`, and with `scripts/bench.sh --fast` adding Numba 0.68.0, `2026-10-25-trackB-fast.json`). Against 0.2.0's run (`19fcdbe`), median ms, xarray / NumPy backend: 10M grouped mean 113.6 → 96.2 / 112.4 → 97.2, with `fast` 48.8 on NumPy; 10M grouped centring 120.8 → 108.7 / 134.5 → 103.7, with `fast` 57.5; 90,000 groups 31 → 15 / 30 → 14; small grouped workloads on xarray 2.4 → 1.0. Geometric-mean speed-up over all 26 workloads: 1.13 xarray and 1.07 NumPy without `fast`, 1.25 and 1.15 with it. Slower: the 10M-point §7 pipelines on the xarray backend, 57.0 → 71.4 (formula 37.9 → 47.8), about 20%, and on the NumPy backend 55.9 → 60.0 (formula 37.4 → 40.3), about 7%; sub-millisecond workloads moved by 0.1–0.8 ms in both directions, as much as the same code varies between the two runs (a filter: 3.5 against 2.9 ms). Profiled on the xarray backend, the §7 pipeline spends 26 ms in xarray's indexing (unchanged), 13 ms in the float64 distance sum (4 ms in float32 before), and 7 ms in `matmul` (about 14 ms for `einsum` before), so the expected change is about +2 ms; the rest is run-to-run variation, which this workload shows on the xarray backend. The float64 accumulation of three-value sums is the one real cost, about 9 ms per 10M points; whether to keep it there is decision 15.
- Decision 15, few-value sums: float64 kept, and a compiled one-pass version added to the `fast` extra (`_compiled.in_order`): the same float64 additions in the same order, the division, and the rounding in one parallel pass over the results, for one reduced axis of a 1-D or 2-D array (other shapes keep the NumPy path). It gives the same bits as the NumPy path, tested on 30 random cases each in several layouts (NaN, infinities, signed zeros, wide ranges, sum and mean, `na_rm`, `min_count`, and no positions at all, which the property tests found failing first: an empty reshape with `-1`). The sum over x, y, z of 10M points: 4.0 ms compiled, 12.2 ms on the NumPy path, 4.2 ms for the old float32 sum. With `fast`, the 10M-point §7 pipeline takes 49.3 ms on the NumPy backend (55.9 in 0.2.0) and 58.7 on the xarray backend (57.0), and the formula form 30.4 (37.4).
- Gates: the suite with the compiled path (1,916 passed with the few-value tests) and on minimum dependencies without Numba (1,840 passed); the 65-case corpus identical on both backends (its float32 sums and means were already correctly rounded); the demo agrees across backends; lint has one finding fewer; the library modules import without xarray, pandas, or Numba.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

1. **Library scope.** The three layers (`labels`, `grouped`, `array`), or `labels` and `grouped` only, which are nearly standalone now and carry the two distinctive pieces. Recommendation: all three; the collection and joins are what make the rules usable on real data.
2. **Name and home.** A package name, and a separate repository or a subpackage of this one released on its own. Recommendation: a separate repository once step A5 is done; until then, a subpackage. Name chosen (2026-10-25): `labelarray`, free on PyPI and GitHub, saying what distinguishes it from xarray's `NamedArray` and named-arrays: labelled axes, not only named ones; `labarray` was considered and set aside, since "lab" reads as laboratory. The base error is `labelarray.LabelArrayError`.
3. **The alignment rule.** Whether exact alignment (§5.5) travels with the library as its only mode, or the library offers a looser mode (xarray's outer join) with exact as the default. Recommendation: exact only in the first version; a mode can be added, a changed default cannot.
4. **The floating-point contract** stated above: adopt it, or keep bit identity with today's per-group reductions. Everything in track B steps 2 and 3 waits on this.
5. **Ungrouped reductions.** Whether ungrouped float32 sums and means also move to float64 accumulation, so one rule covers both, or only grouped ones change. Recommendation: both, with their agreement tested.
6. **Compiled kernels.** Numba as an optional extra, a Rust or Cython extension built into the library, or neither. Recommendation: Numba first, behind the fallback.
7. **Columnar storage.** Backend-internal, applied on read for holders with a field axis, or left to the user's layout. Recommendation: backend-internal; the user never sees the layout.
8. **Order of the tracks.** Steps A1–A3 first (they move the grouping object the kernels attach to), then track B inside the modules that later move unchanged, then A4–A7. The alternative, library first, delays every speed-up by the library's API design.
9. **The speed criterion.** Replace "1.5 times faster than the xarray backend on every workload" with end-to-end times against the previous release for both backends, the ratio reported, and no workload more than 10% slower than the release before; see the milestone 9 note on why the ratio narrows as shared code improves.
10. **The planner.** The notes make lazy execution the main mode, with eager as lazy plus an automatic compute after each verb so each verb is written once, and an executor that fuses and reorders steps and runs chunk by chunk over memory-mapped arrays without Dask. Milestone 9 decided the NumPy backend never rebuilds Dask support; this reverses that, and it is the largest piece of the design. What the measurements say: fusion and reordering gain little in NumPy (fusing `sqrt(x² + y² + z²)` saved 0.3 ms of 6.8 at 10M points, because ufuncs are memory-bound and fast; fusion pays with compiled or threaded execution), and "filter before heavy computation" applies only when the predicate does not need the heavy result, which in §7 it does (the filter needs the rotation and the distance). The planner's value is peak memory and out-of-core execution, not speed on in-memory data. Two conditions whatever is decided: labels, dims, dtypes, and attributes stay eager even when values are lazy, as in xarray and Dask, so every check, note ("changed: x reordered to match …"), message, and inspection line is produced at plan time unchanged; and eager stays the default (notebooks, pandas, and xarray are eager; Polars offers both and recommends lazy for big data), with the per-verb overhead guarded by the 34,688-point workloads (0.3–0.7 ms today). Staged approach: (a) verbs record plan nodes and execute them at once through the existing backend methods, which are already the executor's primitives; this gives `explain()` (the plan preview), running a pipeline on a head slice, and each verb written once, and is mostly a refactor; (b) chunked execution over memory maps only if out-of-core without Dask is a requirement, built after (a) and measured against Dask on the xarray backend, which exists and is tested, with chunked grouping under the floating-point contract since tree-combined reductions are not bit-identical across chunk sizes. Recommendation: (a) after track B; (b) not until a workload needs it.
11. **NaN group keys.** Keep today's error on a value key with NaN, or adopt the notes' rule: all NaN one group, with `dropna` choosing whether it is kept (pandas' `dropna=False`). Recommendation: adopt it with `dropna=True` as the default, matching pandas, and a note saying how many positions were dropped.
12. **A release before the floating-point change.** Everything committed since 0.1.0 is user-visible and numerically unchanged (xarray and pandas optional, the exact label rule, join checks, durations read by tidy3a, faster grouping); track B changes results. Recommendation: release 0.2.0 after A1–A3 and before track B, so 0.3.0's note can say "float32 sums and means now accumulate in float64" on its own; the README's "not yet published" line is fixed in the same commit.
13. **Milestone 8's Polars grouping engine.** It targeted the problem track B solves in NumPy (10M-point grouped mean 13.6 ms through Polars, against about 40 ms projected compiled, 3.4 ms parallel), and would be a third path to keep in agreement on the accuracy corpus. Recommendation: mark the engine part of milestone 8 superseded by track B, keep the tidy3 bridge, and revisit Polars only if a workload shows it ahead after B.
14. **Parallel compiled kernels.** 3.4 ms on ten threads against 10 ms on one; thread chunking changes float64 partial sums (2 × 10⁻¹⁶ relative), so results can differ at the float64 level across core counts, rarely after rounding to float32, which the contract allows. Recommendation: parallel by default, `set_options(threads=1)` for strict repeatability, documented, and the accuracy corpus checked at 1 and at 10 threads.

15. **Few-value sums.** Ungrouped float32 reductions of fewer than 8 values per result (the distance's sum over x, y, z) cost about 9 ms more per 10M points in float64 than in float32. Options: keep float64 (more accurate: the float32 sum was not correctly rounded for 22% of points); keep float64 and add a compiled one-pass version to the `fast` extra (about 4 ms, only with Numba); or add in float32 in a fixed order (as fast as before and still independent of layout, but as inaccurate as before). Recommendation: keep float64, add the compiled version. Decided (2026-10-25) as recommended.

Criteria rerun on `23bf733` (2026-10-25), step B4 proper. 0.2.0 itself was benchmarked again from a worktree of `v0.2.0` in the same session, then `23bf733` without and with `--fast`, one after another, in the pinned environment with the real data (`benchmarks/results/2026-10-25-23bf733.json`, `…-23bf733-fast.json`; the 0.2.0 run is not kept in the repository). The speed criterion is judged as revised in decision 9: end-to-end against the previous release, for both backends, no workload more than 10% slower.

| Criterion | Result | Met |
|---|---|---|
| Correctness | The suite on both backends: 1,916 with Numba, 1,840 on minimum dependencies without it; the 65-case corpus and the property tests agree; float32 sums and means correctly rounded against exact references; the only differences are the documented ones in `docs/backends.md` | Yes |
| Speed, NumPy backend | Against 0.2.0: geometric-mean speed-up 1.13 without `fast`, no workload more than 10% slower; 1.11 with `fast`, where only the two 34,688-point §7 pipelines are slower, by 0.1 ms (0.45 → 0.56 and 0.42 → 0.47), the cost of calling the compiled kernels on small arrays. 10M grouped mean 105.5 → 94.4 (50.9 with `fast`), grouped centring 119.0 → 101.4 (57.5), §7 57.8 → 55.3 (46.7), formula 37.8 → 36.4 (28.0) | Yes without `fast`; with `fast` after a size threshold |
| Speed, xarray backend | 1.26 without `fast`; the 10M-point §7 formula is 11% slower (39.4 → 44.5), the float64 three-value sum. 1.25 with `fast`; the same two 34,688-point pipelines 0.1 ms slower, and the 10M formula 35.3 | No without `fast` (one workload, 1 point past the limit); with `fast` after a size threshold |
| NumPy against xarray backend now | Geometric mean 1.69 faster (1.68 with `fast`), from 1.00 to 3.43: shared code made large workloads equal, small ones keep the NumPy backend's lower overhead | Reported |
| Dependencies | Installs and runs with NumPy alone since 0.2.0 (checked on the published wheel) | Yes |
| Import time | 50 ms for `import tidy3a` (41 in 0.2.0: the library modules), 59 with the NumPy backend, 235 once the xarray backend loads; neither Numba nor xarray is imported | Yes (reported) |
| Maintenance | `numpy_backend.py` 969 lines, no verb logic; shared library modules 483 lines (`labelarray`), the label rule and grouping 377 | Yes |

The compiled kernels are slower than the NumPy path below about 100,000 values (34,688 values: 48 against 42 µs for the few-value sum, 201 against 155 µs for a grouped mean) and 2 to 8 times faster from 300,000 (3M: 1.1 against 3.4 ms, 1.6 against 12.7 ms). Using them only from a size threshold removes the two small slowdowns and changes no result, since both paths give the same bits.

16. **The NumPy backend as the default when xarray is installed.** The criteria are met, with the two caveats above. One semantic difference the timings do not show: the NumPy backend does not keep xarray storage encodings (`scale_factor`, `_FillValue`, a stored `dtype`), so with it as the default, `tidy(xr.open_dataset(…))` written back to netCDF would lose them without a word. Options: (a) the NumPy backend becomes the default for data it holds without loss: reader output, and in-memory xarray data without storage encodings; data with encodings, and what already stays on xarray (lazy, chunked, memory-mapped, auxiliary coordinates, multi-level labels, time zones), stays on xarray; `set_backend("xarray")` opts out; (b) keep xarray the default for another release. Recommendation: (a) in 0.3.0, alongside the numerical change, after the size threshold for compiled kernels and the encodings rule, each with tests; the xarray backend's one slow workload without `fast` (the 10M formula, 11%) then matters only to those who choose it.

Decided (2026-10-25): option (a). Done, not committed: `get_backend()` with no choice made is NumPy, and `backend_for` keeps data with xarray storage encodings (on the dataset, a variable, or a coordinate, such as a time axis's units) on xarray when no choice was made at all; `set_backend("numpy")` and `backend="numpy"` keep their meaning, NumPy wherever it can hold the data, encodings dropped as documented. The compiled kernels run from the sizes where they pay: `sums.COMPILED_FROM` (100,000 values) for grouped sums and `sums.IN_ORDER_COMPILED_FROM` (300,000) for few-value sums, whose compiled version was still slower at 180,000 values (89 against 78 µs) and inside a small pipeline (0.74 against 0.53 ms). Tests first (`tests/test_default_backend.py`, and a test that small inputs use the NumPy path and large ones the compiled one): in-memory data and reader output use NumPy by default; data with encodings, including a netCDF file read and written back, keeps them on xarray; lazy data stays on xarray; choices still decide. `docs/backends.md`, the README, docstrings, and the changelog say the NumPy backend is the default, no longer experimental; the one visible difference for code written against 0.2.0 is that `where(...)` predicates receive a `NumpyArray`, and `set_backend("xarray")` restores 0.2.0's behaviour.

A constraint, not a decision: Numba supports a new NumPy release some months after it appears. With `numpy>=2.0` as the floor, the `fast` extra pins a Numba version, and CI tests the newest NumPy without Numba as well as the fast path where Numba is available. Open on purpose: whether the NumPy backend becomes the default when xarray is installed; that is the milestone 9 go/no-go, rerun at step B4 against the revised criterion.

**Decided (2026-10-25)**, all as recommended: 1 all three layers; 2 a subpackage now, `tidy3a.labelarray`, and a separate repository and distribution named `labelarray` once the API settles; 3 exact alignment only in the first version; 4 the floating-point contract as stated, with every library compared (NumPy, pandas, Polars, Arrow, DuckDB, xarray with flox and bottleneck, Dask, PyTorch, JAX) promising repeatability and accuracy rather than bit identity, so today's promise was stricter than any of them; 5 ungrouped float32 sums and means follow the same rule, with grouped and ungrouped agreement tested; 6 Numba as the optional extra `tidy3a[fast]`, both paths required to give the same float32 results on the accuracy corpus, Rust later if the library stands alone; 7 columnar storage internal to the backend; 8 steps A1–A3, then track B, then A4–A7; 9 the speed criterion becomes end-to-end times against the previous release for both backends, the ratio reported, no workload more than 10% slower than the release before; 10 plan recording after track B, eager by default, labels eager, no chunked executor until a workload needs it; 11 NaN keys form one group, dropped by default, with a note; 12 release 0.2.0 after A1–A3; 13 milestone 8's engine superseded; 14 parallel by default with `threads=1` available.

Release 0.2.0 status (2026-10-25): published. Commit `98ab27b`, tag `v0.2.0`, GitHub Release [0.2.0](https://github.com/rleyvasal/tidy3a/releases/tag/v0.2.0); the release workflow (version gate, CI matrix, trusted publishing) succeeded, and `pip install tidy3a==0.2.0` in a clean environment installs NumPy and tidy3a only and runs the README quick start. Preparation, as recorded before publishing: Version `0.2.0`, a dated changelog section grouped by topic whose lead line says results are numerically unchanged from 0.1.0, the README status line, and `scripts/check.sh` extended to install the wheel with the `[xarray]` extra as CI does. `scripts/check.sh` passed on the release tree: Python 3.11–3.14 each 1,815 passed and 8 skipped; minimum NumPy 2.0, pandas 2.2.2, and xarray 2024.6 1,781 passed and 17 skipped; the source distribution, metadata, the plain wheel (NumPy only, `to_dict()`), and the wheel with the extra (`collect()`). Rerun after the package name was written `tidy3a` everywhere and `tidy3a.core` became `tidy3a.labelarray`: the same counts, all passing. Remaining, as `docs/releasing.md` describes: commit and push, tag `v0.2.0`, publish the GitHub Release, which runs the release workflow and publishes to PyPI through trusted publishing.

Release 0.7.0 status (2026-10-27): prepared by the owner's request, a minor release for milestone 12: a dataset read in blocks computes only the variables a pipeline reads (the temperature summary of the 31.8 GB NetCDF file in 158 s against 1,030 s), and `write(path)` writes rows that need not fit in memory to `.npy` or Zarr, block by block (28.8 GB at a 1.01 GB peak); with the refusal of a caller's `where()` selector on data read in blocks, which 0.6.0 checked on no values, and two fixes the registry's tests found: `any()` and `all()` of missing values on blocks counted as in memory (`8fbf23b`), and text index coordinates written to Zarr as variable-length strings (`c8b31ce`).
