Release 0.9.0 status (2026-10-28): prepared by the owner's request, a minor release for milestones 14 and 15: `blocks="auto"` with `memory=`, blocks sized for a budget by an estimate from the data's types that reads nothing, whole storage chunks, and a scan's blocks sized for all its frames (the sweeps and NetCDF files on the USB drive within their budgets and level with sizes chosen by hand); and blocks that scale better with threads: summaries merged in a fixed tree on the workers (the same bits on any number of threads; float64 sums and means, integer means, and spreads differ in their last bits from 0.8.0's), reductions of each position first (a daytime summary of 2,000 grids 7.2x faster on one thread, 16x on four), and a block's groups merged into the table in one pass (labelarray's `merge_sorted`). It requires labelarray 0.1.6 (published the same day). `scripts/check.sh --parallel` on exactly the release's contents (`8653440` with the version and the requirement), on the 12-core machine, in 462 s: Python 3.11, 3.12, and 3.13 each 3,544 passed and 10 skipped; minimum NumPy, pandas, and xarray 3,428 passed and 48 skipped; the source distribution, metadata, and the wheel plain, with xarray, and with fast. Its Python 3.14 job ran on the free-threaded build, which uv there prefers once installed, and passed (3,544); the standard 3.14, on the 24 GB machine, passed too (3,544).

The two labelarray micro-optimizations (2026-10-26): done, staged in both repositories with labelarray 0.1.3 prepared. `labelarray.grouped.Grouping` keeps labels given as a NumPy array as a read-only copy instead of a list of Python values, and `expr._multi_groups` passes the groups' axis labels as `np.arange`; a voxel summary of 200,000 points went from 22 to 20 ms on the NumPy backend. `labelarray.sums._in_order` converts the few rows to float64 at once and adds them in the same order as before (`_in_order_general` keeps the missing-value rules and the no-row case), the last addition of a sum written into the float32 result: 45.7 to 35.9 µs for three rows of 34,688 values, the same bits (a test compares both paths over layouts, special values, no kept axes, and no rows; a random-pipeline test found the no-row case first). Lint cleanups: import order in `tests/test_dask_float32.py`, and `itertools.pairwise` and no warnings in labelarray's chunked tests. Gates: labelarray's tests (756) and `scripts/check.sh`; tidy3a's suite (2,278) and minimum dependencies (2,217) on the local labelarray 0.1.3, the corpus identical on both backends, the demo unchanged, lint two findings fewer. tidy3a now requires labelarray 0.1.3, so labelarray must be published first.

Rough edges (2026-10-26): fixed, staged for review. A verb that removes the field axis now ends it (`TidyArray._evolve` drops a field axis the new data lacks), so `explain()` reports it and a new one can be declared (`tests/test_field_axis_lifetime.py`). Several value keys now get their per-group key values by indexing each key's label array once (`expr._label_array`) instead of a Python loop over the groups, and `summarise` takes them as arrays; the voxel summary on 200,000 points went from 33 to 22 ms on the NumPy backend (`_multi_groups` 19 to 2 ms). What remains of tidy3a's own work there is about 3 ms turning the group axis's 97,000 labels, a list in `labelarray.grouped.Grouping`, into arrays twice (`labels_from`, `counts`); labelarray accepting label arrays would remove it, in a later labelarray release. Gates: the suite (2,246 passed) and minimum dependencies (2,187), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo unchanged, lint unchanged, and `scripts/check.sh` (Python 3.11–3.14 each 2,240 passed; minimum dependencies 2,152; the distributions).

Decision 10, stage (b), reviewed (2026-10-26): stays deferred, the owner's decision after a survey. With Dask, the xarray backend already keeps data out of memory through a pipeline: `mutate` stays lazy, `filter` and `group_by` compute only their mask and group codes (one value per position), and `summarise` gives a lazy result (counted with Dask's callbacks on 2M points in 8 chunks). Elsewhere, arrays delegate out-of-core work to Dask or Cubed (bounded memory over Zarr chunks, with xarray support), while tables have dedicated engines (Polars' streaming engine, the default in 2.0; DuckDB's spilling hash aggregation since 0.9; Vaex over memory maps), each a project of its own. One gap was measured: float32 grouped sums and means on chunked data use xarray's and Dask's arithmetic, so 21–24 of 32 groups differed from the in-memory, correctly rounded results; no surveyed tool gives chunk-independent results either (Dask and flox vary with the chunking, DuckDB documents thread-dependent results, Spark's are nondeterministic, Polars' streaming and in-memory engines differed in 32 of 32 groups), though the literature does (ReproBLAS' binned accumulators, ExBLAS and other superaccumulators), and labelarray's estimates, bounds, and exact per-exponent integer sums combine across chunks in any order, so a chunk-independent kernel remains possible if a workload needs it. For now, the README's Numbers section states the exception `docs/backends.md` already gave, and suggests loading the data first where results must match.

Decision 10, stage (b), the measured gap closed (2026-10-26): the owner chose the chunk-independent kernel over the README exception; the chunked engine itself stays deferred. labelarray 0.1.2 adds the pieces (`sums.chunk_estimate`, `settle`, `chunk_exact`, `exact_results`, `from_chunks`): a chunk's float64 totals, sums of magnitudes, counts of values, of values present, and of +inf and -inf, and the term count of its error bound, which add across chunks (the term count by its maximum plus the number of chunks, Higham's lemma 3.3), then certification as in memory, then, for the groups the bound cannot settle, exact sums per chunk as int64 buckets per float32 exponent (254 of them, plus the counts), which add. `XarrayBackend._float32_chunked` runs the estimate as one `dask.array.blockwise` task per chunk, combines through one `dask.array.reduction`, and settles in one `map_blocks` task over a single block, so the result stays a lazy one-chunk Dask array sharing the source's tasks with the rest of the collection (a `dask.delayed` call would have wrapped them, reading each chunk once per reduction); the exact pass, when needed, is a second `blockwise` computed from inside that task. Chunks take the NumPy estimate, not the compiled kernel: Numba's parallel kernel aborts when entered from several Dask threads at once, and the chunks already run in parallel. Measured on 2,000,000 values in 32 groups at six chunkings (50,000 to one chunk, and a 7-value first chunk): 0 of 32 differ from the exact reference, every sum and mean the same bits as in memory, about 70 ms. Tests: labelarray's `tests/test_sums.py` (chunk combining in shuffled orders, ties across chunk boundaries, specials without an exact pass, empty chunks, `min_count`) and `tests/test_dask_float32.py` (the corpus at four chunkings against the in-memory bits, kept axes, laziness and one read per chunk, the second pass only for ties, cancellation across a boundary, missing keys).

Decision 10, stage (b), no longer deferred (2026-10-26): the owner has a larger-than-memory workload, so the chunked engine was prototyped (`prototypes/chunked_executor.py`; its tests became `tests/test_blocks.py` in phase 1) and measured against the Dask path, which the plan named as the test. The Dask path does not deliver larger-than-memory today: on 80M points × 5 float32 fields (1.5 GB, `.npy`) through `mutate(distance)`, `filter(distance <= 60)`, `group_by(ring)`, `summarise(n(), mean(intensity))`, it peaked at 5.4 GB whatever the chunking (2M or 500k points), against 5.9 GB eager with the columns loaded first; all of it in `filter`, where `XarrayBackend.keep` turns the mask into an integer array of kept positions, fancy-indexes every chunk with it, and builds the 1-based position labels of the new axis eagerly, as the plan's condition that labels stay eager requires. (An earlier run with `da.from_array` on a memory map was discarded: `from_array` reads the whole file to fingerprint it; the Dask figures use one `from_delayed` reader per block.) The prototype reads a block of rows from the memory map and copies it, so no mapping outlives its block; wraps the block as an ordinary NumPy-backend frame and runs it through tidy3a's own `mutate` and `filter`, so each verb is written once, which was decision 10's main condition; factorizes the key per block and merges the blocks' partial summaries by key; and for float32 sums and means uses labelarray 0.1.2's chunk pieces, with the second pass over the file only for the groups the bound cannot settle, blocks running in parallel in both passes. Measured on the same file and pipeline, 10 cores, the file in the page cache (so compute-bound: on a file larger than RAM, sequential disk throughput dominates, and the engine's advantage is that it finishes, not its speed): 1 worker and 2M-row blocks 9.5 s at 0.28 GB; 4 workers 1.9 s at 0.73 GB; 8 workers 1.4 s at 1.3 GB; 4 workers and 500k-row blocks 0.24 GB; the file read four times as one input (320M points, 6.4 GB) 7.3 s at the same 0.73 GB. The counts and the float32 means were the same bits as the eager and the Dask results in every run checked, and the second pass ran every time, since zero-mean synthetic data is the worst case for the bound (it took 0.5–0.7 s of the 1.4–1.9 s once parallel). Tests at block sizes from one row to the whole file, with 1 and 3 workers: ties and cancellation that force the second pass, several files as one input, no `group_by`, missing keys, and refusals of what needs the whole axis. Not proved: the voxel workload (several keys, `cells()`, about 90,000 groups, where the prototype's list of partial tables would not do), a file larger than RAM (the disk had 2.6 GB free), real data, and float64, which has no chunk-independent kernel and combined close to, not the same bits as, in memory. Plan, in three phases. **Phase 1**, the prototype productised in the package: `read_array(path, ..., blocks=rows)` gives a frame holding the first block, so every verb runs on it as it is called and its checks, notes, errors, and `explain()` appear at verb time, as decision 10 requires, while the recorded verbs run over all blocks when a result is asked for (`collect()`, `pull`, `to_dict()`, any verb that needs the whole axis); row-wise verbs, one plain key, `n`, `sum`, `mean`, `min`, and `max` of any row-wise expression, expressions of those, an incremental merge into one accumulating table, `.npy` and raw files, several blocks at once with `threads=`; what the engine cannot combine is refused when the verb is called, with the whole-axis alternative named. **Phase 2**, the voxel workload: `MultiKey` and the transforms (`cells`, `bins`, `rounded`), which are row-wise, so the cost is merging combination keys across blocks; `dropna=False`; block-in, block-out pipelines that filter a large file into a smaller one; measured on nuScenes, not synthetic data, which decides whether the engine earns its place. **Phase 3**, contract decisions for the owner: float64 sums and means across blocks as "repeatable, not bit-identical", or labelarray's exact buckets extended to float64 exponents (2,046 of them); `std` and `var` combine (Chan's formula) but not bit-identically; `median` cannot combine and stays refused. Left out: Zarr and NetCDF stay with Dask on the xarray backend; fusion and reordering, which the measurements above showed gain nothing in NumPy. Milestone 9's rule that the NumPy backend never rebuilds Dask support stands: this is a block driver over tidy3a's verbs on NumPy, not chunked arrays.

Phase 1 status (2026-10-26): done, staged for review. `read_array(path, ..., blocks=rows, threads=1)` (`.npy` and raw files; an in-memory array, `lazy=True`, `order="F"`, and a first axis that `field_axis=` or `variables_from=` turns into columns are refused) gives a `ChunkedFrame` (`tidy3a.chunked`), a `TidyArray` holding the first block, built by the reader's own code on a copy out of the memory map, which closes with the block. Its `_apply_verb` runs each verb on the first block through `TidyArray`'s own path, so the verb's checks, notes, and recorded step come first, then the block checks (`BlockError`): a reduction inside `mutate` or `filter`, anything after `group_by` but `summarise`, a `group_by` that is not one plain field with `dropna`, and in `summarise` a reduction other than `n`, `sum`, `mean`, `min`, `max`, `over=`, `min_count=`, or a summary that is not a reduction or an expression of reductions (each reduction is replaced by a hidden name and combined on its own; the expression is evaluated once on the merged table, so `(max(z) - min(z)) / n()` and `sqrt(mean(z * z))` work). Any other verb, `collect()`, `to_xarray()`, `to_dict()`, and the tidy3 bridge run the recorded verbs over every block (`ThreadPoolExecutor`, the partial tables consumed as they arrive) into one accumulating table keyed by the sorted group labels (`np.union1d` and `searchsorted` widen it when a block brings new groups), then settle: float32 sums and means through `labelarray.sums.from_chunks`, the second pass over the blocks in the same pool for the groups the bound cannot settle; float64 ones as per-block float64 totals and counts present; integer ones as int64 totals; `min` and `max` by `reduceat` per block; `n` by counts. The result is the frame `summarise` gave on the first block with every block's values, its dropped-key note recounted over all blocks, its last recorded step's effect from the merged result, and it is computed once; `repr` and `explain()` of the chunked frame show it once it exists, the first block before. No row anywhere gives the first block's (empty) summary. Measured on the 80M-point file and pipeline above, the file in the page cache: 1 thread and 2M-row blocks 7.4 s at 0.39 GB; 4 threads 1.5 s at 0.95 GB; 4 threads and 500k-row blocks 1.5 s at 0.39 GB; counts and float32 means the same bits as in memory in each. The verbs on the first block of 2M rows took 0.4–0.9 s. Tests: `tests/test_blocks.py`, on both backends: float32 summaries bit for bit at block sizes from 7 rows to the whole file with 1 and 3 threads, and one-row blocks on a small file; expressions of reductions; no `group_by`; a block the filter empties and keys absent from some blocks; missing keys counted over every block; float64 and int32 inputs; a big-endian raw file; the first block's inspection and `explain()`; each refusal; the reader's option checks; an empty file. Not in phase 1, as planned: several keys, transforms, `dropna=False`, block-in block-out pipelines (phase 2); the float64, `std`, `var`, and `median` contract (phase 3).

Phase 2 status (2026-10-26): done, staged for review. `group_by` on blocks takes any value keys: plain fields, `cells`, `bins`, `rounded`, several at once (`axis=`), with or without `dropna`; an axis key and a duration (`bins(t, "1 day")`, whose windows start from the first time present) are refused. Each key is merged across blocks on its own (`_Part`): its labels kept in the order `group_by` gives them, by a cell's low edge parsed from the `"[low, high)"` text (text order is not numeric order), by a bin's place among the edges (every bin is a group in every block, so the lists must agree), and by value otherwise (`np.unique`, the same order as the in-memory factorizing); the missing group, recognised by its label (NaN, NaT, or a transform's `"NaN"`), is one slot after the labels, last as in memory; a block's local codes map to the key's codes by `searchsorted` for numbers and times and by a dictionary for text. Several keys combine as `np.ravel_multi_index` of the parts' codes, first key major, so the sorted combined codes are the in-memory order; when a block widens a key the table's codes are unravelled, moved, and ravelled again, which keeps them sorted since each key's move is monotone; a block's pieces are made once per local combination (`np.unique` of its rows' combined codes), not once per row. Rows missing any key under `dropna` are left out before the pieces, and the note counts them over every block, naming the keys that dropped rows as in memory. A pipeline that does not end in `summarise` now joins the blocks' rows (`_concatenate`): each block's `to_dict()` after the row-wise verbs, variables concatenated along the blocked axis, the labels `filter` gives an unlabelled axis offset by the block's start so they are the file's positions, an axis's own labels concatenated, then `from_records`; a verb that needs the whole axis (`slice`, joins) runs on the joined rows. Variable attributes do not survive the join (`from_records` takes none), and the joined rows are in memory, not written to a file, which stays open. Measured on real data, nuScenes Mini's 3,531 LIDAR sweeps joined into one raw float32 file (122,600,960 points, 2.45 GB) through `mutate(distance)`, `filter(distance <= 50)` (120,003,908 kept), `group_by(cells(x, 1), cells(y, 1), cells(z, 1), axis="voxel")`, `summarise(n(), mean(intensity))`, 84,475 voxels: blocks of 2M rows on 4 threads 8.0 s at a peak of 1.65 GB (the verbs on the first block 1.0 s); in memory on the NumPy backend 36.5 s at 7.14 GB; the voxels, counts, and float32 means identical. The blocks are faster here because factorizing three cell keys per 2M-row block costs less than once over 120M rows. One difference found and left: in memory, a single key's text labels are NumPy text when `n()` is the first summary (`labelarray.grouped.counts` freezes `np.asarray(labels)`) and objects otherwise (`labels_from`); blocks give objects; `tests/test_blocks.py` compares text labels as values, and making `counts` use `labels_from` is a labelarray follow-up. Tests added: the voxel pipeline (three keys, two transforms, `axis=`) at four block sizes with 3 threads; each kind of key alone and a field with a cell; `dropna=False` for a field, a cell, a rounded value, and two keys, and the dropped-key notes for one key and two; 100,000 points in more than 50,000 voxels merged; values outside every bin as `group_by`'s own error; rows joined with their positions at three block sizes, with an axis's own labels and a `slice` after, and with no row kept anywhere.

Phase 3, the contract (2026-10-26): implemented as recommended, and accepted by the owner the same day. (1) Float64 sums and means across blocks are repeatable, not bit-identical: each block's float64 totals and counts add in block order, whatever the thread count (the pool gives the blocks' results in order), so a block size always gives the same bits, which differ in the last bits between block sizes and from the in-memory results. The exact alternative was not built: labelarray's float32 scheme rests on a float64 accumulator holding 29 bits beyond a float32 mantissa, so its error bound settles almost every group and the integer buckets per exponent have 2^39 values of headroom; a float64 accumulator holds nothing beyond a float64 mantissa, so nearly every group would need the exact pass, and float64 buckets need two int64 per exponent (2,046 of them) per group and column; a chunk-independent float64 kernel is the compensated-estimate and superaccumulator work the stage (b) review named (ReproBLAS, ExBLAS), a project of its own if a workload asks for it. (2) `std` and `var` combine: each block gives per group, in float64, the values present, their mean, the sum of squared deviations, and the NaN among them, and the blocks combine by Chan, Golub, and LeVeque's parallel update; the result is the population variance (`squares / present`, as `np.var`, which the in-memory reductions use), NaN where no value is present or, without `na_rm`, where a NaN was, cast to the in-memory type (float32 for float32 input, which in memory is computed in float32 arithmetic; the blocks' float64 moments are close, within 1e-5 relative in the tests, not the same bits). (3) `median` stays refused: it cannot combine from per-block pieces. Also added, since they combine exactly: `any` and `all` (per block by `logical_or` and `logical_and`), and `min_count=` on sums (`from_chunks` takes it for float32; float64 and integer sums are NaN where fewer values are present); every summary is cast to the type the first block's `summarise` gave it, so an integer sum with `min_count` is float64 as in memory. Tests: the spread, flags, `min_count`, and `na_rm` summaries on float32, float64, and int32 inputs with NaN, against the in-memory types and values; `median` and `over=` refused.

Larger than memory, measured (2026-10-27): the nuScenes Mini sweeps file written twelve times back to back on an external drive (`/Volumes/Sandisk 1tb/tidy3a-testing/sweeps-x12.bin`, 29.4 GB, 1,471,211,520 points; the machine has 24 GB), ExFAT over USB reading at 35.7 MB/s (`dd` over the first 4 GB), through the voxel pipeline above. The results are checkable without an in-memory run, which is impossible at this size: every voxel count must be twelve times the single-copy count, and the float32 means the same bits, since the correctly rounded mean of twelve copies is the mean of one. Blocks of 2M rows on 4 threads: 759 s at a peak of 1.85 GB; 500k rows on 4 threads: 896 s at 0.65 GB; 2M rows on 1 thread: 946 s at 1.18 GB; in each, the 84,475 voxels, 1,440,046,896 points kept (twelve times 120,003,908), and the mean bits equal to the single-copy result, and the first block's verbs 0.9–1.8 s. The runs sat at about 2% CPU: the file was read once at the drive's speed (29.4 GB in 759 s is 39 MB/s), so on real intensities the error bound settled every group and the second pass did not run, unlike the zero-mean synthetic data where it always did. Memory was flat at the blocks in flight whatever the file size: the same 1.85 GB would hold for a terabyte.

§7 pipelines against 0.2.0 (2026-10-26): closed. Timing each step on 34,688 points showed the rotation 8–13% faster than in 0.2.0 and the filter unchanged; all of the slowdown was in the distance step, `sum(p**2, over="i")`. On the NumPy backend, most was not the sum: the pipeline asked the backend seven times whether a name is a field-axis label, and each answer went through the label index (`get_indexer`, `exact_cast`) for five text labels, about 9 µs each, about 63 µs per run. `NumpyBackend.has_label` now compares text directly for text axes of up to 64 labels (`_FEW_LABELS`): text matches only text and equality of text is exact, so the exact rule holds without the index; 0.35 µs. On the xarray backend, `_float32` reduced a one-position slice to get the result's axes, coordinates, name, and attributes, about 86 µs, more than the sum; it now builds the result directly, keeping attributes as xarray's reductions do under the current `keep_attrs` setting (one tiny probe reduction per setting, since the default changed between xarray versions), and arrays with indexes other than pandas' keep the slice. What remains on the xarray backend is decision 15's fixed-order float64 sum of the three values, about 45 µs against NumPy's float32 sum; a fused version with identical bits measured 38 µs against 47, which is labelarray's to adopt if it is worth a release. Ten alternating rounds in the pinned environment (load 3.0–4.5, interquartile range 1–7% of the median), milliseconds, 0.2.0 / 0.4.1 / `main`:

| 34,688 points | NumPy backend | xarray backend |
|---|---|---|
| §7 rotate, distance, 50 m filter | 0.414 / 0.455 / 0.377 (−9% against 0.2.0) | 1.195 / 1.317 / 1.212 (+1%) |
| §7 with the formula | 0.380 / 0.413 / 0.323 (−15%) | 0.930 / 1.021 / 0.930 (0%) |
| Mean intensity per ring | 0.500 / 0.441 / 0.420 | 2.425 / 0.883 / 0.833 |
| Intensity centred per ring | 0.561 / 0.501 / 0.462 | 2.496 / 0.977 / 0.921 |

Tests: `tests/test_has_label.py` (exact answers for text and numeric labels, on few and many labels, both backends) and `tests/test_xarray_float32_metadata.py` (the direct result against xarray's reduction under each `keep_attrs` setting). A first minimum-dependency run found `xr.indexes` is not an attribute in xarray 2024.6; `from xarray.indexes import PandasIndex` works on every supported version. Gates: the suite (2,240 passed) and minimum dependencies (2,181), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo unchanged, lint unchanged, and `scripts/check.sh` (Python 3.11–3.14 each 2,234 passed; minimum dependencies 2,146; the distributions).

Quiet-machine benchmark (2026-10-26): the 34,688-point workloads the 0.3.0 note could not judge, timed with no other heavy process running (load 1.85–3.52 on 10 cores, from the window server, Docker, and this app) in the pinned environment of the earlier rounds (NumPy 2.5.4, xarray 2026.9.0, Numba 0.68.0, labelarray 0.1.0 for `main`): eight alternating rounds, each running 0.2.0, 0.3.0, and `main` (`9041058`) once in a rotated order, with and without `fast` where it exists; each figure the median of the rounds' medians of 401 runs, the rounds within 3% of each other. `fast` changes nothing at this size (104,064 values, below the compiled kernels' threshold), as expected. 0.3.0 against 0.2.0: grouped workloads 1.14–1.16 times faster on the NumPy backend and 2.65–2.83 on the xarray backend; the §7 pipelines 5–8% slower on both, within the 10% criterion, which confirms the release. `main` against 0.3.0: 5–9% slower on every workload, all of it the plan recording (decision 10, stage a): `main` with recording turned off measured within 1% of 0.3.0 in four further alternating rounds. Within 10% of the release before, but the §7 pipelines are then 11–15% slower than 0.2.0, so the recording's snapshots should get cheaper before the next release.

`main` is within 3% of 0.3.0 on every workload; recording costs 0–27 µs per pipeline, near the noise at this size (the same rounds with recording off differ from it by −5 to 27 µs). What remains against 0.2.0 is 0.3.0's own: its §7 pipelines are 7–9% slower than 0.2.0 here, within the criterion, so `main` sits 9–11% slower than 0.2.0 on them. The criterion compares each release with the one before, which `main` meets.

Grouping by several value keys (2026-10-26): done, staged for review, after a survey the owner asked for. dplyr, pandas (`observed=True`, the default since 3.0), and Polars give one row per combination that occurs, with the keys as columns (a MultiIndex in pandas); xarray (2024.09 and later) gives one axis per key with every combination, empty ones NaN; Open3D, PDAL, and MinkowskiEngine keep occupied voxels only. Measured on 200,000 points in 1 m voxels over 200 × 200 × 20 m: 97,492 occupied of an 800,000-cell grid (12.2%); pandas and Polars 27–30 ms, xarray 653 ms with flox and 10.1 s without, the grid 3.2 MB per float32 variable, which at 0.1 m voxels would be 800 million cells. Decided (the owner): one row per occupied combination, sorted by key, each key a variable along one new axis, `group` by default and named by `axis=`. `expr.MultiKey` is a value key holding the keys; resolving it factorizes each key as before (transforms included), numbers each position's combination of key codes, and keeps the combinations that occur in sorted order, which is each key's own order in turn (cells as numbers, not as their text), or sorts rows of codes when there are too many combinations to number. With `dropna`, a position missing any key is left out, with one note naming the keys that had missing values; `dropna=False` keeps missing values as groups, sorted last within their key. `summarise` puts each key's value per group first, as variables named after the keys, and ends the grouping by default, since the keys of one `group_by` call form one grouping (`groups="keep"` keeps it as the new axis); grouped `mutate`, `select` (which keeps the key variables), the cached groups, and `explain()` work as for one key. Errors: `axis=` with fewer than two value keys, keys along different axes, an axis name already used by the data or by a key, two keys with one name, and a summary named like a key. Timing (200,000 points, 96,846 voxels, NumPy backend): counting 5 ms, against 12 ms for pandas' floor and groupby; a float64 mean 7 ms; a float32 mean 118 ms, as slow with one key over the same groups (127 ms): labelarray's certificate cannot decide groups whose exact mean lies on a float32 tie, common for two-value groups, and decides them with Python fractions, which is labelarray's to make faster. Tests: `tests/test_multi_keys.py` (15, on both backends, against pandas), the README's example; the test that refused several keys now checks that they combine. Gates: the suite (2,163 passed) and minimum dependencies (2,104), the corpus identical on both backends, the demo unchanged, lint unchanged, and `scripts/check.sh` (Python 3.11–3.14 each 2,157 passed; minimum dependencies 2,069; the distributions).

Speed against 0.2.0 for the release note, measured with the machine loaded by other work (load 3.5–5.9 on 10 cores), so 0.2.0 and `main` were timed in four alternating rounds in environments with the same packages (NumPy 2.5.4, xarray 2026.9.0, Numba 0.68.0), medians of medians. With `fast`, every 10M-point workload is as fast as 0.2.0 or faster on both backends: grouped means 1.81–1.83 times, grouped centring 1.61–1.83, the §7 pipeline 1.03 (xarray) and 1.20 (NumPy), the formula form 1.05 and 1.42. Without `fast`, grouped workloads are 1.07–1.14 times faster and the NumPy backend's §7 pipelines unchanged (1.02–1.04), while the xarray backend's §7 pipelines are 0.84 and 0.80, the float64 three-value sum (decision 15). The 34,688-point workloads could not be judged under that load: `main` ran the same code at that size with and without `fast` (104,064 values is below the 300,000 threshold) and measured 0.79 and 0.55 ms. The pinned benchmark on a quiet machine remains to confirm them.

Precedents: pandas' grouped sums and means use Kahan compensation per group; Polars accumulates float32 means in float64 and returns the output type; Apache Arrow returns float64 for floating sums and means; DuckDB documents that its compensated `fsum` stays sensitive to input order; PyTorch documents that batched, sliced, and device-specific calculations may round differently; NumPy's own sums depend on axis and layout. The pandas and Polars descriptions are of current implementations, not guarantees.

### Milestone 11 — Lazy frames over the block driver

Planned (2026-10-27). Scope chosen by the owner from five candidates: blocks over any source, and several results from one pass. Left for later milestones: streaming output (a pipeline without `summarise` written block by block to a file, with the header's row count written last) and an automatic block size (a memory budget, with rows and threads chosen from the first block's measured cost per row). Left out for good: deferred verbs with no sample run, Polars' kind of laziness, which would drop the verb-time checks and `explain()` that decision 10 requires for a saving of about one second, the first block's run. What stage (b) showed is that the block driver already is the lazy design's useful part (each verb written once, checks at verb time, memory bounded by the blocks in flight, exact results), so this milestone widens it rather than building a second engine: no new mode, no second set of verbs, the same `ChunkedFrame`.

**Blocks over any source.** `tidy3a.chunked.Blocks` holds a file reopened per block; it becomes one of three sources behind the same `load(start, stop)`: a file (`.npy`, raw; today's), an in-memory NumPy array (`read_array(array, dims=..., blocks=rows)`, lifting the refusal; a block is a view, so what blocks buy is bounded intermediates, five block-sized arrays through five `mutate`s instead of five arrays the size of the data, and a cheap exact pass), and an xarray Dataset or DataArray, lazy or not (`tidy(data, blocks={"time": 100})`: a mapping, since a dataset has no storage order to make a first axis; the block is `data.isel({axis: slice(start, stop)})`, computed, so file-backed NetCDF and Zarr and Dask-backed data read one block at a time, then wrapped as `tidy()` wraps it, on the backend `backend_for` picks, which keeps storage encodings on xarray). The blocked axis must be an observation axis; the block holds every other axis whole, so for gridded data a block of time steps is that many whole grids, and the memory bound is rows times the grid. `blocks="chunks"` takes a Dask-backed source's chunk boundaries along the axis as the blocks, so each block computes whole chunks exactly once; it is the default when the data is chunked along that axis. This is the bounded-memory path for lazy gridded data that the Dask path lacked (stage (b): `filter`'s eager position labels), without rebuilding Dask support on the NumPy backend: Dask computes a block, tidy3a's verbs run on it in memory. The checks, `_execute`, `_concatenate`, and the tables do not change; `Blocks.labelled` and the source description come from the source.

**Several results from one pass.** Frames derived from one chunked frame share its `Blocks` and a common prefix of recorded verbs (the same `Verb` objects, so a prefix is found by identity). Today `per_ring = cloud >> ... >> summarise(...)` and `voxels = cloud >> ... >> summarise(...)` each read the file: 759 s each from the drive. `scan(*frames)` runs them in one pass: the frames' step tuples form a trie; per block, the block is loaded once and the trie walked depth first with the frame after each prefix memoised, so the shared `mutate` and `filter` run once, and each leaf takes its partial table (a summarised frame) or its rows (one that joins) from the intermediate it hangs off; the tables merge as blocks arrive, as now. The second pass, where a leaf's float32 estimates cannot settle a group, is one more shared scan: each leaf settles first (`labelarray.sums.settle`, public, separately from `from_chunks`), the unsettled groups of every leaf and reduction are gathered, one pass computes their `chunk_exact` buckets together, and `exact_results` finishes them; nothing in labelarray changes. Threads map over blocks as now, each worker walking the whole trie for its block. After `scan`, each frame holds its result, so `pull`, `collect()`, and `explain()` behave as they do after a single run; `scan` returns the results as a tuple (`per_ring, voxels = scan(a, b)`), skips frames already computed, and refuses frames from different sources. `_run()` becomes `scan` of one frame, so there is one code path. What it costs: the trie walk is the per-block work of the leaves' distinct suffixes, so two branches sharing `mutate` and `filter` cost one scan plus the second branch's `group_by` and summaries per block; on the cached nuScenes run that is about 8 s plus a few, against 16 s for two runs, and from the drive one 759 s read against two.

Steps: 1. the source protocol with the array and dataset sources, tested on an in-memory array, a file-backed NetCDF fixture (as `tests/test_dask.py` builds one), and a Dask-backed dataset with `blocks="chunks"`, counting that each chunk is read once; results against the same pipelines in memory, bit for bit where stage (b) promises it. 2. The step trie and `scan`, tested with a counting verb that proves the shared prefix runs once per block, with results identical to separate runs, with a leaf that joins rows beside leaves that summarise, and with the shared exact pass on the tie corpus. 3. README and CHANGELOG. 4. Measured on nuScenes, cached and from the drive, two branches (per-ring summary and voxels) in one scan against two runs. Exit: a lazy NetCDF or Dask-backed dataset runs a filter-and-summarise pipeline with memory bounded by the block, and two results from one file read it once, each the same bits as its own run.

Step 1 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `tidy3a.chunked.Source` has three implementations behind one `block(start, stop)`: `FileSource` (today's, the map reopened per block), `ArraySource` (`read_array(array, ..., blocks=rows)`, the refusal lifted; a block is a view), and `DatasetSource` (`tidy(data, blocks={"time": 100})`, with `"chunks"` as the size, or `blocks="chunks"` alone when exactly one axis is Dask-chunked; a `TidyArray` goes through `to_xarray()`; the block is `isel` along the axis, computed, wrapped by `tidy()` with the same `field_axis=` and `backend=`). `Blocks` holds the source, the size or `"chunks"`, and the threads, and gives the spans from either. Summaries over blocks with other axes now work, which gridded data needs: a reduction's argument may vary along the blocked axis and others, and is flattened row first with its group codes repeated per row, so a block of seven time steps of a 5 × 6 grid summarises 210 values per step; `n()` counts the other shared observation axes per row, as `_count` does in memory; the float, integer, and spread pieces carry their own value counts, since a mean over a grid divides by values, not rows. Measured nothing new: the step is API and plumbing. Tests (`tests/test_blocks.py`, both backends): an array in memory in blocks, summarised and joined; an xarray dataset in blocks, summarised and joined, with `explain()`'s preview; a Dask-backed dataset of delayed chunk reads with `blocks={"point": "chunks"}` and `"chunks"` alone, on 1 and 3 threads, each chunk read once for the run and once for the first block, the same bits as in memory; unequal chunks; a `(time, lat, lon)` grid blocked along time, grouped by a key along time and ungrouped, with `n()`, `mean`, `max`, `min`, and `std` against the in-memory results; the dataset options refused (`"chunks"` on unchunked data or a file, a missing axis, two axes, zero positions). Not done: Zarr and NetCDF fixtures themselves (the Dask-backed fixture stands for them, since `isel` and `compute` are what a block does on either).

Step 2 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `tidy3a.scan(*frames)` runs several chunked frames over their source in one pass and gives their results as a tuple. The frames' row-wise verbs form a trie keyed by verb identity (`_trie`; `group_by` and `summarise` are each leaf's own work on its rows, so two summaries after the same `filter` share one node); per block, the block is loaded once and the trie walked depth first (`_walk`), each prefix's frame alive while its branches run and each verb run once per block; a summarising leaf takes its partial table from the frame at its node, a joining leaf its rows. The tables merge as the blocks arrive, in order, as before. The second pass is shared: each table settles first (`labelarray.sums.settle`), the combinations no error bound could settle are gathered across every leaf and float32 sum, and one more pass over the blocks computes their `chunk_exact` buckets together, at most `EXACT_PAIRS` combinations in flight per block (a larger set takes several passes, whole requests at a time); `exact_results` finishes them and `min_count` applies as `from_chunks` does. `_run()` is `scan` of one frame, so there is one code path; after a scan each frame holds its result, so `pull`, `collect()`, and `explain()` behave as after a single run, a computed frame is given as it is, the same frame twice runs once, and frames from different `read_array()` or `tidy()` calls, or frames in memory, are refused with a `BlockError`. Tests (`tests/test_blocks.py`, both backends): a counting verb in the shared prefix, run once on the first block and once per block for two summaries in one scan (8 runs for 7 blocks), with each result the same bits as its pipeline in memory, and no run at all when the frames are scanned again; a joining frame beside two summarising ones, one at the root with no row-wise verb, on 1 and 3 threads; a frame that is another's prefix, scanned together; the shared exact pass on the tie corpus, two branches with unsettled float32 sums, counted as one pass over the blocks (3 runs per block in all) with `EXACT_PAIRS` as shipped, and as one pass per combination with `EXACT_PAIRS` patched to 1, the same bits either way; a Dask-backed dataset with three frames in one scan, each chunk read once; the refusals. Not measured yet: step 4 takes the nuScenes numbers.

Review of 3c453fd (2026-10-27): an outside review of steps 1 and 2 found two memory problems and seven differences from the in-memory results; every one reproduced, and each is fixed with a regression test on both backends (`tests/test_blocks.py`, the section headed "Review of 3c453fd"). Memory: the second pass finished each request's buckets as its pass ended (`exact_results` within the pass, so what a leaf keeps is one float32 per settled combination, not 2 KB of buckets), and the pool no longer runs ahead through every block: `_in_order` starts at most `threads + 1` blocks ahead of the one whose result is merged next, so the results waiting in order stay bounded however unevenly the blocks take (`pool.map` had submitted every block at once, and a slow first block let the rest queue their finished results). Equivalence: each block is labelled with its positions on the whole axis when the axis has no labels of its own, so `col("point")` and a filter by position see what they see in memory, and the first block is shown unlabelled as in memory (its positions are its own); the joined result drops the labels again when no filter ran. `dot()` summing over the blocked axis, and a formula whose `\sum` lowers to one, are refused at verb time (formulas are lowered against the first block to see what they sum). Integer means add in float64, as `np.mean` does, so four values of 2**62 mean 2**62; integer sums wrap in int64 as `np.sum` does. Joined rows keep the coordinates along the blocked axis, the attributes, and the storage encodings: the backends join their own collections (`join_along`, with `label_positions` and `unlabel` added to the protocol) instead of rebuilding from `to_dict()`. `tidy(frame, blocks=)` carries the frame's field axis and backend, and refuses a grouped frame (a grouping needs every position of a group). A group of missing values first seen in a later block gives NaN for `min` and `max` with `na_rm=True` (the empty slot is filled with NaN where NaN is skipped, not ±inf), and an empty group gives NaN. A single `bins()` key lists every bin, as in memory, with the empty bins' values (`_Table.complete`); several keys list the combinations seen, as in memory. A dataset whose variables have different Dask chunks along the axis takes an explicit block size (chunk discovery no longer fails on it). The review also recommended, as a design matter, making the first-block run optional with an explicit preview and treating laziness as an execution policy; that is recorded under the decisions below, not acted on.

Step 3 status (2026-10-27): done. The README's larger-than-memory example builds two branches off one `filter` and scans them, with a paragraph on what `scan` shares; the CHANGELOG has the entry.

Step 4 status (2026-10-27): done, cached and from the drive. From the drive: the same two branches over the 29.4 GB file (the sweeps written twelve times, 1.44 billion points kept) on the USB drive, 24 GB machine, blocks of 2M on four threads: both in one scan 930.6 s at a peak of 1.63 GB; per ring alone 883.6 s at 1.28 GB; voxels alone 882.9 s at 1.42 GB. So the two results cost 931 s in one scan against 1,767 s as two runs, 53%, at 32–33 MB/s, the drive's speed, for every run; the scan's extra 47 s over one run is the second branch's keys and reductions, which a cached run showed cost about 4 s per 2.45 GB. Every result in the scan is the same bits as its own run, and against one copy of the data (the cached runs) the rings and voxels are the same, every count is twelve times, and every mean is the same bits. The scan and per-ring runs ran the code at 56ff56d; the voxel run started later, on the working tree with the records step under way, and its results are the same bits. Cached, as measured first: nuScenes Mini's 122.6M sweep points (2.45 GB, in the page cache), blocks of 2M points on four threads, two branches off one `mutate` and `filter` (distance within 50 m): a per-ring summary (`n()`, `mean(intensity)`, `mean(distance)`; 32 rings) and 1 m voxels (`n()`, `mean(intensity)`; 84,475 voxels). Each as its own run: per ring 3.7–4.1 s at a peak of 2.0 GB, voxels 4.9–5.1 s at 2.3 GB; both in one scan: 7.6–7.8 s at 2.3 GB, against 8.6–9.2 s for the two runs. Every result in the scan is the same bits as its own run, and the voxels, counts, and means are the same bits as the single run measured for stage (b). What the scan saves when the file is cached is the shared work only, the read, `mutate`, and `filter`, about 1 s of 8.6: the branches' own work (the voxel keys, the reductions) is most of a cached run. From the drive the read is the run (759 s at 36 MB/s for one pass), so one scan should take about one run's time against two; that measurement needs the drive connected. For comparison, the code at 3c453fd gave the voxels in 5.0–5.3 s at 2.1–2.3 GB under the same conditions, so the review's fixes cost nothing measurable; the stage (b) numbers (8.0 s, 1.65 GB) were taken with the file not yet in the page cache.

**Exit (2026-10-27): met**, on a NetCDF4 file larger than memory. The file: two years hourly (17,520 steps) on a 1° grid (180 × 360), seven float32 fields (temperature, pressure, humidity, two wind components, precipitation, cloud), plus `hour` and `month` along time; 31.8 GB, chunked by day per field, uncompressed, written to the USB drive (859 s); the machine has 25.8 GB of memory. Opened with `xr.open_dataset` (the netCDF4 backend's lazy arrays, no Dask) and `tidy(data, blocks={"time": 168}, threads=4)`: weeks, 105 blocks. The pipelines share a daytime `filter` on `hour` and branch into a monthly temperature summary (`n()`, `mean`, `max` of Celsius) and a monthly wind summary (`mean` and `max` of `sqrt(u² + v²)`). Building both, with `repr` and `explain()`: 0.69 s on the cold file at a peak of 0.13 GB, nothing read. `preview()`: 22.9 s for blocks 1, 53, and 105 (384 of 17,520 steps), 1.67 GB. Both results in one scan: 1,003.6 s at a peak of **2.10 GB**; the temperature summary alone: 1,030.0 s at 2.07 GB, so the second result cost nothing; both at 31–32 MB/s, the drive's speed. Against an in-memory reference that loads, month by month, only the five variables the results use and runs the same verbs: every column of both results the same bits (counts, float32 means, maxima), and the counts are the calendar's (January 31 days × 13 daytime hours × 2 years = 806; February 741; December 793, as the file ends on 30 December 2025). **What it shows next:** each block computes all seven fields, so the temperature summary reads 31.8 GB where 4.5 GB of temperature, plus `hour` and `month`, would do; at the drive's speed, about 150 s instead of 1,030 s. The reference, which loads only what it uses, read 13.6 GB in 512 s. Column pruning (a block computes only the variables the pipeline reads) is the next milestone's first step.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

Decided (2026-10-27): all three as recommended.

1. **The name.** `scan(*frames)`, or `tidy3a.collect(*frames)`; the latter reads naturally but means something else from `frame.collect()`, which gives an xarray Dataset. Recommendation: `scan`, one scan of the source.
2. **`blocks=` for datasets.** A mapping naming the axis, or an integer taken as the first axis of the first variable. Recommendation: the mapping; a dataset has no storage order, and naming the axis says what the memory bound is.
3. **The default for chunked data.** `blocks="chunks"` when the source is Dask-chunked along the axis, else the user must give a size. Recommendation: yes, the chunking is the storage's own block size.
4. **Open (2026-10-27): the first-block run.** A reviewer of 3c453fd would make the first block's run optional: lazy frames would record verbs with checks on metadata where possible, an explicit `preview()` would run a sample, and `collect()` or `scan()` would run the plan through the block engine; laziness would be an execution policy, not a property of the input. The gains claimed: no I/O or computation while a pipeline is built, variables selected before they are read (a `DatasetSource` block computes every variable of the dataset today, dropped ones included), unused branches assembled cheaply, and whole-axis verbs found before any block runs. What it would cost: the verb-time errors and `explain()` that decision 10 requires come from running the first block, and the review itself notes that a first block passing is no guarantee for later blocks (a block's own errors still surface during the run). Recommendation: keep the first-block run as the default, and take the two gains that need no new mode: push `select` into the source so a block loads only the variables the pipeline reads (worth measuring on wide datasets), and let `read_array(..., blocks=rows)` and `tidy(..., blocks=)` skip the first block's run on request (`preview=False`), which then gives a frame whose checks happen at collection. Decide after step 4's measurements.

   **Decided (2026-10-27) by the plan's owner: signatures, plus an optional preview.** The recommendation above is set aside. Signatures alone leave the value-level problems to block 40; running the first block catches more, but it is a real read (from the drive, a block of 2M points), and it can mislead, since the first block is not always representative of the rest. This follows the owner's original design notes (each node's output signature known without data; eager kept for debugging) and the reviewer's recommendation. Design, to be planned as its own milestone:

   - **Building a pipeline reads nothing.** `read_array(..., blocks=)` and `tidy(..., blocks=)` take the source's schema from its metadata: the variables, their axes and dtypes, the sizes of every axis, and the labels of the axes that are not blocked (a file's from `dims=` and `labels=`; a dataset's from its coordinates, small axes only). Nothing is computed or mapped.
   - **Signatures by running the verbs on a zero-row block.** Each verb is checked when it is called by running it on an empty block with the source's schema, so the verbs stay written once and the checks are the eager checks: unknown names, axes, and field labels; the block checks (reductions inside `mutate` or `filter`, `dot` and formula sums over the blocked axis, `median`, `over=`); every result's variables, axes, and dtypes. Measured (2026-10-27) on the block pipelines, both backends: the summaries' dtypes on zero rows match the full run's. Three things need care: (1) a Dask source's empty block must be built from the variables' dtypes and shapes, since `isel(0:0).compute()` still computed the first chunk; (2) a key whose labels are text (`cells`, `rounded`) gives float64 labels on zero rows, so a key's label type comes from its transform, not from the empty run; (3) the notes an empty block writes ("all removed by filter") are dropped. The registry's signature functions in the owner's notes can replace the zero-row run verb by verb later, where it is wrong or slow.
   - **`preview()` runs a sample, explicitly.** `frame.preview()` runs the pipeline on a few blocks spread along the axis (by default the first, middle, and last, so a sample is not only the start of the file) and returns the result in memory, with `explain()` measured on the sample and labelled as one; `preview(blocks=[0])` names the blocks. It reads those blocks, and says so. The sample's result is never kept as the frame's result.
   - **Errors at run time name the verb and the block.** A value-level error (values outside every bin, a key's text where a number was expected) names the verb as the user wrote it, as now, and the block: "in block 40 of 147 (positions 78,000,001–80,000,000)".
   - **What a frame shows.** Without a preview, `repr` and `explain()` give the schema-level effect of each step (axes, variables, dtypes; sizes that depend on the data shown as unknown) and say that nothing has been read; after `preview()`, the sample's measured effects; after `collect()` or `scan()`, every block's.
   - **Cost to users: none yet.** `blocks=` is unreleased (CHANGELOG, "Unreleased"), so the first-block run can go before anyone depends on it.
   - Steps: 1. the schema and an empty block from each source without a read (a counting source proves it); 2. `ChunkedFrame` checks verbs on the empty block and no longer reads the first; 3. `preview()`; 4. the block in run-time errors; 5. `repr` and `explain()`. Tests: building a pipeline reads nothing, from a file, an array, and a Dask-backed dataset; every existing eager-against-blocks comparison unchanged; the errors a first block caught are still caught at verb time, or named with their block at run time. Found while checking: `std()` and `var()` of text fail with NumPy's `TypeError`, eagerly too, rather than a `SummariseError` naming the variable; to fix with this work.

   Step 1 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `Source.empty()` gives a block of no positions with the source's schema without reading: a file's from the dtype (native byte order, as `FileSource.block` gives) and position shape `read_array` already has, so the file is not even mapped; an array's as a zero-length view; a dataset's by slicing to no positions and replacing every lazy value along the axis, data or coordinate, with an empty NumPy array of its type, a `DataArray`'s own values included. Tests: no reopen for `.npy` files, big-endian included; no Dask chunk computed, for a Dataset and a DataArray with a lazy coordinate along the axis (`isel(point=slice(0, 0)).compute()` computed the first); the empty block's variables, axes, and types are the first block's with zero positions. Nothing uses it yet: step 2 does. Checking the block verbs on it found a fourth thing for step 2 to handle: **`min()` and `max()` of no positions are float64**, whatever the type, in memory too (labelarray's `_reduce_data` fills with `np.full(shape, np.nan)`, and the xarray backend with `astype(float)`), so float32 becomes float64 and times become a float NaN rather than NaT; integers have no missing value, so float64 is the only choice there. The fix keeps a float type's own NaN and gives times NaT; it needs a labelarray release, so it is recorded here and pinned by a test (`test_the_block_verbs_on_an_empty_block_give_the_types_of_a_run`) that fails when it lands.

   Step 4 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. An error raised while a block runs, tidy3a's or labelarray's (which `translating` converts at `collect()`), keeps its class and gains a line naming the block: "in block 3 of 4 (positions 2,001–3,000 on point)", in the first pass and the exact pass. Tested with a value outside every bin that only block 3 holds, on 1 and 3 threads, through `pull` and through `scan`.

   Reductions of text and times (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `sum`, `mean`, `std`, `var`, and `median` of text, and `sum`, `std`, `var`, `median`, `any`, and `all` of times, failed inside NumPy on both backends, whole or grouped; each is now a `SummariseError` (a `MutateError` in a grouped `mutate`) naming the reduction and the variable, and saying what does work (`n()` and `group_by` for text; `min`, `max`, and their difference for times). Left as they are, each a backend difference to settle on its own: `min` and `max` of text work on xarray and fail on NumPy (labelarray has no loop for text); `any` and `all` of text give `True` on NumPy and `1.0` on xarray; `mean` of times works on xarray and fails on NumPy. Also seen: `any` and `all` of floats give float64, not bool, on both backends, with or without data.

   Steps 2, 3, and 5 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. **Step 2:** `ChunkedFrame.open` wraps `Source.empty()`; each verb runs eagerly on it, with NumPy's warnings about empty means silenced, so the eager checks are the checks and nothing is read while a pipeline is built; the first block is read once, with the others, when a result is asked for (a Dask-backed source has each chunk computed exactly once now, where the first was computed twice). Each step's snapshot marks the sizes that are 0 on the empty block unknown (`None`, printed `?`): the blocked axis and the groups a key finds, while a `bins()` axis keeps its count; and leaves out the notes the emptiness wrote (a filter on no positions "removes all"), which `_notes` also drops from a summary. The cast of a merged summary to the type it has in memory now takes the empty block's types, except where the data decides the type (`_data_decides`: an integer `min` or `max` is float64 when a group is empty and on the empty block always, and the integers' own type otherwise), where the merge's type, which follows the in-memory rule, stands. Value-level errors (a value outside every bin) move from verb time to the run, where they name their block. **Step 3:** `preview(blocks=None)` runs the frame's verbs on a sample through the same scan (`Blocks.chosen`; a block's number still counts among every block, so an error in a sample names its place in the whole), by default the first, middle, and last blocks, `blocks=` naming others from 1; it reads those blocks only (twice where the exact pass is needed), gives the result in memory with a note and an `explain()` header saying which blocks of how many positions it covers, and never sets the frame's result. **Step 5:** a frame's `repr` without a result is its schema: each variable with its axes and sizes (`?` where unknown), the field axis, the groups, the notes, and the blocks, with "nothing read yet"; `explain()` gives each step's effect on the schema, the header "nothing read yet: 7 blocks of 3,000 positions on point", and, once a result is computed, the last step measured on every block. Tests (`tests/test_blocks.py`, both backends): no reopen of the file while a pipeline is built and `repr`/`explain()` read, then one per block when a result is asked for; no Dask chunk computed before the run, each exactly once in it; the sizes shown as `?`, the `bins()` count shown, no emptiness note; the sample of blocks 1, 4, and 7 the same bits as the same pipeline in memory on those rows, with its note and header, and the frame's own result afterwards unchanged; named blocks, rows from a sample with the whole axis's positions, a sample's error naming block 3 of 4, and wrong block numbers refused. The README's example previews before it scans.

   The four things step 1 found for step 2 (2026-10-27): the Dask empty block and the dropped emptiness notes are done (above); a text key's labels are numbers on the empty block, which nothing reads (a summary's key labels come from the merged groups), so it stays pinned by its test; `min()` and `max()` of no positions are fixed where they arise, in labelarray's `_reduce_data` (a float or complex type's own NaN, a time type's NaT, NaN among objects; float64 only for integers and booleans), and in the xarray backend's own empty case, which had the same `astype(float)`. That also fixed a crash: `min` of times grouped by `bins()` with an empty bin failed on the NumPy backend, as labelarray could not stack a float NaN with times. The labelarray change needs a release (0.1.4) before tidy3a can require it; until then tidy3a's tests pass against the local labelarray and the minimum-dependency check would not.

   The backend differences (2026-10-27): settled. `min` and `max` of text work on both backends and give objects (labelarray's `_reduce_data` compares text as objects; the xarray backend, which gave objects whole and NumPy text grouped, compares them as objects too). `any` and `all` of text are refused, as in R and dplyr; the backends gave `True` and `1.0`. `std` and `var` of durations, which failed in NumPy on both backends, are refused by name. The mean of times stays the xarray backend's: matching xarray's datetime mean bit for bit on the NumPy backend would follow xarray's algorithm, which changed in 2025.01, across the versions tidy3a supports; the NumPy backend refuses it by name, pointing to `backend="xarray"`. On blocks, `min` and `max` of times, durations, and text merge as in memory (the merge filled unseen groups from `np.iinfo`, which times and text have no extremes in), and an integer `min` of an empty `bins()` group is float64 NaN as in memory, where the merge had shown its fill, the largest integer (from the bins listed in 64a2119).
5. **Decided (2026-10-27) by the plan's owner: node identity by structural hash.** Each node's identity is a hash of its operation, its settings, and its inputs' hashes, so two pipelines written separately that do the same work hash the same, and the hash keys every cache: key codes, the scan's shared prefixes (the trie today compares verbs by identity), and partial results. Three rules from the owner: sources have a stable identity (a file's path, modification time, and size; an in-memory array's identity, never its contents); settings are hashable and canonical (transform records hold plain values, floats a fixed representation); and anything touching a lambda is unique, a taint that passes to everything downstream. The pitfall is false sharing, two nodes that hash equal but behave differently, tested by checking that every settings field is in the hash. What exists: labelarray's transforms already compare and hash by value through `_settings()`; tidy3a's expressions (`Ref`, `BinOp`, `Call`, `Reduce`, `Literal`, `ValueKey`, `MultiKey`) and verbs compare by identity, and a verb's options live partly in its `_apply` closure. Additions recommended with it:

   - **Hash is the index; equality is the truth.** Hash a canonical encoding with `hashlib.blake2b` (stable across processes, unlike Python's `hash`, whose strings are salted per process; it matters once caches outlive a run), and on a hit compare the canonical encodings too, so a collision cannot share. A missed share costs time; a false share costs a wrong result, so every doubtful case hashes apart.
   - **Types are part of a value.** Python has `1 == 1.0 == True` with equal hashes, but `x * 1` and `x * 1.0` give different dtypes for an integer `x`, and integer and float edges can label bins differently. The encoding is (type, value), floats as `float.hex()` (exact; one NaN; `-0.0` apart from `0.0`, which only misses a share).
   - **In-memory identity is a token, not `id()`.** `id()` is reused once an object is freed, which is exactly a false share; a source gets a token when it is opened and keeps the array alive while the token is in use. An array changed in place keeps its token, so a cache that outlives one scan can serve stale results: caches are per scan to begin with, and a cache across scans needs a cheap check or a documented rule.
   - **Files: more than path, time, and size.** The resolved path, size, `st_mtime_ns`, and inode where the file system has a stable one (exFAT's times are 10 ms; FAT32's 2 s), checked again when a run ends, so a file rewritten during a run refuses its results. A Dask-backed source has an identity already: Dask names its graphs by `dask.base.tokenize`, a structural hash of the same kind; use it rather than another.
   - **Hash expressions, not only verbs.** Sharing then reaches inside verbs: `mean(intensity)` in two different `summarise` calls shares its partial pieces, and `cells(x, 1)` shares its codes between groupings that differ in their other keys, as long as the rows beneath are the same (the input hash carries every filter above). A key's codes hash its expression, its transform, `dropna`, and its input.
   - **What taints.** Lambdas in `where(predicate)` selectors; a formula's names from the caller's scope, unless their values are plain (a formula's identity holds the values of the names it uses, not its whole scope); `env()` values that are arrays (by token, as sources). A verb's closure is not hashed: its operation and recorded arguments are, so every verb records all its options (an audit of `verbs.py`, enforced by the test below).
   - **Testing false sharing.** Settings become frozen dataclasses (tidy3a's nodes) or keep `_settings()` (labelarray's transforms), so a test enumerates every field or constructor argument, changes one at a time, and asserts the hash changes; a field added later is caught without editing the test. Then the type cases (`1`, `1.0`, `True`; `-0.0`, `0.0`; NaN), and a property test: equal hashes give equal results on random data, both backends.
   - **Order.** Decision 4 and this want the same thing first, verbs and expressions as records with all their settings explicit, so they share their first step; the scan's trie then keys on hashes, which is a small change in `_Node.after`.

   Records step status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. Expressions (`Ref`, `NameRef`, `Literal`, `Vector`, `BinOp`, `UnaryOp`, `Call`, `Reduce`) and keys (`AxisKey`, `ValueKey`, `MultiKey`) are frozen dataclasses; `==` still builds expressions, so identity is a separate value, `tidy3a.identity.structure`: a frozen dataclass is its type and every field, read from the dataclass, so a field added later is in it without being listed; values carry their types (floats by `float.hex`); arrays, xarray objects, and frames are `Same` (identity, held so the `id` is not reused); callables and unknown types are `Unique`. `Verb.structure()` is its operation and recorded arguments; a verb without a recorded operation (`bind_cols`, the joins) is `Unique`; the text is left out, since it shows objects by their `repr`. Selectors record their helper and arguments; formulas their source, `bind`, and the plain values and references their names pick up from the caller's scope (the caller's functions play no part, so an imported `sqrt` does not taint a formula). The scan's trie matches verbs by structure, so pipelines written apart share their common steps; Python's own equality of these tuples is the comparison for now, and blake2b waits for a cache that outlives a run. Found and fixed on the way, each a false share a hash would have made: `group_by` kept `dropna` and `axis` only in its closure; `starts_with("x")` and `starts_with("x", ignore_case=False)` had the same text and no settings; `np.float64` is a subclass of `float`, so `x * np.float64(1)` would have matched `x * 1.0`, though NumPy 2 promotes float32 data with the first and not the second. Also fixed: `any_of()` read a generator twice and selected nothing; `select(~selector)` printed an object address in its text. Tests (`tests/test_identity.py`, both backends): five pipelines written twice match; every named option of every block verb changes the structure, and a coverage test fails when a verb gains an option not checked; arguments, selector settings, and key transforms each change it; every field of every node changes it; values of different types (`1`, `1.0`, `True`, NumPy scalars, `-0.0`) are apart and NaN is one value; arrays match by identity only; a caller's function and a verb with no operation match nothing but themselves; formulas take the values their names use; a scan of two pipelines written apart runs their common steps once per block with the in-memory results, and a lambda keeps both branches apart.

### Milestone 12 — Reading only what is used, and writing what does not fit

Planned (2026-10-27). Scope chosen by the owner from four candidates: column pruning and streaming output. Left for later: the operation registry of the owner's design notes (each reduction's partial, merge, and finalize as entries keyed by name) and an automatic block size. Milestone 11 bounded memory by the block; it did not bound what a block reads, nor the size of a result. Its exit measurement showed the first: on the 31.8 GB NetCDF file, every block computes all seven fields, so a temperature summary read 31.8 GB where about 4.5 GB would do (1,030 s at the drive's speed, against about 150 s). The second is a limit of `_joined`: a pipeline without `summarise` joins the blocks' rows in memory, so its result must fit.

**Column pruning.** A block computes only the variables the frame reads: for a scan, those any of its frames reads. What a frame reads is found on the empty block, where every verb already runs (decision 4): the verbs' expressions are resolved there, so a `Context` that records each variable it resolves (a name, a field label through its holder, an axis's coordinate) gives the variables read, formulas and selectors included, as the data's own names; a frame that joins its rows also needs every variable its result keeps. Anything the record cannot vouch for reads everything: a `where(predicate)` selector, which looks at values, a verb with no recorded operation, a verb that writes a field label (its holder is rewritten whole). `DatasetSource.block` then takes `data[needed]` before `isel` and `compute`, so a lazy or Dask-backed dataset reads only those variables' chunks; a file's records hold every field of a row together, so `FileSource` gains nothing and keeps reading rows whole. One trap: `n()` counts the observation axes every variable shares (`_per_position`, `_count`), so dropping `hour` and `month`, which lie along time only, would turn hours into grid cells; `n()` takes its axes from the full schema, not the pruned block. The results are the same bits as without pruning, which every block test checks by running with and without it.

**Streaming output.** `write(path)`, a terminal verb, runs a pipeline that keeps rows and writes them block by block, in order, as the blocks arrive (`_in_order` already bounds those waiting), so the result need not fit in memory: a `.npy` file for a single array (NumPy only; the header's row count is written last), or a Zarr store for a dataset (`to_zarr(append_dim=axis)`, xarray and zarr installed), each written under a temporary name and renamed when every block is in, so a failed run leaves no partial file under the name given. Positions labels, attributes, and storage encodings are written as `collect()` would give them, and reading the file back gives the same bits as `collect()` for data that fits. On data in memory, `write` writes the frame whole. In a scan, a writing frame beside summarising ones shares the blocks' read as a joining one does.

Steps: 1. column pruning, with a test that counts each variable's Dask chunk reads (a summary of one variable reads only it, the key, and what `n()` needs none of), every existing block test run with pruning on and off, and the fallbacks; measured on the NetCDF file (the temperature summary alone, against 1,030 s). 2. `write(path)` to `.npy` and Zarr, tested by reading back against `collect()`, with a failed block leaving no file, in a scan beside a summary, and with memory bounded on a result larger than a block. 3. README and CHANGELOG. 4. Measured: a filter of the 29.4 GB `.npy` file (points within 50 m, about 98% of them, more than memory) written back to the drive, peak memory against the block, and read back. Exit: a summary of one field of a wide dataset reads only that field and its keys, the same bits as before; a result larger than memory is written at bounded memory and reads back the same.

Step 1 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. While a frame read in blocks checks each verb on its empty block, `tidy3a.expr.recording_reads` collects the variables the verb reads: `resolve` notes a variable, a field label's holder, or an axis's coordinate; `resolve_vector` its variables or holder; a field-label write in `mutate` the holder, which is rewritten whole; a `where(predicate)` of the caller's notes `EVERYTHING`. Each `ChunkedFrame` carries its steps' reads (`_reads`, None for everything); `_needs` adds, for a frame that keeps rows, the variables its result keeps; a scan loads the union, and `DatasetSource.block` takes `data[needed]` before `isel` and `compute` (with none named, the smallest variable along the axis, so the block still has it). `n()` takes its per-row count from the schema the verbs ran on (`_Plan.per_position`, from the empty block), not from the pruned block. `DatasetSource` chooses the backend once, for the whole data, so a block holding some variables is on the same backend. Measured on the 31.8 GB NetCDF file of milestone 11's exit, the same blocks and threads: the temperature summary alone **158.1 s at a peak of 0.55 GB, against 1,030.0 s at 2.07 GB** without pruning (it reads temperature, hour, and month: 4.5 GB, at the drive's speed); both results in one scan **459.5 s at 1.05 GB, against 1,003.6 s at 2.10 GB** (temperature and the two wind fields, 13.6 GB); every column of both the same bits as the unpruned run, so as the in-memory reference. Tests (`tests/test_blocks.py`, both backends): a Dask-backed grid of delayed reads counted per variable, where a summary reads only its field and `n()` still counts hours, not grid cells; `n()` alone reads one variable along the axis; keys, formulas, filters, and kept rows are read and nothing else; a caller's predicate reads everything; a field label written reads its holder; every block on the backend chosen for the whole data; the existing Dask test reads only the three variables its pipeline uses, and the scan test the four its three frames use. Every other block test runs pruned (the default) against its in-memory result. Found on the way: a `where(predicate)` that looks at values runs on the empty block when the verb is checked, so a predicate that answers differently on no values picks other columns than it would on data; and on blocks each block could pick other columns. Fixed (2026-10-27): a frame read in blocks refuses a caller's `where(...)` when `select` is called, before the verb runs on the empty block (`_refuse_value_selectors`), naming the alternatives: select by name or type, or `preview()` a sample and select by name; `where(is_numeric)`, which looks only at types, is kept. The `EVERYTHING` read stays, for in-memory frames and any value-looking read a later verb adds. Tests: a lambda selector refused plain, excluded, and in a list, with nothing read, and `where(is_numeric)` still summarising; the identity test that kept two branches apart with lambdas now keeps them apart with `x * 1` and `x * 1.0`.

Step 2 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `tidy3a.write.write(path, overwrite=False)` is a verb with a recorded operation and arguments; `tidy3a.write.Writer` writes a `.npy` file (one variable, its type a number or time, the blocked axis first; the header's shape padded to a fixed width, so its row count is written last over the same bytes) or a Zarr store (`to_zarr` with `mode="w"` for the first block that has rows, then `append_dim`; `consolidated=False`, which Zarr 3 would warn about; only the encodings Zarr shares with NetCDF kept; text as variable-length strings, so a later block's longer text is not cut to the first block's width), under `.{name}.partial-{pid}` beside the path, renamed when every block is in; `abort` removes it. A path that exists (unless `overwrite=True`), a suffix not `.npy` or `.zarr`, and a `.npy` of several variables or of text are refused when the verb is called, before any block. On a `ChunkedFrame` that keeps rows, `write` is a leaf of the scan whose blocks go to the writer in order, never into `_joined`, the blocks' positions dropped where joining them would (no filter ran); `scan(*frames, writes=[(frame, path)])` writes beside other results in the same pass, and a failure in any block aborts every writer before it is raised, naming its block. `write` gives the file back as a frame: in blocks of the same size for data read in blocks (`read_array` of the `.npy` with its dimensions, the other axes' labels, the field axis, and the name; `tidy` of `xr.open_zarr`), lazily otherwise. A summary, which fits in memory, is written whole. `zarr>=2.18` is a new extra (`tidy3a[zarr]`, with xarray) and in `dev`; `write.py` may import xarray inside a function, as `tests/test_backend.py` allows it. Tests (`tests/test_write.py`, both backends): a `.npy` file read back as `collect()` gives it, in memory and in blocks of 7, 1,500, and 20,000 positions, `np.load` reading the header's count; a Zarr store likewise, with the filter's positions; rows never joined in memory (`_joined` patched to fail); text of any width, times, attributes, and NetCDF encodings dropped; an existing path, a wrong suffix, and two variables in a `.npy` refused before a block is read, and `overwrite=True`; a value only block 3 holds failing there with no file left under the name or beside it; a write beside a summary reading each block once; a pipeline going on from what it wrote; a summary written whole; an empty result written as an empty array. Found on the way: a verb's own function is given the `ChunkedFrame` itself, so a verb that pipes it into another verb (`frame >> pull(...)`) asks that frame for a result and runs every block at verb time; tidy3a's verbs work on `frame._data` and do not.

Step 3 status (2026-10-27): done with steps 1 and 2: the README's larger-than-memory section says a dataset's blocks hold only the variables read, and its example writes the near points to a `.npy` file and prints the shape read back; the CHANGELOG's Unreleased section has both.

Step 4 status (2026-10-27): done. The points within 50 m of the 29.4 GB sweeps file (the nuScenes sweeps written twelve times, read as a raw float32 file in blocks of 2M points on four threads) written back to the same USB drive with `>> select("values") >> write(path)`: **1,637.1 s at a peak of 1.01 GB**, reading 29.4 GB and writing 28.8 GB through the one drive (36 MB/s together); the file holds 1,440,046,896 rows of five float32 fields, 28,800,938,048 bytes, its header's count written last, more than the machine's 25.8 GB of memory. Read back in blocks and summarised per ring (`n()`, `mean(intensity)`, and `mean` of the distance recomputed from the written x, y, z): **824.4 s at 0.71 GB**, and every column the same bits as the earlier drive run over the source with the same filter (milestone 11, step 4), so the file holds exactly the rows the filter keeps. A dry run on the file's first 4 MB matched a NumPy filter bit for bit.

**Exit (2026-10-27): met.** A summary of one field of a wide dataset reads only that field and its keys (the temperature summary of the 31.8 GB NetCDF file in 158.1 s against 1,030.0 s, the same bits), and a result larger than memory is written at bounded memory (28.8 GB at a 1.01 GB peak) and reads back the same.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

Decided (2026-10-27): all three as recommended.

1. **How to find what a frame reads.** Recorded while the verbs run on the empty block, or found from the expressions without running them. Recommendation: recorded; the verbs resolve names already (field labels, coordinates, formulas, selectors), so a record cannot disagree with them, where a separate analysis could.
2. **Output formats.** `.npy` and Zarr first, NetCDF4 later (appending along a dimension needs the `netCDF4` library itself, which xarray's `to_netcdf` does not do), or all three now. Recommendation: `.npy` and Zarr; both append natively, and NetCDF4 can follow once its writer is chosen.
3. **What `write` gives.** Nothing but the path, or the written data opened as a frame read in blocks, so a pipeline can go on from it (`frame >> filter(...) >> write("near.zarr") >> group_by(...)`). Recommendation: the frame; reading what was written is the common next step, and nothing is read until a result is asked for.

### Milestone 13 — An operation registry, and the median on blocks

Planned (2026-10-27), as recommended after milestone 12 and accepted by the owner.
