The owner's design notes give each operation one registry entry keyed by its name: for a reduction, a partial step (one chunk to a small summary), a merge (two summaries to one), and a finalize (the summary to the answer), so a new reduction is one entry and the executor never changes. The block driver has the same three steps but hard-codes them: `_pieces` makes six kinds of pieces (flags, extremes, moments, float32 estimates, float sums, integer sums), `_Table.add` merges each kind, `_Table.settle` and `finish` finalize them, `_fill` and `_settled_extreme` know each kind's empty value, and `COMBINABLE` lists the names a block can answer. Adding one reduction means touching each. And the reductions a block cannot answer are the ones a summary of a large dataset most often wants after a mean: the median and quantiles, refused today with `median(...) cannot be combined across blocks`.

**The registry.** `tidy3a.reductions` holds one `Reduction` per name: `partial(values, codes, groups, node)` gives one block's pieces per group; `merge(held, piece, node)` combines two, in any order; `fill(dtype, node)` is a group's value before any block has it; `finish(held, counts, node)` gives the answer per group, and may ask for more passes (`passes`, below). The built-in nine become entries, written from today's code without a change in what they compute: the float32 sums keep their estimate and their exact second pass (`labelarray.sums`), the spreads their moments, the extremes their typed fills. `_Table` keeps the groups and the counts and asks the registry for the rest; `COMBINABLE` is the registry's names. Every block test gives the same bits before and after. Public registration (`tidy3a.register_reduction`) lets a library add one: an in-memory function on a NumPy array (which both backends call, as they call NumPy's for the built-ins) and the three block steps; it is a verb's identity like any other (milestone 11's structural hashing, by name and the entry's identity), and a reduction registered without block steps is refused on blocks, as the median is today.

**The median and quantiles on blocks, exactly.** A median is not a sum of pieces, but its rank is: the group's values counted per range of the value's bits find the range that holds the middle rank, and a second count within that range finds the value. Float32 values ordered as unsigned 32-bit keys (sign flipped, the rest inverted for negatives, as radix sorts order floats) take two passes of 16 bits: the first counts each group's values per top 16 bits and finds the bucket holding rank k (and k + 1 for an even count); the second counts that bucket's values per low 16 bits and finds the values, so the median is `np.median` of the one or two values, the same bits as in memory. Float64, int64, and times take four passes of 16 bits; NaN follows `na_rm` as in memory (a group with one is NaN unless `na_rm=True`, which leaves it out of the count). Counts are kept per (group, bucket) that occurs, not per group times 65,536, so many small groups cost what their values do. `quantile(x, q)` is the same with rank `q × (n − 1)` and NumPy's linear rule between the two values; it is new in memory too, with `np.quantile`'s default method. The cost is passes, not memory: a float32 median reads the data three times (the summary's own pass and two more), which `scan` shares with the frame's other results, and the extra passes read only the variables the median needs (milestone 12).

Decided (2026-10-27) by the plan's owner: all three decisions below as recommended.

Step 1 status (2026-10-27): done. `tidy3a.reductions` holds the nine built-ins as entries of a `Reduction` class: `Count` (`n()`, the table's own count, no pieces), `Total` (`sum`, `mean`: float32 estimates with their exact second pass, float64 and integer sums), `Extreme` (`min`, `max`, with their typed fills and empty groups), `Spread` (`std`, `var`, moments merged by Chan, Golub, and LeVeque's update), and `Flag` (`any`, `all`). Each holds the code that did its job in `chunked.py`, moved, not rewritten; `_Table.add`, `complete`, `settle`, and `finish`, `_pieces`, and the exact pass look entries up by name and know none of them, and `COMBINABLE` is the registry's names. Writing the registry's test found a bug every release with blocks had: `any()` and `all()` of missing values (fixed in `8fbf23b`, before the refactor, and ported into `Flag`). Tests (`tests/test_reductions.py`, both backends): the registry is what blocks can answer; every registered reduction (`sum`, `mean`, `min`, `max`, `std`, `var`, `any`, `all`) over float32, float64, and int32 values with missing ones and float32 ties, with and without `na_rm`, in blocks of 1, 7, and 333 positions, against the same summary in memory: the same bits where blocks promise them, within one rounding (float32) or 1e-12 (float64) where they add in float64. Every block test unchanged.

Step 2 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. A reduction's `finish` returns its column and a list of asks (`tidy3a.reductions.Ask`: its own `state`, and the `size` of one block's answer); `_passes` replaces the float32 exact pass's own code: it packs every frame's asks, whole and in order, into rounds whose answers hold at most `pass_budget()` values per block (`labelarray.sums.EXACT_PAIRS`, as before), reads the blocks once a round, has each block answer each ask (`again`), joins the answers across blocks in any order (`join`, adding by default), and lets each reduction `resolve` its ask into its column, with any further asks, which join the next round, until none is left; `close` runs after. The float32 sums are its first user: `Total.finish` asks for the unsettled groups in batches of `EXACT_PAIRS`, `again` is `chunk_exact`, `resolve` writes `exact_results` into the groups' places, so its passes and their counts are as before (the existing tests that count reads pass unchanged), and `chunked.py` no longer imports `labelarray.sums`. Tests: the registry and block tests unchanged; a test-only `max` that asks for two more passes, the second asked by the first's `resolve`, from two frames in one scan: with a large budget both frames' asks share each round (2 rounds, 4 blocks read each), with a budget of one ask a round 4 rounds, the resolves in order, and the max unchanged.

Step 3 status, the median (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `tidy3a.reductions.Median`: the first pass counts each group's values present and missing (`rank:<dtype>` pieces, added); `finish` leaves missing the groups with none present and, without `na_rm`, those with a missing value, as `np.median` does, and asks for the rest one target per group for an odd count and two for an even one (ranks `(n - 1) // 2` and `n // 2`); each pass counts every target's candidates, the group's values present whose order-preserving key starts with the target's prefix, by the next 16 bits of the key, as sparse `(target × 2**16 + digit, count)` pairs that `join` adds across blocks, and `resolve` takes the digit where the counts pass the rank, until every bit is decided. Keys: floats with the sign bit flipped and negatives inverted, signed integers and durations with the sign bit flipped, integers under 32 bits as 32, so float32 and 32-bit integers take two passes after the first, float64, int64, and durations four, float16 one. The one or two middle values then go through `np.median` per group (stacked), so the mean of an even count's two middle values, an integer median's float64, and a duration's truncation are NumPy's own; an odd count's value is taken whole. Every built-in reduction now runs on blocks, so the `cannot be combined` refusal has no built-in case until a reduction registered without block steps (step 4). Measured against memory on 10 types (float16 to float64, int8 to uint64, durations), with and without `na_rm`, grouped, by `bins()` with an empty bin, and whole, in blocks of 1, 97, and 3,000 positions: no difference in any bit. One exception, documented and pinned by a test: where both zeros, -0.0 and 0.0, lie in the middle, the block median orders -0.0 first (IEEE's total order) and NumPy keeps whichever its partition leaves, so the value is the same and its sign may differ. Tests (`tests/test_blocks.py`, `tests/test_reductions.py`, both backends): float32, float64, int16, int64, and durations, with and without `na_rm`, odd and even counts, ties, an empty bin, and a group of one, the same bits as in memory, at 13 blocks × (1 + 2) or (1 + 4) block reads, each read holding only the median's variable and its key; two frames' medians sharing their passes (4 blocks × 3 reads); the registry test with the median, exact for every type; the zeros' sign. Next, `quantile(x, q)`: new in memory as well, which the NumPy backend's labelarray does not compute yet, so it needs a labelarray release (0.1.5).

Step 3 status, the quantile (2026-10-27): done, staged for review, with labelarray 0.1.5 prepared and not published. In labelarray, `Array.reduce` and `grouped.reduce` take `q`, and `_reduce_data` gives `np.quantile` or, with `na_rm`, `np.nanquantile`, in NumPy's type (float32 stays float32, integers give float64, durations durations), and the missing value of that type for no positions, where NumPy raises; `q` outside 0 to 1 is a `ValueError`. In tidy3a, `quantile(x, q, over=, na_rm=)` is a reduction with `q` a field of `Reduce` (so of its structure); the backend protocol's `reduce` and `group_reduce` take `q`; the NumPy backend passes it to labelarray, and the xarray backend computes a quantile through labelarray's `Array` too, since xarray's own `quantile` gives float64 for float32; text, times, and booleans are refused by name. On blocks, `Quantile` is the median's passes with NumPy's rank rule: the values at `floor(h)` and the next (the last at the end), `h = q × (n − 1)` as NumPy computes it, then `np.quantile` of the two at `h − floor(h)`, grouped by that place, so each group's answer is NumPy's from all its values, its quirks included (an infinity beside the rank gives NaN). Measured against memory on six types (float32, float64, int16, int64, uint64, durations) with ties, infinities, and missing values, q of 0, 0.1, 0.5, 0.73, and 1, with and without `na_rm`, grouped, by `bins()` with an empty bin, and whole, in blocks of 1 and 333 positions: no difference in any bit. tidy3a requires labelarray 0.1.5, for the NumPy backend's quantile. Tests: in labelarray, four types, five q, `na_rm`, whole and grouped with an empty bin, against NumPy, and q refused outside 0 to 1; in tidy3a, the quantile on both backends against NumPy, q and the kinds of value refused, its text, the quantile on blocks at five q with the passes counted (7 blocks × 3 reads), and the registry test with it. Found by the check script's minimum-dependency job (2026-10-27): NumPy 2.0 to 2.3 convert a Python-float `q` to the type of float data, so a float32 quantile's virtual index `(n − 1) × q` is computed in float32, and its weight is float32; NumPy 2.4 keeps `q` a Python float and computes both in float64. The in-memory answers differ in their last bits between those versions, and the block quantile, written against 2.4, differed from NumPy 2.0's at q = 0.73 and with `na_rm`. It now computes the index in the type the installed NumPy does (`_index_type`, from `np.lib.NumpyVersion`) and gives NumPy the weight in that type, so its bits follow the version it runs on; the quantile and median tests pass on NumPy 2.0 and 2.5.

Step 4 status (2026-10-27): done, staged for review. `tidy3a.register_reduction(name, eager, *, partial=, merge=, finish=, fill=)`, marked experimental in its docstring, adds a reduction and gives the function that writes it. In memory, `eager(values, axes, na_rm)` reduces a NumPy array over the numbered axes on either backend; tidy3a takes the result's axes and labels from a built-in `count` over the same data (`backend.with_values`), so it looks as a built-in's would, and runs the function per group when grouped. On blocks, the three steps become a registry entry (`_Registered`): `partial(values, codes, groups, na_rm)` gives pieces with the groups along the last axis, `merge` combines two, `finish(held, counts)` gives the answers, and `fill` may be an array that broadcasts to one group's pieces, so a piece of several rows can fill each row its own way. Without block steps, data read in blocks refuses the reduction when `summarise` is called, before its function runs on the empty block (`_refuse_unblocked`). A built-in name, a name that is not an identifier, and some block steps without the others are refused; a function whose result has the wrong shape is refused naming both shapes. The function is also called with no values (an empty group, and the empty block a frame read in blocks checks `summarise` on), which the docstring says, since `np.max` and `np.percentile` of nothing raise. `COMBINABLE` is gone: the registry itself says what blocks can answer, and the refusal lists it. Tests (`tests/test_reductions.py`, both backends): a geometric mean through sums of logs, in memory against NumPy per group, bit for bit, with its text; on blocks of 1, 97, and 3,000 positions, grouped, by bins with an empty bin, and whole, within 1e-12 of memory; a range whose two rows fill with +inf and -inf, the same bits; a reduction without block steps answering in memory and refused on blocks; the refusals; two frames with a registered reduction sharing one pass.

Step 5 status (2026-10-27): done. The CHANGELOG's Unreleased section has `quantile`, the median and quantiles on blocks with their passes and the zeros' sign, `register_reduction` with its empty-input rule, and the registry inside. The README's larger-than-memory section lists `median` and `quantile` among what a block summarises, says how the passes work and what they cost, no longer lists `median` as refused, adds `median(distance)` to its runnable example, and ends with a runnable `register_reduction` example, a range, in memory and in blocks, with the empty-input case handled; the API table lists `quantile` and `register_reduction`. The README tests run both, and keep the reduction registries as they found them (a fixture restores them in place after each example), since the example's registration is global and the registry test, run after, found it.

Step 6 status (2026-10-27): done. On the 31.8 GB NetCDF file of milestone 11's exit (25.8 GB of memory), weeks on four threads, the daytime `filter`, Celsius, `group_by(month)`, then `n()`, `median`, and `quantile(…, 0.9)` in one summary: **471.9 s at a peak of 1.23 GB**, 315 block reads, three passes of 105 blocks (the summary's, then two of 16 bits, the median's and the quantile's asks sharing each round), every read holding only temperature, hour, and month (with `celsius`, the name `mutate` makes, which the source has none of). About 157 s a pass, as one read of those three fields from the drive took in milestone 12 (158 s); this run does not separate the drive from the counting in the later passes, whose 4.5 GB fit in the page cache. Against the in-memory reference, month by month on only those three variables (127.5 s at 2.30 GB, partly from the cache the blocks left): the months, the counts, every median, and every 90th percentile the same bits.

**Exit (2026-10-27): met.** The median and a quantile of a field per group over a file larger than memory, the same bits as in memory, at a peak of 1.23 GB; and reductions registered by the tests (a geometric mean, a range) running on blocks. The milestone needs labelarray 0.1.5 (`quantile`), published the same day.

Steps: 1. the registry: `tidy3a.reductions`, the nine built-ins as entries, `_Table` and `_pieces` asking it, every block test the same bits, and a test that an entry's three steps give the in-memory result for random splits into blocks. 2. Passes in the driver: a reduction's `finish` may ask for another pass over the blocks with what the last one found (the float32 sums' exact pass becomes the first user of this, so there is one mechanism); the scan shares each pass among its frames, as it does the exact pass now. 3. The median on blocks, float32 and float64, integers and times, with and without `na_rm`, odd and even counts, ties, and empty groups, against `np.median` per group, bit for bit; then `quantile(x, q)` in memory and on blocks. 4. `register_reduction`, tested with a reduction defined in the test (a range, `max - min`, and a geometric mean through sums of logs), in memory and on blocks, with its identity in a scan. 5. README and CHANGELOG. 6. Measured on the 31.8 GB NetCDF file: the median temperature per month, three passes, against the in-memory reference month by month. Exit: the median and a quantile of a field per group over a file larger than memory, the same bits as in memory, at memory bounded by the blocks, and a reduction registered by a test running on blocks.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

1. **The median on blocks: exact passes, a sketch, or values held.** Exact passes cost two more reads for float32 (four for float64) and give the same bits as in memory; a sketch (t-digest, KLL) costs no extra read and gives an approximation within a stated error; holding each group's values works only when they fit. Recommendation: exact passes; every other result on blocks is exact, and `scan` shares the passes with the frame's other results.
2. **Public registration now, or the registry inside tidy3a first.** Recommendation: both in this milestone, the public one marked experimental in its docstring for a release; a registry no one outside can use does not test its contract.
3. **`quantile` in the same milestone.** Its rank rule is the only difference from the median, and it is new in memory too. Recommendation: yes, with NumPy's default (linear) rule only; other methods when asked for.

### Milestone 14 — An automatic block size

Planned (2026-10-27), as recommended after milestone 13 and accepted by the owner. Left for later by milestones 11 and 12, and an open question in the owner's design notes ("automatic chunk size"). Today `blocks=` is a number of positions (or `"chunks"`) and `threads=` how many blocks run at once, and the memory a run takes is the block's size times the blocks in flight (`_in_order` starts at most `threads` + 1 ahead of the one being merged) times what the verbs make of a block. The caller has to guess the first two from the third. The drive runs measured it: on the 29.4 GB sweeps file (about 20 bytes a position), 500,000 positions on four threads peaked at 0.65 GB, 2,000,000 on four at 1.85 GB, and 2,000,000 on one at 1.18 GB; on the 31.8 GB NetCDF file, weeks (168 time steps) on four threads peaked at 2.07 GB with every field read, and at 0.55 GB with only the temperature summary's (milestone 12). A block in flight costs a few times what it reads, beyond a base of about 0.25 GB, and what it reads depends on the variables the run needs, nearly fourfold on the NetCDF file.

**The interface.** `read_array(path, ..., blocks="auto", memory="2GB")` and `tidy(data, blocks={"time": "auto"}, memory=...)`: the number of positions per block is chosen so that the blocks in flight, at their estimated cost, fit the budget. `repr` and `explain()` show the size chosen and the budget. `threads=` stays the caller's; the size is chosen for it.

Steps: 1. The estimate: a block's cost per position from the types and shapes of the variables a run reads, and what its steps add (new and replaced columns, a filter's copies, group codes, a summary's working arrays), all known from the run on the block of no positions (decision 4), so nothing is read; the number of positions is the budget, less a base, divided by the blocks in flight times that cost. Tests compare the estimate with the peak of a block's run measured by `tracemalloc` over the block tests' pipelines. 2. `blocks="auto"` and `memory=` (bytes, or text such as `"2GB"`), the default budget, the size fixed when a result is asked for and shown by `repr` before that, and a refusal naming the sizes when one position does not fit the budget. 3. Storage chunks: for NetCDF, Zarr, or Dask data chunked along the axis, the size rounded down to a whole number of chunks, so no chunk is read twice. 4. A scan sizes its blocks from all its frames together, and every pass reads the same blocks. The summary table's groups and the passes' counts are not counted, since the data decides them; the documentation says so. 5. README and CHANGELOG. 6. Measured on the 29.4 GB sweeps file and the 31.8 GB NetCDF file, with budgets of 1 GB and 4 GB on 1, 4, and 8 threads. Exit: on both files the peak under the budget, the time within 10% of the best size chosen by hand that stays under the same budget, and the same bits as a run given the same size by hand.

Step 1 status (2026-10-27): done. `tidy3a.sizing.cost(frames)` gives a `Cost`: bytes per position of a block running (`work`) and of a finished block's result waiting to be merged, joined, or written (`held`), and bytes per block whatever its size (`fixed`); `Cost.rows(budget, threads)` is the most positions that fit, from `RESERVE` (256 MB for the interpreter, the libraries, and the summary table), `threads` blocks running, and `threads` + 2 results waiting. The estimate is read off the block of no positions, so nothing is read and the same frames give the same cost: what a block loads (the variables a dataset's block computes, or every one, and the axis's labels); what each verb adds, the arrays it does not share with its input (a mutate's own, a filter's copies, the labels with them), its temporaries taken to be as large, along the walk `scan` takes; and a summary's keys, the values its reductions compute or copy, the rows' codes repeated per value, and each reduction's own steps, which each registry entry now declares (`Reduction.working`, a `Working`: bytes per value in the first pass and in a later one, and per block). Measured with `tracemalloc`, rounded up: a float32 sum or mean 32 bytes a value in its first pass and 80 in its exact pass, with labelarray's bins and the exact pass's buckets at most 40 MB a block; a median or quantile 16 and 128; the extremes 24, the spreads 32, the flags 16; a registered reduction's guessed at 64. It is a bound: a filter is taken to keep every row, and later passes to be asked for. What grows with a block's groups is left to the data, as the summary table is: labelarray's float32 estimate takes about 3.5 KB a group below its 2**22 bins, so counting a group per position would size blocks for data with a group per row, whose table is as large as the data anyway. Tests (`tests/test_sizing.py`, both backends): each reduction's first and later passes allocate no more than they declare, over its types, with 10 and 1,000 groups; a block's peak, every pass included, grows per position by no more than the estimate, nor by less than an eighth of it, for a point cloud read from a `.npy` file (voxels, rings, rows kept) and a gridded dataset (the daytime summary, the median and a quantile, sums with a flag and a variance, two keys, rows kept) — the estimate is 2.2 to 5.2 times the measured growth; nothing is read; frames scanned together cost at least the most of each alone and at most their sum; and a budget gives the most positions that fit. The suite: 3,206 passed.

Step 2 status (2026-10-27): done. `read_array(..., blocks="auto", memory=...)` and `tidy(data, blocks={axis: "auto"}, memory=...)`: `memory=` is bytes or text (`"2GB"`, `"512MiB"`; GB is 10**9 bytes, GiB 2**30), a quarter of physical memory by default (`os.sysconf`, or Windows' `GlobalMemoryStatusEx`; a system that reports neither asks for `memory=`), and goes with `"auto"` only. `Blocks` keeps `"auto"` and the budget; `Blocks.sized(frames)` gives the blocks for frames that run together, the estimate's most positions (at most the data's size), and refuses a budget that fits no position, with the sizes: a position's bytes as the verbs run on the threads, what is set aside, and what to change. A frame chooses its own size once (`ChunkedFrame._layout`), when it is opened and after each verb, so a verb that leaves no block within the budget is refused when it is called, as other checks are; `repr` and `explain()` show it ("8 blocks of 39,432 positions on point, sized for memory=320 MB"), `preview()` numbers blocks by it, and a run of one frame reads it. A scan sizes its blocks for all its frames together (step 4 tests it). `write()` reads its file back with `"auto"` and the same budget. Tests (`tests/test_sizing.py`): the size is the estimate's and shown; a size chosen gives the same bits as the same size given, for float64 means and spreads; the size follows the verbs (a summary of one field gets larger blocks than rows of every field) and the threads; nothing is read to choose it; `memory=`'s forms, its default, and its refusals; `memory=` without `"auto"` refused; a budget too small refused when the data is opened and when a verb is called; a written file read back with `"auto"`. The suite: 3,222 passed.

Step 3 status (2026-10-27): done. `Source.stored(names)` gives the storage's chunk boundaries along the axis for the variables a run reads: a dataset's Dask chunks, or else the chunks a file stores them in (xarray's `preferred_chunks`, which its NetCDF4 and Zarr backends set), every variable's whole at once where their sizes differ (their least common multiple, or the largest where that exceeds the data). With `"auto"`, `Blocks.sized` makes the blocks of whole consecutive chunks, as many as fit the size chosen (`_whole`), so regular chunks give a multiple of the chunk and irregular ones (a file a month) are grouped as they come. A file's chunk larger than the size is read in parts of it; a Dask chunk larger is refused, since Dask computes a chunk whole for any of its positions, which the budget would not hold, with the rechunking that fits. `repr` says which ("whole chunks of its storage", or "its storage chunks split to fit"). A size given, and data in memory, are not rounded. Tests (`tests/test_sizing.py`, with Zarr standing in for NetCDF4, whose library the test environment lacks): blocks of whole chunks of a Zarr store opened lazily and with Dask, the in-memory result; irregular monthly Dask chunks grouped whole; a Dask chunk too large refused; a file's chunk too large read in parts; variables in chunks of 30 and 20 read in blocks of 60s; a size given and data in memory left as they are. The suite: 3,236 passed.

Step 4 status (2026-10-27): done. A scan of several frames sizes its blocks for all of them together, the frames it writes included (`Blocks.sized(every)` in `_scan_once`, step 2), with the estimate's walk through their shared prefix (step 1), and every pass after the first reads those same blocks. Their size may be smaller than a frame's own, so a frame's float64 sums and means, integer means, and spreads in a scan may differ from its own run's in their last bits, as between any two sizes, and are the same bits as a run given the scan's size by hand; every other result is the same bits as its own run. `scan`'s docstring says so, and `read_array`'s what the budget leaves to the data: the groups a summary finds, and the rows a pipeline without `summarise` or `write()` keeps in memory. Tests (`tests/test_sizing.py`): a scan of three frames (voxels, a median and a quantile with their passes, rows kept) reads the blocks sized for all three, no larger than any one's own, in every pass, and gives each frame's own float32 means, medians, quantiles, counts, and rows; a scan's float64 mean and spread are those of its size given by hand; a frame a scan writes makes its blocks smaller, and the file holds its rows. The suite: 3,242 passed.

Step 5 status (2026-10-27): done. The README's "Files larger than memory" has a paragraph on `blocks="auto"` and `memory=`: the default budget, the estimate that reads nothing, the size chosen for the verbs that run and shown by `repr` and `explain()`, whole storage chunks, a scan's size and its float64 last bits, what the bound assumes and leaves to the data, and the refusal; the API table names it. The CHANGELOG's Unreleased section has the feature and the estimate inside. The README's examples run (9 passed); the suite: 3,242 passed.

Step 6 status (2026-10-28): done. Measured on the USB drive (24 GB machine), each run its own process: the voxel summary of the 29.4 GB sweeps file (`.bin`, read with `read_array`) and the daytime temperature summary of the 31.8 GB NetCDF4 file (`tidy` of `xr.open_dataset`, chunks of 24 time steps), runs of the two alternated so each sweeps pass, larger than memory, cleared the NetCDF data from the page cache. Peaks are `ru_maxrss`.

Measuring found three things, fixed before the runs recorded here, each with its tests: (1) the estimate replayed the verbs on a dataset's empty block of every variable rather than of those read, so a filter was taken to copy all seven fields (`Source.empty(names)` now gives the block as `block` loads it); (2) a later pass (the median's digits, the float32 sums' exact pass) took 80 to 128 bytes a value of the whole block, so the bound charged every block for the worst case; it now works on slices of `SLICE` = 65,536 values, rows whole, their answers joined as blocks' are (the registry's contract), so it costs a fixed amount per block running, and a test with slices of 7 values gives the in-memory bits for the median, a quantile, and float32 sums whose exact pass runs; (3) on the sweeps file with one thread, "auto" chose blocks of 2.04M positions and the run peaked at 1.24 GB against a 1 GB budget (0.90 GB on a rerun): one block alone peaked at 0.40 GB against its bound of 0.99 GB, and what `tracemalloc` saw stayed at 0.29 GB over 60 blocks while the process's footprint rose from 0.46 to 1.23 GB, so the allocator keeps some of what blocks of changing sizes free; the bound now counts a block's arrays at 1.5 times (`sizing.ALLOCATOR`). With these, the climate summary's estimate went from 9.85 MB to 3.6 MB a time step.

Block size made no measurable difference to time: the drive sets it (blocks of 130k to 2.6M positions, 12 to 240 steps; cached, 70 blocks of 24 steps took 1.13 s against 10 of 168 at 1.03 s). **Exit (2026-10-28): met.** On both files the peak under the budget, with the margin the one run that went over (the sweeps file at 1 GB on one thread) at 0.81 GB; the time within 1% of the best size chosen by hand under the same budget on four threads (the NetCDF file's against milestone 12's 158.1 s for 168 steps, since this session's ran cached), and level with it on one and eight; the same bits as runs given sizes by hand. The "auto" runs other than the 1-thread rerun were measured before the margin, which only makes their blocks smaller and their peaks lower, and, as measured, leaves their time alone.

Decided (2026-10-27) by the plan's owner: all four decisions below as recommended.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

1. **Estimated from the types, or measured on the first block.** Milestone 11's plan had rows chosen from the first block's measured cost per row. A measurement follows the real cost, but the size would differ between machines and runs, and with it the float64 and integer sums and the spreads (repeatable for a given size, not bit-identical across sizes), the block numbers in errors and in `preview()`, and the count of blocks, which could not be shown before a read. Recommendation: estimated from the types, so the same data, verbs, budget, and threads give the same blocks every time; measurement checks the estimate in the tests and on the drive.
2. **When the size is fixed.** At open, from every variable, which is cautious when a run reads fewer (on the NetCDF summary, blocks about four times smaller than needed); or when a result is asked for, from what the run reads. Recommendation: when a result is asked for; `repr` shows, before that, the size a run would use.
3. **The default budget.** `memory=` required with `"auto"`, or a share of physical memory by default. Recommendation: a quarter of physical memory (6 GB on the 24 GB machine of the measurements), shown by `repr`, set with `memory=`.
4. **Threads chosen too.** Milestone 11's plan had rows and threads chosen. The only evidence is on the drive, where four threads saved 20% (946 to 759 s). Recommendation: `threads=` stays the caller's and the size is chosen for it; step 6 measures 1, 4, and 8 threads before threads are chosen automatically.

### Milestone 15 — Blocks that scale with cores

Planned (2026-10-28), after the owner asked why a run in blocks does not get faster with more threads, as PyTorch's and Dask's do. Measured on a 12-core machine (AMD Ryzen 9 9900X, 24 threads, 64 MB of L3 cache) with the data in memory, so no drive set the pace: the voxel summary of 40M points took 6.15 s on one thread and at best 5.56 s on eight (1.1x); the daytime summary of 2,000 grids 0.75 s and 0.42 s on four (1.8x), no better beyond. Free-threaded Python 3.14t gave 1.1x and 2.1x, so the GIL is a small part. Two things are most of it:

1. **The merge is serial, and each costs what the table holds.** Each block's summary is merged into the table on the main thread, in order (`_Table.take` and `add`): `np.union1d` sorts every key of the table with the block's, a block with new groups copies every reduction's array wider, and a key whose groups move renumbers every key. With 540,000 voxels, merging took 4.37 s of the 6.11 s run (72%), and 35.7 s of 37.9 s with blocks of 65,536 points; the blocks' own work, which threads share, was 1.61 s, so no number of threads or processes could make that run more than about 1.4 times faster. Four processes on 10M points each took as long as one on 40M.
2. **A gridded summary is bounded by memory bandwidth.** Each NumPy step streams the block through main memory (the filter's copy, the mutate's column, the group codes repeated for each of a position's 64,800 cells, the float64 conversions), with little arithmetic per byte: the same operations in eight processes slowed as much as in eight threads (`x*x + x` 8.4x against 8.1x, `bincount` 4.3x against 4.4x).

How others avoid both: Dask and xarray run NumPy in threads, which release the GIL, and combine partial results in a tree of tasks, not one by one; PyTorch's arithmetic runs in compiled kernels on its own thread pool, several steps a pass over memory; Mojo's `parallelize` hands numbered pieces of work to its runtime's threads with no GIL, oversubscribed about 16 pieces a core, and `vectorize` runs each in SIMD.

Steps, as revised (2026-10-28, below): 1. `_Table.combine(other)`: two tables as one, their groups, counts, and pieces merged by the registry's merges, which hold in any order; the float32 sums' error bound already holds for totals added in any grouping. 2. A fixed merge tree in the workers: each block's summary a table of its own, pairs merged by block number as soon as both are ready, in the worker threads; the tree's shape depends on the block numbers alone, so the result is the same on any number of threads. 3. Reductions of each position first: where every value of a position has one group (a grid per time step grouped by month), each reduction reduces the position's values first and takes one code per position, not the code repeated for each value; the float32 sums' estimate and exact pass in labelarray (0.1.6), the registry's other entries in tidy3a. 4. Compiled grouped reductions and table merges in labelarray's `fast` extra (Numba): one pass over a block for all of a summary's reductions of a value, without repeated codes or temporaries, over fixed pieces of the block each with its own accumulators, combined in a fixed order, so the bits do not follow the threads; two tables' sorted keys merged in one linear pass. 5. Compiled row-wise verbs in tidy3a: a pipeline's `filter` and `mutate` expressions and its keys' codes compiled into one kernel per structure (milestone 11's identity, cached on disk), run once over the block in parallel: arithmetic, comparisons, logic, `sqrt`, `exp`, `log`, the trigonometric functions, `abs`, `floor`, and `if_else`; anything else runs on NumPy as before. 6. A free-threaded Python 3.14t job in `scripts/check.sh` and CI. 7. README and CHANGELOG. 8. Measured on the 12-core machine after step 4 and after step 5: in memory, and on files generated on its SSD. Exit: with the `fast` extra on 12 threads, against today's code on one thread, the rings summary at least 4 times faster (2.2x at best after step 2), the daytime summary at least 4 times (1.8x), and the 540,000-voxel summary at least 2.5 times (1.6x); the same bits on any number of threads; with `blocks="auto"` the peak under the budget, the kernels declaring their working memory. The targets are estimates, which step 8 confirms or reports against.

Decided (2026-10-28) by the plan's owner: all five decisions below as recommended; on the revision, all six of its decisions (after them) as recommended.

Step 1 status (2026-10-28): done. `_Table.combine(other)` takes a table into another as if its blocks had been merged after the first's: each key's groups widened by the other's (`_Part.widen`), the first table's codes renumbered where its groups moved and the other's mapped into them (`_Part.codes`), and its counts and pieces added by `_Table.add`, which merges them with the reductions' own merges and now counts the blocks behind them (`blocks=`), which the float32 sums' error bound reads. The other table's arrays are copied, so no two tables share one. Tests (`tests/test_merge_tree.py`, both backends): every block's summary a table, combined in pairs by number, left to right, right to left, and in random trees, against the blocks merged in order, for keys of a value, of cells whose range grows from block to block (so the groups move), with missing values kept and dropped, two keys, and none; in blocks of 1, 97, and 1,000 positions; with every reduction (`n`, float32, float64, and integer sums and means, extremes, spreads, flags, a median, a quantile): the same keys, counts, labels, and blocks, the same bits for what adds exactly, float64 sums within rounding, and the same bits for the float32 sums their estimates settle; an empty table changes nothing. The suite: 3,284 passed.

Step 2 status (2026-10-28): done. Each block's summary becomes a table of its own in the worker that made it (`_scan_once`), and a frame's tables are merged as a binary counter (`_Tree`): two tables of 2**k blocks each become one of 2**(k+1) as soon as both are there, the merge handed to the pool (`_merged`), and the last merged from the right; the main thread waits only for the end. A merge waits only on work handed to the pool before it, which has begun by the time it begins, so the pool cannot deadlock however few its threads. Tests (`tests/test_merge_tree.py`): every key gives the same bits, float64 sums and spreads included, on 1, 2, 3, and 8 threads; a scan's frames each get their own tree; the 30 merges of a 31-block run all run off the main thread. The suite: 3,300 passed.

Measured on the 12-core machine, 40M points in memory, blocks of 1M: the 540,000-voxel summary went from 1.1x to 1.6x on eight threads (4.06 to 4.27 s against 6.55 s on one); the daytime summary stayed at 1.8x, its table being 12 groups; rings, 32 groups, 2.2x; 10 m voxels, about 900 groups, 1.8x. Two limits remain. Every block of that data already holds about 335,000 of the 540,000 voxels, so its 39 merges are each as large as the table, and side by side each slows from 77 to 409 ms. And the machine's memory gives about 57 GB/s at most, of which one thread copying arrays takes 46 GB/s (81%): NumPy's steps each stream whole arrays through memory with little arithmetic a byte, so two or three threads fill it, whatever else changes. Processes, free-threaded Python (at most a tenth more), blocks within the cache, and an allocator that keeps memory for reuse (a single thread 20% faster, the same scaling) each stayed near 2x. So the milestone was revised (2026-10-28) to bring in compiled kernels, which do several steps a value while it is in the cache, as PyTorch's and Mojo's do (steps 4 and 5 above), with the exit restated from these measurements.

Step 3 status (2026-10-28): done; labelarray's part is not released yet. Each registry entry has `partial_rows(node, values, codes, groups, each)`, its first pass over a block whose positions each hold `each` values with one code: the extremes reduce each position first (numbers and times; text, compared as objects, repeats the codes as before), the flags each position's flags and missing marks, the spreads each position's moments combined by Chan, Golub, and LeVeque's update, the median and quantile count each position's values present and missing, float64 and integer sums and means sum each position first, and the float32 sums hand labelarray the positions' values with one code each. A registered reduction repeats the codes (`Reduction.partial_rows`), its steps being the caller's; `sizing` counts the repeated codes for it alone (`by_position`). In labelarray, `chunk_estimate` given a code per position and several values to each sums each position first (`_by_position`: its total, magnitudes, and values present, +inf, and -inf), then the positions per group in chunks as `_estimate` does, its terms counting the position's additions and the group's, so `_certify`'s bound holds; the exact pass still repeats the codes, a slice at a time. tidy3a works with labelarray 0.1.5 too, repeating the codes inside its estimate, so it need not require 0.1.6. Tests: in tidy3a (`tests/test_positions_first.py`), every reduction with its types and `na_rm` over positions of six values, in blocks of 1 and 150, against the summary in memory (the same bits where blocks promise them), and each entry's first pass against the codes repeated (the same bits for extremes, flags, the median's counts, and integer sums; float64 sums and spreads within rounding); in labelarray, positions of five values with ties, cancellation, missing values, and infinities, in 1, 4, and 37 chunks: the correctly rounded sums and means, the tie's sum by the exact pass, and the counts of the codes repeated. The suites: tidy3a 3,550, labelarray 825.

Measured on the 12-core machine, the daytime summary of 2,000 grids of 180 x 360 in memory, in blocks of 48 time steps, best of three: 0.886 s on one thread before this step, 0.123 s after (7.2x), 0.054 s on four threads (16x), 0.071 s on twelve; the same bits.

Step 4 status (2026-10-28): done as the table merge, and the reduction kernels left out, by the owner's choice after the profile below. Profiled on the 12-core machine after step 3, one thread: of the 540,000-voxel summary's 6.67 s, merging tables took 2.8 s, 1.73 s of it `np.union1d`'s hashing and 0.77 s `searchsorted`; of the rings summary's 0.92 s, labelarray's float32 estimate took 0.10 s, and the rest was spread over the row-wise verbs and the keys. labelarray's `grouped.merge_sorted(first, second)` merges two sorted arrays of distinct keys in one pass, giving their union and where each one's keys stand in it: a compiled two-pointer merge with `labelarray[fast]`, else a stable sort, which merges the two runs it is given in one pass (on 500,000 and 400,000 keys, 2.6 ms and 6.8 ms against 100.5 ms); `_Table.add` uses it. The voxel summary then took 4.72 s on one thread and 2.92 s at best (four threads), 2.1 times faster than the 6.15 s on one thread before the milestone, its merging 0.82 s. With step 3 done, the float32 estimate is about a tenth of a run, so compiled reduction kernels have little left to win; the keys (0.66 s for the voxels' cells), the filter's copy (0.31 s), sorting, and conversions cost more, which step 5 is for.

Decision 4 changed (2026-10-28): Numba's parallel kernels cannot be entered from several Python threads at once with its default threading layer (which is why labelarray's estimate kernel is not used on blocks already), so the kernels run serially inside a block, releasing the GIL, and tidy3a's block threads run them side by side; each kernel then also gives the same bits on any number of threads, taking its values in order. A run of one large block is not parallel within it. labelarray must be released (0.1.6) before tidy3a's next push, which imports `merge_sorted`.

Step 5 status (2026-10-28): not built, replaced by milestone 16, by the owner's choice after a prototype on one block of 1M points of the rings summary, on the 12-core machine: tidy3a's run of the block took 25.4 ms; the same steps in NumPy by hand 15.5 ms; Numba for the expression and filter alone, as this step planned, 14.3 ms; one fused Numba pass computing the distance, the filter, the ring's code, and the count and sum per ring 6.9 ms (3.7x). Run side by side the fused pass gave 144 blocks a second on one thread, 533 on four, 1,056 on eight, and 1,464 on twelve (10.2x): it reads each value once and writes no array, so memory bandwidth no longer holds it. At that rate the 40M points take 27 ms on twelve threads. Compiling the expressions alone saves about a millisecond in 25; the gain needs the whole summary of a block in one pass, which is milestone 16. Step 6, the free-threaded Python job, moves to milestone 16 with it. Step 7: the CHANGELOG's Unreleased section and the README have the merge tree, the reductions of each position first, and the one-pass merge. Step 8: measured with each step, above.

**Exit (2026-10-28): met for gridded summaries, short for point clouds.** On the 12-core machine, against the code before the milestone on one thread: the daytime summary of 2,000 grids 16 times faster (0.886 s to 0.054 s on four threads; target 4); the 540,000-voxel summary of 40M points 2.1 times (6.15 s to 2.92 s on four threads; target 2.5); the rings summary 2.2 times (0.88 s to 0.39 s on eight; target 4). Every run gives the same bits on any number of threads. Milestone 16 takes up the point clouds, from the prototype above.

#### Decisions for the plan's owner

1. **How blocks are merged.** A fixed tree by block number: the same bits whatever the number of threads; float64 sums and means, integer means, and spreads differ in their last bits from 0.8.0's, being added in another grouping, and repeat for a given block size. A table per thread: a little faster, but those results would follow the number of threads. Merging in order, as now: no speedup for summaries of many groups. Recommendation: the fixed tree.
2. **Where reductions of each position first live.** In labelarray (0.1.6) or in tidy3a. Recommendation: labelarray, where the reductions and their exactness arguments are, and which is published on its own.
3. **Compiled kernels** (Numba's parallel loops, the way Mojo and PyTorch work): in this milestone or later. Recommendation: later, a milestone of its own, once steps 2 and 3 are measured.
4. **Free-threaded Python.** Recommendation: a 3.14t job in the checks now; NumPy supports it, and it catches what would break before anything relies on it.
5. **Where to measure.** Recommendation: the 12-core machine, in memory and on files generated on its SSD, where the CPU and not the drive sets the pace; copying the 30 GB files over the network would take longer than writing new ones.

#### Decisions on the revision (2026-10-28)

1. **The kernel tool.** Numba (labelarray's `fast` extra uses it already; it releases the GIL, runs parallel loops, and supports Python 3.10 to 3.15, free-threaded 3.14t included, from wheels), Cython or C (wheels to build per platform), Mojo (its Python interop is new, with reported trouble on NumPy arrays), or numexpr (expressions only). Recommendation: Numba.
2. **Where the kernels live.** Recommendation: grouped reductions and merges in labelarray, where the reductions and their exactness arguments are; fused expressions in tidy3a, where the expression trees are.
3. **Bits between the compiled and plain paths.** Within each, the same on any number of threads; between them, counts, integers, extremes, float32 sums, and medians are the same bits, while float64 sums and means, integer means, and spreads may differ in their last bits, added in other pieces. Recommendation: accept that and say so, rather than slow the NumPy path to match.
4. **Two kinds of threads.** `threads=` runs blocks side by side, which mostly overlaps reading with work; Numba's threads work inside a block. Recommendation: Numba gets the cores divided by `threads=`, so the default of one block at a time lets the kernels use every core and the two never oversubscribe the machine.
5. **What is not compiled.** Recommendation: the listed expressions compiled, the rest on NumPy, nothing refused; a pipeline's first run pays a second or two to compile, and later runs reuse the cached kernel.
6. **Order.** Recommendation: the reduction and merge kernels (step 4) before the expressions (step 5), measured after each.

### Milestone 16 — Fused summary kernels

Planned (2026-10-28), from milestone 15's prototype: on a block of 1M points of the rings summary, tidy3a's run took 25.4 ms, NumPy's steps by hand 15.5 ms, and one fused Numba pass (the distance, the filter, the ring's code, and the count and sum per ring) 6.9 ms, which ran 10.2 times faster on twelve threads than on one, reading each value once and writing no array. So: where a summary's every piece can be compiled, one pass a block does all of it, the row-wise verbs, the keys' codes, and every reduction's pieces, and the block's table joins the merge tree as now; the rest runs on NumPy as before, a block at a time if need be.

Steps: 1. The planner and the switch: `tidy3a.fused.plan_for(frame)`, whether a frame's summary compiles and, where not, which step and why; compilation on when Numba is installed (`tidy3a[fast]`) and not turned off (`TIDY3A_COMPILED=0`); importing tidy3a loads no Numba. 2. Expressions to source (`fused/expressions.py`): each step cast to the type NumPy gives it, known from the run on the block of no positions, so the bits are NumPy's; tests compare every compiled operation with NumPy's over types and special values, fused multiply-adds included. 3. Keys (`fused/keys.py`): dense codes for integer and boolean fields, for `cells`, `bins`, and `rounded`, as labelarray computes them, and for float fields whose values in a block are all whole within the range, checked by the kernel, the block falling back otherwise; several keys as a product of ranges. 4. Reductions (`fused/reductions.py`): accumulators giving the pieces each registry entry's first pass gives (`partial_rows`), the float32 sums' those of labelarray's `chunk_estimate`; the later passes stay as they are. 5. The driver: a compilable summary's blocks run its kernel, any block whose keys do not fit falling back; `repr` and `explain()` say what is planned and, after a run, what ran, blocks compiled and on NumPy and why; `sizing` counts a block's dense tables. 6. The cache (`fused/cache.py`): generated source and Numba's compiled kernels under `~/.cache/tidy3a/kernels` (`TIDY3A_KERNELS` to move it), each file named by a hash of the source and of the tidy3a, labelarray, Numba, and NumPy versions and the CPU, written atomically. 7. A free-threaded Python 3.14t job in `scripts/check.sh` and CI, moved from milestone 15. 8. README and CHANGELOG. 9. Measured on the 12-core machine, in memory and on files on its SSD. Exit: with `tidy3a[fast]` on 12 threads, against the code before milestone 15 on one thread, the rings summary at least 4 times faster and the 540,000-voxel summary at least 3 times; the same bits as the NumPy path for counts, integers, extremes, flags, float32 sums, and medians; the same bits on any number of threads; with `blocks="auto"` the peak under the budget; importing tidy3a loads no Numba.

Decided (2026-10-28) by the plan's owner, with four amendments of the proposal's (marked):

1. **Functions.** `+ − × ÷`, `sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, comparisons, logic, and `if_else` compile, NumPy's intermediate types and rounding kept; a step falls back where agreement with NumPy is not established. `exp`, `log`, and the trigonometric functions stay on NumPy at first. Amendment: `**` compiles for the exponents NumPy itself computes exactly (2 as `x*x`, 0.5 as `sqrt`, -1 as `1/x`, 1), so `\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}` compiles; any other exponent, and `//` and `%`, fall back.
2. **Keys.** Dense integer codes where the product of the keys' ranges is at most 16 million and the dense tables fit the memory budget; otherwise NumPy. Time and text keys stay on NumPy. Amendments: the limit counts bytes (the product of ranges times each group's pieces, which `sizing` counts against a block's share of the budget); and a float key compiles where every value of a block is whole and within the range, which the kernel checks, so a point cloud's `ring` stored as float32 compiles, the labels staying the float values; a block that fails falls back.
3. **Ownership.** tidy3a generates the whole fused kernel. Its float32 pieces follow labelarray's `chunk_estimate` contract; labelarray keeps the certification, the exact pass, and the final rounding. Tests compare the paths.
4. **Visibility.** `repr` gives a compact status; `explain()` names the steps that fall back and why. What was planned is told apart from what ran, mixtures of compiled and NumPy blocks included.
5. **Dependency.** Numba optional, through `tidy3a[fast]`; when installed, eligible blocks compile on their own. Importing tidy3a loads no Numba and compiles nothing. Amendment: `TIDY3A_COMPILED=0` turns compilation off, to compare the paths and to debug.
6. **Compile time.** Generated source and compiled kernels are cached on disk, under `~/.cache/tidy3a/kernels` by default, reused across runs, and invalidated when the code, the types, or the dependencies change (amendment: by the hash in each file's name, with atomic writes, `TIDY3A_KERNELS` to move the folder).

The compiler lives in `src/tidy3a/fused/`: `__init__.py` (`plan_for`), `expressions.py`, `keys.py`, `reductions.py`, and `cache.py`; the driver calls it where `_scan_once` makes each block's summary.

The blocks' backend (2026-10-28, by the owner's request, between steps 2 and 3): a dataset read in blocks chose its backend once, on the lazy data, so NetCDF4 and Zarr files ran their blocks on xarray, laziness and storage encodings keeping them there, though each block is computed into memory. Measured on the 12-core machine, the daytime summary of a 3.66 GB NetCDF4 file and of a 2.8 GB Zarr store of 2,000 grids of seven fields (180 x 360, chunks of 24 time steps), in blocks of 48, best of three from the cache: NetCDF4 0.236 s on xarray against 0.209 s on NumPy on one thread, 0.178 against 0.151 on four; Zarr 0.370 against 0.313 and 0.212 against 0.179; Zarr with Dask 0.471 against 0.424 and 0.315 against 0.275: NumPy 10 to 16% faster, the same bits in every run. `DatasetSource` now chooses the blocks' default backend on the block of no positions, in memory, so neither laziness nor storage encodings decide, while what else the NumPy backend cannot hold still keeps xarray; `set_backend` and `backend=` are followed. `write()` to Zarr kept the source's portable encodings from blocks on xarray, which NumPy blocks lack (a variable packed into 16-bit integers was written as float64, 4,045 bytes against 3,218), so the writer now takes the source file's portable encodings (`write.portable_encodings`) for the variables no `mutate` assigns to, each only where the written variable has the source's type, and none where a `mutate` writes into an array's fields; a variable the verbs change is written plain on both backends. Tests (`tests/test_block_backend.py`): lazy and Dask-backed Zarr and Dask-chunked data in memory run their blocks on the default backend; a coordinate beyond the axis's labels keeps xarray; a backend asked for is followed; both backends give a file's summary the same bits on one and three threads; a file written from NumPy blocks has the same values and portable encodings as from xarray blocks, a packed variable kept packed and a changed one plain, time units kept, a DataArray's too.

Step 1 status (2026-10-28): done. `tidy3a.fused.plan_for(frame)` gives a `Fusion`: no reasons where the summary compiles, else each step that does not and why, read off the verbs on the block of no positions (the variables a run reads, as `block` gives them), so every step's type is NumPy's and nothing is read. It walks `filter`'s predicates and `mutate`'s assignments in order, each seeing the ones before, a formula through what it lowers to, `select` passing; the keys (integer, boolean, and float fields, and their `cells`, `bins`, and `rounded`; times, durations, text, and other transforms on NumPy); and the reductions (the built-ins; a registered one on NumPy). What compiles: `+ - * /`, `**` with the exponents 2, 0.5, -1, and 1, comparisons, `& | ~`, `sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, and `if_else`, on booleans, integers, and float32 and float64; half-precision and complex values, `//`, `%`, other powers, and every other function on NumPy. Compiling is off when `TIDY3A_COMPILED=0` or Numba is not installed, which `importlib.util.find_spec` asks without loading it; a pipeline that keeps its rows is not planned for. `Fusion.words()` and `lines()` give the status and every reason; showing them in `repr` and `explain()` moved to step 5, so the plan is never shown as compiled before a kernel runs. Tests (`tests/test_fused_plan.py`): the rings and voxel summaries, every reduction, chained assignments, the functions and logic, a selection, bins and rounding, a grid, a boolean key, and no key compile; `exp`, a cube, `//`, `%`, time and text keys, complex and half-precision values, and kept rows are named with their reasons; a registered reduction runs on NumPy; each reason once; compiling off without Numba or when turned off; nothing read; and importing and planning in a fresh interpreter load no Numba. The suite: 3,598 passed.

Step 2 status (2026-10-28): done. `fused.expressions.Compiler` turns an expression into source for one value in a kernel's loop, at position `i`, or at value `j` of it for a grid's values (a step of both being per value); each step's operands are cast to the type NumPy computes it in and its result to the type NumPy gives it, both from the step run on the block of no positions, and a Python number takes the type NumPy 2 gives it beside an array (`float32 - 273.15` stays float32). Taken: `+ - * /`, comparisons, `&`, `|`, `~`, `-`, `+`, `sqrt`, `abs`, `floor` (an integer's own value, as NumPy 2.5 gives it), `if_else`, and `**` as NumPy computes it exactly: 2 as `x*x`, 0.5 as `sqrt`, -1 as `1/x`, 1 as `x`, and for an integer base only 2 and 1 (NumPy takes the C library's `pow` for the others, which need not be `sqrt` on every platform); a boolean sum or product is made boolean again, as NumPy's is a logical one. Refused (`Unsupported`, so NumPy runs it): every other function and operator, before its values are computed; a step whose type is half precision (which NumPy gives, for instance, the square root of an 8-bit integer, and Numba lacks), complex, a time, or text; a variable that does not vary along the blocked axis. The planner now asks the compiler for every expression, so what is planned is what compiles. `fused.kernels` builds a loop over a block's positions, and over a position's values where a step needs them, compiled by Numba, imported only then, with `nogil` (tidy3a's threads run kernels side by side), NumPy's error model (division by zero gives an infinity or a NaN), and no `fastmath`. Tests (`tests/test_fused_expressions.py`, both backends): for 11 pairs of types (float32 and float64 alone and mixed, integers of 8 to 64 bits signed and unsigned, booleans, booleans with float32), every expression NumPy takes for them, over every pair of special values (zeros of both signs, infinities, NaN, the smallest subnormal, the largest float, integer limits), compiled into one kernel, gives NumPy's type and bits, a NaN for a NaN; `x * y + z` among them, which would differ had the multiply and add been fused; a grid's values with its positions' (`t - 273.15`, the daytime test, their product, `if_else`, `sqrt(t) + h`) give NumPy's; a cube, `exp`, `//`, `%`, and an integer to the power 0.5 are refused. The suite: 3,632 passed.

Step 3 status (2026-10-28): done. `fused.keys.key_for(part, context, compiler)` gives a key's placement, or `Unsupported` and why (an unsigned 64-bit key, whose values may not fit the signed bucket numbers; a key that varies within a position; times; other transforms). `kernel_source` builds a pass over a block's positions placing each key's value, compiled by the source of `fused.expressions`, in a bucket numbered by an integer: the value itself for an integer or boolean key, or a float key whose value is whole; for `cells` and `rounded`, labelarray's `_Line` placement (a float64 guess corrected against the exact edges, which are integer multiples divided once in float64, or integer arithmetic for integer values); for `bins`, a binary search as `np.searchsorted(edges, value, side="right") - 1` gives it. It writes each position's bucket and whether its value is missing (NaN), and each key's lowest and highest bucket and count of missing values, and returns -1 at the first value where agreement with NumPy is not established: a float key's value that is not whole, a negative zero (which NumPy may label as either zero), an infinity, a value outside every bin, a step 2**50 or more from the origin, or an edge beyond 2**53, so the block falls back to NumPy, which gives its result or its error. `groups` turns the buckets into what `resolve_value_key` gives a block: the occupied buckets in order (counted over the range, or sorted where the range is far wider than the values, as labelarray's `_occupied` does), each position's code, the labels by labelarray's own `_labels` for the occupied buckets (every bin for `bins`), and the missing group last, dropped or kept as `dropna` says. The planner asks `key_for` for each key. Tests (`tests/test_fused_keys.py`, on one backend, since the placement sees plain arrays): `cells` of sizes 1, 0.1, 2.5 from -1, and 0.3 from 0.05, `rounded` of 0.5, 0.1, and 3 from 1, `bins` with edges at tenths, and cells of an expression, on float32 and float64 values on and beside the edges, halves among them, with missing values dropped and kept; value keys, `cells`, `rounded`, and `bins` of 8- to 64-bit integers; whole floats with missing values, booleans, and integers across 2**62: the same labels and their types, codes, and positions dropped as `resolve_value_key`; and the fallbacks above. The suite: 3,722 passed.

Step 4 status (2026-10-28): done. `fused.reductions.Slot` is one reduction in a kernel: arrays per group filled as the kernel reads each value of each position once, and `pieces` turns them into what the registry entry's `partial_rows` gives, so a block's table is built and merged as before. Float32 `sum` and `mean` give labelarray's estimate pieces, the total and the sum of magnitudes each added with Neumaier's compensation, within `(6u + 4(nu)^2)` of the sum of magnitudes, which is within the plain bound of 7 additions while a group holds at most 2**25 values, so the terms say 7 (the count beyond it), and labelarray's `settle` certifies with them, adding the blocks merged; the values present, counted, +inf, and -inf exactly. Float64 sums and means, and means of integers and booleans, add in position order, `np.bincount`'s; integer sums in int64; `min` and `max` as `minimum` and `maximum` (a NaN wins) or `fmin` and `fmax` with `na_rm` (a NaN passed over), -0.0 below +0.0 whichever comes first, as NumPy 2.5 orders them; `any` and `all` with their missing marks; `std` and `var` their moments, the squared deviations in a second pass, as NumPy's first pass takes them; `median` and `quantile` their counts. `summary_source` builds the kernel, which also counts each group's positions. Tests (`tests/test_fused_reductions.py`, on one backend): every reduction, over its types (float32, float64, integers of 8 to 64 bits, booleans), with and without `na_rm`, one value a position and six, over values with NaN, both infinities, both zeros, the largest float, a subnormal, and float32 ties: the same bits as `partial_rows` for extremes, flags, integer sums, and the median's counts; float64 sums the same bits for one value a position; spreads the same finished results, infinities in the same places (near float64's largest value the paths' means may overflow at different steps, and the squares overflow either way); float32 sums the same counts and, settled and with labelarray's exact pass where it must run, the same float32 results, the compensated bound leaving no more groups to it than NumPy's; and the zeros' and NaN's order in `min` and `max`. The suite: 3,763 passed.

Step 5 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. `fused.driver.build(frame)` compiles a summary the planner says compiles into two kernels, after replaying its verbs on the block of no positions; `_scan_once` runs them on each block it loads, and the frames that do not compile walk their verbs as before, over the same blocks. A block runs: `place`, one pass testing the filters and placing each key's value in a bucket, stopping at a value where agreement with NumPy is not established; each key's groups from its range, counted where the range is at most four times the block's positions (a compiled pass marking the buckets occupied), sorted otherwise; `dense`, a compiled pass giving each kept position its group among the dense combinations of the keys' groups, as `np.ravel_multi_index` numbers them, or none where it is dropped for a missing key; `accumulate`, one pass adding each value into its group's slots; and the occupied groups as the table `_partial` gives, which joins the merge tree. A block whose values do not agree, or whose keys' groups make more than 16 million combinations or more bytes of dense tables than `dense_limit` (128 MiB, or with a budget an eighth of a running block's share of it), runs on NumPy, its table the same; so does every block of a frame whose kernel Numba refuses (one of Numba's errors in `build`). Kernels are compiled once a process for each source (`fused.kernels.COMPILED`); the disk cache is step 6. `repr` of a summary read in blocks says "planned run: a compiled kernel a block", or "NumPy, as" its first reason, and `explain()` ends with every reason; a variable a `mutate` makes on NumPy is refused in the steps that read it (`Inherited`) without a reason of its own. Once a result is computed it notes how the blocks ran ("ran: 40 blocks in a compiled kernel", and any on NumPy, with why), as its last step's effect in `explain()`; a sample from `preview()` does not. `sizing` counts a compiled frame's dense-table limit among a block's fixed bytes. The tests that count the NumPy walk's steps or passes, or peaks of NumPy's steps, run with `TIDY3A_COMPILED=0`. Tests (`tests/test_fused_driver.py`, both backends): six summaries (the rings with every reduction, missing rings kept, a grid of cells and rounding, bins, no key, an `if_else` expression) on one thread and three, a grid of values a position by month, and two keys of which one is sorted, missing values dropped and kept: the same variables, types, labels, and bits as the NumPy run of the blocks; a block that does not agree, too many groups for dense tables, and a kernel Numba refuses, run on NumPy with their notes; a scan of a compiled frame, a NumPy one, and kept rows over the same blocks; `repr` and `explain()` planned and ran, a sample, kept rows, and compiling turned off; and the sizing of the dense tables. The suite: 3,771 passed.

Measured on the 12-core machine, data in memory, best of three after a warm-up run that compiles, against the NumPy run of the blocks on today's code (Python 3.14, Numba 0.68.1), every result the NumPy run's bits: the rings summary of 40M points in blocks of 1M 0.656 s compiled on one thread (NumPy 0.876 s), 0.175 s on four, 0.098 s on twelve (NumPy 0.419 s), 9.0 times the 0.88 s before milestone 15 (exit target 4); the 540,000-voxel summary 4.19 s on one thread (NumPy 4.34 s), 2.41 s at best on eight (NumPy 3.21 s on twelve), 2.55 times the 6.15 s (target 3); the daytime summary of 2,000 grids in blocks of 48 0.325 s on one thread (NumPy 0.129 s), 0.038 s on twelve (NumPy 0.069 s), 23 times the 0.886 s. The first build took 25 ms a block of the rings, of which the kernels 8.4 ms and NumPy's indexing between them the rest, so the keys' groups and dense codes moved into the compiled passes above (13.7 ms a block). The voxels fall short on what follows the first pass: of a run of 10M points on one thread (1.6 s), the compiled first pass took 0.37 s, merging the blocks' tables 0.54 s, and the exact pass of the float32 mean 0.98 s, on NumPy, since 21,470 of 533,985 voxels (4%) are not settled by the estimate, the same on both paths: most hold a few points, whose mean often lies exactly on a float32 tie, which the estimate's bound cannot decide although the float64 sum of a few float32 values is exact. A grid's values are added one at a time into their group's slots, which one thread does slower than NumPy's reductions of each position first. Left open: an exactness mark among labelarray's estimate pieces, or the exact pass run on the compiled codes, for the voxels; and accumulators held per position for a grid.

Step 6 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. `fused.cache.load` keeps each kernel as a module, `k<hash>.py`, under `~/.cache/tidy3a/kernels` (`TIDY3A_KERNELS` names another folder), whose functions Numba compiles with `cache=True`, so it keeps the machine code beside the module, one entry for each set of argument types, and a later run reads it instead of compiling. The hash, 32 hexadecimal digits of SHA-256, covers the module's source and the tidy3a, labelarray, Numba, llvmlite, NumPy, and Python versions and the CPU (its name and features, as LLVM reports them), so a change to any of them gives new files and the old are not read again. A module is written to a temporary file in the folder and moved into place, so no run reads half of one; Numba writes its own files the same way. Each module is registered under its own name (`tidy3a_kernel_<hash>`) before it runs, which a kept kernel that calls a helper needs, as Numba names the helper by its module. Where the folder cannot be made or written, or Numba warns that it cannot keep a kernel, the kernel is compiled in memory, as before; `kernels.compile_source` asks the cache first and keeps either in the process's `COMPILED`. The suite keeps its kernels in a temporary folder of its own, removed when it ends, so it never writes the user's cache. Nothing yet removes old kernels: the suite's fused tests leave 86 modules, 7.5 MB with Numba's files. Measured on the Mac: the rings summary on 20,000 points in a fresh interpreter took 0.65 s compiling its four kernels and 0.33 s reading them kept (four cache hits, no misses), most of the rest importing Numba; the fused tests (185) 13.1 s compiling and 2.1 s with their kernels kept. Tests (`tests/test_fused_cache.py`): a second interpreter reads the four kernels the first kept and compiles nothing, with the same result; the modules' names and the temporary files; a changed source, or a changed version, gives a new module; the default folder and `TIDY3A_KERNELS` with `~`; and a folder that cannot be made compiles in memory. The suite: 3,775 passed.

Step 7 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. `scripts/check.sh` and CI run a free-threaded Python 3.14 job (`3.14t`): it checks that importing tidy3a, Numba, xarray, Zarr, and the compiled driver leaves the GIL off, then runs the suite with it off (`-X gil=0`). Every other version is asked of uv as `<version>+gil`, since uv took an installed free-threaded build for "3.14" on the 12-core machine. Polars publishes no free-threaded wheels, and building it from source failed there (its linker died, and it would take minutes a run), so the extras split: `test` is `dev` without Polars, which the free-threaded job installs, and `dev` is `tidy3a[test]` and Polars; the tables' tests that need Polars skip. Two findings: (1) on x86, NumPy's `minimum` and `maximum` give, for a tie of -0.0 and +0.0, the operand they meet second, so the sign of a grouped extreme's zero follows the order, and the vectorized loops, in which values are compared; on ARM they order -0.0 below +0.0, as IEEE 754-2019 does, and so does the kernel. The zeros test compares the signs with NumPy's where NumPy orders them, and elsewhere the values, NaN, and the kernel's own order; the summaries' tests on random data, which have no tied zeros, give NumPy's bits on both. (2) The planner's tests assumed Numba, which the minimum-versions job lacks; planning needs none, so they plan as where it is installed, but those about its absence. Checked on the 12-core machine, every job side by side (293 s): Python 3.11 to 3.14, 3,809 passed each; free-threaded 3.14, 3,756 passed with the GIL off; minimum NumPy and xarray, 3,480 passed; distributions, ok. The suite on the Mac: 3,775 passed. Each job took about three minutes there, run side by side; CI's runners are slower, and its 10-minute limit is to be watched.

Step 8 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. The README's installation says what `fast` now compiles and the Python versions tested, free-threaded 3.14 among them; "Files larger than memory" has a paragraph on the compiled path: what compiles and what runs on NumPy, `repr`'s planned run, `explain()`'s reasons and the result's "ran" note, the blocks that fall back and why, the one difference from NumPy's bits (a tie of the zeros in `min` and `max` on x86), the kernel cache and `TIDY3A_KERNELS`, `TIDY3A_COMPILED=0`, and the measurements, the voxels' smaller gain and why; the API table's settings name the two variables; the development section lists the check's jobs. Prose only, since the README's examples also run without Numba (the minimum-versions job). The CHANGELOG's Unreleased section has the compiled summaries, the kernel cache, and the free-threaded job with the `test` extra. The README's examples run (9 passed).

Step 9 status (2026-10-28): measured; the exit is met for the rings summary and short for the voxels. On the 12-core machine (Python 3.14, Numba 0.68.1; the measurement script is kept on that machine, in `~/tidy3a-work/m16/final.py`), best of three after a warm-up run, every result compared with NumPy's run of the blocks on one thread. In memory: the rings summary of 40M points in blocks of 1M took 0.828 s on NumPy on one thread and 0.431 s on twelve, compiled 0.654 s on one, 0.174 s on four, 0.104 s on eight, and 0.097 s on twelve, 9.1 times the 0.88 s before milestone 15 (target 4); the 540,000-voxel summary 4.36 s and 3.20 s on NumPy, compiled 4.20 s on one and 2.46 s at best (eight), 2.5 times the 6.15 s (target 3); the daytime summary of 2,000 grids 0.094 s and 0.066 s on NumPy, compiled 0.328 s on one and 0.039 s on twelve, 23 times the 0.886 s. From files on the SSD, read from the page cache: the rings from a 40M-point `.npy` file 0.774 s and 0.437 s on NumPy, compiled 0.141 s on twelve; the voxels 4.54 s and 3.28 s, compiled 2.51 s at best; the daytime summary from the NetCDF4 file 0.210 s and 0.171 s on NumPy, compiled 0.133 s at best (four threads), and from the Zarr store 0.318 s and 0.178 s, compiled 0.146 s (eight), reading and decompressing the files setting the pace. On free-threaded Python 3.14 with the GIL off, in memory: rings 0.903 s and 0.425 s on NumPy, compiled 0.102 s on twelve; voxels 4.45 s and 3.31 s, compiled 2.67 s; daytime 0.098 s and 0.068 s, compiled 0.037 s; NumPy's steps scale no better without the GIL, as memory bandwidth, not the GIL, holds them. Every run, compiled or not, on any number of threads and either build, gave the same bits as NumPy's run on one thread. Importing tidy3a loads no Numba (`tests/test_fused_plan.py`). With `blocks="auto"` on twelve threads, from the `.npy` file, peak resident memory of the whole process: the rings at `memory="1GB"` 0.25 GB (942 blocks of 42,479 positions, 0.70 s) and at `"4GB"` 0.66 GB (48 blocks, 0.39 s), compiled; the voxels at `"1GB"` 1.23 GB, over the budget, and at `"4GB"` 1.81 GB, both on NumPy in every block, since their dense tables (1M combinations of about 64 bytes) pass the limit a budget gives a block (an eighth of its share: 7.6 MB and 38.9 MB here), and the voxels' groups, which the estimate leaves out, hold the rest. The result's note named each block's count of combinations as a reason of its own, which made a long list where blocks differed; a reason now names the limit only ("its keys' groups' dense tables would take more than 7.62 MB"), so blocks group by it. The suite: 3,775 passed.

**Exit (2026-10-28): met for the rings and grids, short for the voxels.** Against the code before milestone 15 on one thread: the rings summary 9.1 times faster (target 4), the voxels 2.5 times (target 3), the daytime summary 23 times; the same bits as the NumPy path everywhere measured, on any number of threads, with the GIL and without; importing loads no Numba. Short of the exit: the voxels, whose time after the compiled first pass is merging 540,000 groups a block and the float32 mean's exact pass on NumPy (4% of voxels hold a mean on a float32 tie that the estimate cannot settle); and `blocks="auto"`, under which the voxels' dense tables pass a block's limit, so every block runs on NumPy and the groups put the peak over a 1 GB budget. Left open for the owner: placing groups too many for dense tables by sorting the block's combinations (tables as large as the occupied groups, at most the positions) instead of falling back; an exactness mark in labelarray's estimate pieces, or the exact pass run on compiled codes; and accumulators per position for grids on one thread.

Sorted blocks (2026-10-28, by the owner's choice after step 9's exit): a block whose keys' groups make more combinations than dense tables hold (`DENSE_GROUPS`, or `dense_limit`'s bytes) no longer runs on NumPy. Its kept positions' combinations, numbered by the dense pass as before, are sorted (`np.unique`), each position takes its combination's rank, and the accumulating pass fills tables only as large as the combinations the block holds, at most its positions; the occupied ones are unravelled into each key's groups as before. Only combinations beyond 64-bit codes (`CODES`, 2**62) still run on NumPy, with that reason. The estimate's 336 bytes a position for the voxels already covers what a sorted block holds (about 160: the block, the keys' buckets and codes, the sort's copies, and 64 bytes of tables a group), so `sizing` is unchanged. Tests (`tests/test_fused_driver.py`): the rings with missing ones kept and the grid of cells and rounding, past either limit, on three threads, compiled with NumPy's bits; beyond the codes, on NumPy with its note. On the 12-core machine, the voxels with `blocks="auto"` on twelve threads now compile in every block: at `memory="1GB"` 6.42 s (9.85 s on NumPy), peak 1.31 GB, and at `"4GB"` 3.05 s (3.77 s), peak 2.07 GB; the rings unchanged. The 1 GB run stays over its budget, as the merged tables of over half a million voxels, which the estimate leaves out, hold the rest. The voxels in blocks of 1M are unchanged (their dense tables fit), 2.5 times the baseline, short of 3: their time after the compiled pass is merging the blocks' groups and the float32 mean's exact pass. The README and CHANGELOG say so. The suite: 3,777 passed.
