Exact totals (2026-10-28, by the owner's choice: built, labelarray not released): the voxels' exact pass came from ties, not cancellation: 21,470 of 533,985 voxels (4%) held a float32 mean on a tie between two float32 values, which no error bound decides, though every voxel's float64 sum of its float32 intensities was exactly the true sum (checked against fractions, 335,036 voxels of 1M points). Running the exact pass on compiled codes was tried and dropped: a block's compiled rows took 27 ms against NumPy's 25, both placing three `cells` keys for every point. Instead: labelarray (unreleased, 0.1.7) gains `sums.EXACT`, a `TERMS` value saying a total is the exact sum, which `chunk_estimate` never sets; `sums.merge`, which combines pieces as before and keeps the mark only where adding the two totals was exact too (Knuth's two-sum error); and `settle`, which rounds a marked result from its total directly (`_from_total`, through `_rounded`), so the ties settle with no exact sums. tidy3a's kernel takes each addition's error term, which Neumaier's step computes exactly, and marks a group none of whose additions rounded; the estimate's merge uses `sums.merge`, and a group a table has not held yet is filled with an exact zero (`Total.fill`). With labelarray 0.1.6 nothing is marked and the exact pass runs as before; the results are the same bits either way. Tests: labelarray (`tests/test_sums.py`, 830 passed): `_from_total` against fractions for 6,100 totals, odd 53-bit mantissas, divisors to 2**34, halves, and subnormal results; 500 tie means unsure by the bound and settled when marked, as `reduce` gives them; the merge rule. tidy3a (`tests/test_fused_driver.py`): pairs of adjacent float32 values over six blocks on three threads, compiled with no exact pass, NumPy's run asking for one, the same bits as NumPy's and as in memory; and with labelarray's mark hidden, the exact pass as before. `numpy_run` now runs its frames with compiling off, as a frame runs only when asked: three comparisons had run compiled on both sides, and now compare with NumPy. The tests that count the exact pass's work run with `TIDY3A_COMPILED=0`. The suite: 3,781 passed. Measured on the 12-core machine with the local labelarray: the voxels in memory 2.70 s on one thread and 1.386 s on twelve (NumPy 4.33 s and 3.16 s), 4.4 times the 6.15 s before milestone 15 (target 3); from the `.npy` file 1.43 s; with `blocks="auto"` at `memory="1GB"` 3.36 s (6.42 s before), at `"4GB"` 2.04 s (3.05 s); the rings unchanged (0.098 s); the same bits as NumPy's run everywhere.

**Exit (2026-10-28, amended): met, once labelarray 0.1.7 is released.** The rings summary 9.1 times faster (target 4), the voxels 4.4 times (target 3), the daytime summary 23 times; the same bits as the NumPy path for counts, integers, extremes, flags, float32 sums, and medians, on any number of threads, with the GIL and without; importing loads no Numba. With labelarray 0.1.6, as published, the voxels stay at 2.5 times. Left open: the voxels' peak over a 1 GB budget with `blocks="auto"` (1.31 GB), from their groups, which the estimate leaves out; old kernels in the cache; grids on one thread.

Release 0.10.0 status (2026-10-28): prepared by the owner's request, a minor release for milestone 16: a summary of data read in blocks whose every step compiles runs each block in compiled kernels with `fast` (Numba), falling back a block at a time, its plan and what ran shown by `repr` and `explain()`; groups too many for dense tables sorted, still compiled; exact float32 totals settled by labelarray from the total, with no exact pass; kernels kept on disk (`~/.cache/tidy3a/kernels`); free-threaded Python 3.14 tested with the GIL off; and the notebook layer's name fixes from tidy3. It requires labelarray 0.1.7 (published the same day). `scripts/check.sh --parallel` on exactly the release's contents, on the 12-core machine, in 271 s: Python 3.11 to 3.14 each 3,815 passed and 10 skipped; free-threaded 3.14 3,762 passed with the GIL off; minimum NumPy, pandas, and xarray 3,480 passed and 71 skipped; the source distribution, metadata, and the wheel plain, with xarray, and with fast. A first run minutes after labelarray's upload failed to set up two jobs, as uv did not yet see 0.1.7 on PyPI's index; it passed once the index listed it.

### Milestone 17 — Milestone 16's open items

Planned (2026-10-28), from what 0.10.0 left open, each looked into first:

1. **The merge tree in the budget.** The voxels with `blocks="auto"` at `memory="1GB"` peaked at 1.31 GB: the merge tree holds one merged table per level, and with many small blocks its upper levels each hold nearly every group (on 10M points in 381 blocks, up to 70 MB in 7 tables, the largest of 529,701 of the 533,985 voxels). The tree's shape must follow the block numbers alone, so the same bits come on any number of threads, so it cannot adapt to the groups as it goes. Decided: the estimate counts what the tree may hold, at most one table per level, each bounded by the positions its blocks cover, so `blocks="auto"` prefers fewer, larger blocks and fewer levels; where that proves too pessimistic, a note on the result when the merged groups pass the budget's share.
2. **The kernel cache's size.** Nothing removed old kernels (the fused tests alone leave 86, 7.5 MB). Decided: the folder is held to `TIDY3A_KERNELS_MAX` (1 GB by default); writing a new kernel removes the least recently used, a kernel's module and Numba's files together, and loading one touches its module, so use is recorded; `fused.cache.clear()` empties it.
3. **Grids on one thread.** The daytime summary took 0.33 s compiled on one thread against 0.094 s on NumPy: the kernel adds a position's 64,800 values one at a time through two compensated sums with branches, which without `fastmath` (it would change the bits) cannot use the vector units NumPy's reductions use. Accumulators per position alone did not help (2.95 ms a block against 2.64 for the loop of one sum and one extreme alone); four lanes made 1.80 ms; NumPy's main steps take 0.75 ms. Decided: where a summary's positions hold many values, a compiled kernel computes each position's values, NumPy's reductions take each position first, as the NumPy path does, and the compiled grouping takes the rest, so one thread comes near NumPy's and twelve keep the compiled scaling.

Accepted (2026-10-28) by the plan's owner, all three as above.

Amended (2026-10-28, by the owner's choice): item 1's bound did not hold up: one table per level, each at most the positions its blocks cover, sums to about twice the data's positions whatever the block size, so it would not steer `blocks="auto"` to larger blocks and would refuse every budget. Instead the merge tree is capped (`chunked.TOP`, 5): a table of 32 blocks merges into the running table of every block before it, so at most six tables are held, one of nearly every group; the shape still follows the block numbers alone. Measured as a prototype on 10M points in 381 blocks of the voxel summary: at most 65 MB held against 115 MB, 2.26 s against 2.98 s on one thread, 1.33 s against 1.34 s on twelve; the same bits as the binary counter for the counts and float32 means, and on one thread and twelve.

Steps 1–3 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. (1) `_Tree.add` merges a table reaching `TOP` into the running one; tests (`tests/test_merge_tree.py`): with `TOP` at 1 and 2, two keys over 82 blocks hold at most `TOP + 1` tables and give the same bits on 1, 2, 3, and 8 threads. (2) `fused.cache.limit()` reads `TIDY3A_KERNELS_MAX` through `sizing.budget`; `prune` removes the least recently used kernels (the module's time, which loading sets) until the folder holds at most the limit, never the kernel just written, and runs when one is; `clear()` removes the kernels and nothing else; a module loaded from another folder is no longer reused for this one, which had left the new folder without Numba's files. Tests (`tests/test_fused_cache.py`): five kernels pruned to three by their use; clear; the limit as bytes or text. (3) Where a summary's positions hold at least `BY_POSITION` (256) values, a compiled block places its keys and numbers its combinations as before, a `values` kernel computes the kept positions' values, and `chunked._partial_rows`, the NumPy path's own, reduces each position first and makes the pieces; on one block of 48 grids of 180 x 360, 1.89 ms against 6.99 ms. Tests (`tests/test_fused_driver.py`): grids of 400 values a position, every reduction, on one thread and three, NumPy's bits, and memory's for the exact kinds. The suite: 3,792 passed.

Measured (2026-10-28) on the 12-core machine, after steps 1–3, every result NumPy's run's bits: the daytime summary of 2,000 grids in memory 0.085 s on one thread (0.328 s before, NumPy 0.093 s), 0.055 s on two, 0.048 s on four, 0.058 s on eight, and 0.066 s on twelve (0.039 s before, NumPy 0.067 s): NumPy's reductions of each position, and the values the kernel writes for them, are held by memory bandwidth as NumPy's steps were, so the step gives back the scaling the fused kernel had past four threads. From the NetCDF4 file 0.198 s on one thread (0.393 s before) and 0.136 s at best; from the Zarr store 0.305 s and 0.167 s, the files' reading setting the pace. The voxels with `blocks="auto"` on twelve threads at `memory="1GB"` 3.23 s and a peak of 1.08 GB (3.36 s and 1.31 GB before), still 8% over the budget, and at `"4GB"` 2.06 s and 1.97 GB (3.05 s and 2.08 GB); in blocks of 1M, 1.38 s on eight threads, as before. The rings unchanged (0.101 s on twelve).

By thread count, where safe (2026-10-28, by the owner's choice): a summary of positions of many values runs NumPy's reductions of each position below `KERNEL_THREADS` (8) threads and the fused kernel from there, where every reduction's result is free of the order of its additions (`ORDER_FREE`: float32 sums and means, correctly rounded; integer sums; extremes; flags; the median's counts), so the result is the same bits on any number of threads; a float64 sum or mean, an integer mean, or a spread keeps NumPy's reductions on every number. An extreme taken by NumPy's reductions has its zero signed by the kernel's order (`fused.reductions.zeros_ordered`): only a group whose extreme is a zero can differ, so only its values are looked at, and both paths give the same extremes on x86, where NumPy's vector loops give a tie of -0.0 and +0.0 whichever they meet second. Python's bytecode of a kept kernel, which the machine writes beside Numba's files, is pruned and cleared with them. Tests (`tests/test_fused_driver.py`): an order-free summary of 400-value positions, a group's extremes on zeros of both signs among them, on 1, 3, 8, and 12 threads, NumPy's reductions below eight and the kernel from eight, the same bits on every number, the zeros' tie to -0.0 and +0.0; a float64 sum on NumPy's reductions on eight threads. On the 12-core machine, the daytime summary in memory: 0.083 s on one thread, 0.056 s on two, 0.052 s on four, 0.050 s on eight, and 0.039 s on twelve (NumPy 0.125 s and 0.066 s in the same run); 160 fused tests passed there, on x86, and the suite on the Mac 3,794.

The voxels' last 8% (2026-10-28): not tidy3a's allocations but glibc's. On the Mac, the voxels of 10M points with `memory="1GB"` on twelve threads allocated at most 0.24 GB (`tracemalloc`, every NumPy and Python allocation) above 0.30 GB of imports and kernels, while the process peaked at 1.17 GB; on the 12-core machine, the 40M points peaked at 1.10 GB by default, 0.70 GB with `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=2` (3.34 s against 3.27 s), 0.77 GB with four pools, 0.53 GB with glibc's large-block cut-off fixed at 128 kB (4.13 s), and 0.99 GB at best returning free memory with `malloc_trim` every eight blocks (3.71 s). Limiting the pools only during a run, as the owner chose first, cannot be done: glibc keeps the limit it first applies for the rest of the process, so setting the default back leaves two pools (25 threads allocating made 2, not 25), and a limit set once the pools exist does nothing (25 stayed 25). Decided by the owner: document it. The README's paragraph on `blocks="auto"` says the budget counts the verbs' allocations, not what the allocator keeps, and recommends `MALLOC_ARENA_MAX=2` in Python's environment on Linux for runs on many threads under a tight budget; tidy3a does not touch the allocator. macOS keeps freed memory too, and has no such setting.

Release 0.11.0 status (2026-10-28): by the owner's request, a minor release for milestone 17: the merge tree capped at 32 blocks a table (float64 sums and means, integer means, and spreads may differ in their last bits from 0.10.0's past 32 blocks; still the same bits on any number of threads), the kernel cache held to `TIDY3A_KERNELS_MAX`, positions of many values reduced by NumPy's reductions below eight threads and by the fused kernel from eight where the results are order-free, and glibc's memory pools documented. `scripts/check.sh --parallel` on exactly the release's contents, on the 12-core machine, in 275 s: Python 3.11 to 3.14 each 3,828 passed and 10 skipped; free-threaded 3.14 3,775 passed; minimum NumPy, pandas, and xarray 3,484 passed and 71 skipped; the source distribution, metadata, and the wheel plain, with xarray, and with fast.

### Milestone 18 — More that compiles

Planned (2026-10-28), proposed for the owner. Today one step the compiler does not take sends a whole summary to NumPy: `exp`, `log`, and the trigonometric functions (decision 1 of milestone 16 kept them there), powers other than 2, 0.5, -1, and 1, `//` and `%`, `ceil`, `sign`, and the rest, and time keys. Measured on the 12-core machine, 40M points in blocks of 1M or 2,000 grids in blocks of 48: the rings weighted by `exp(-d / 20)` with the mean of `log(abs(intensity) + 1)` took 1.079 s on one thread and 0.524 s on twelve, on NumPy; the same without `exp` and `log`, compiled, 0.712 s and 0.100 s. The daytime summary with the saturation vapour pressure, `6.112 * exp(17.67 c / (c + 243.5))`, took 0.213 s and 0.181 s on NumPy; without `exp`, compiled, 0.139 s and 0.041 s.

Why not compile them: a kernel's `exp`, `log`, `sin`, and the rest are the C library's, or LLVM's, and NumPy's are its own vectorized routines, chosen by the CPU's features. On a million values each, a Numba kernel's results differed from NumPy's in up to 39% of float32 values on x86 (`exp`) and 24% on ARM (`sin`); and NumPy 2.5.4 differed from itself between the Mac (ARM) and the 12-core machine (x86) on the same inputs, within 3 ulp, in up to 42% of values (`tan` float64; float32 `exp` 39%, float64 `sin` 4%). So no kernel matches NumPy's bits on every machine but NumPy itself; the promise is NumPy's results in the same environment (the same NumPy, the same CPU features). (Even NumPy's float64 random numbers from one seed differed in their last bits between the two.) Correctly rounded functions, the same on every machine, would be a numerical feature of their own, for eager and compiled runs alike, and are not proposed here (the owner's review, 2026-10-28).

Proposed decisions:

1. **NumPy islands.** A step the compiler cannot match to NumPy's bits but that is elementwise along the blocked axis (a function, a power, `//` or `%` on floats) is computed by NumPy for each block, the largest such part of an expression as one island, its operands too (`log(x + y)` is NumPy's `x + y` and then its `log`; a definition from an earlier `mutate` is put in place, as the compiler does), and the kernel reads its values as an input. So the result is NumPy's bits, as on the NumPy path. What an island cannot be: half-precision or complex values (no kernel holds them), times and text, and anything that is not elementwise. `repr` and `explain()` name the islands ("planned run: a compiled kernel a block, with NumPy for exp(-distance / 20)").
2. **Compiled exactly, not as islands:** `ceil`, `trunc`, `sign`, `rint`, and `fabs` (exact in IEEE arithmetic); `//` and `%` on integers, with NumPy's rules (floored, the divisor's sign, 0 for a zero divisor); and on floats, NumPy's own steps (`npy_divmod`: `fmod`, then the sign's and the floor's corrections), checked bit for bit against NumPy over special values as the expressions' tests are.
3. **Time keys:** a `datetime64` or `timedelta64` field and its `bins`, `cells`, and `rounded` of durations, placed as the integers they are held in, their labels as NumPy gives them; values of times stay on NumPy.
4. **Memory:** an island is an array a block per island, which `sizing` counts per position, as it counts NumPy's steps.
5. **Measured,** and the path chosen by measurement where it differs, as milestone 17 found for grids: an island writes an array and reads it again, which may give back some of the fused kernel's scaling; where it does, the path follows the number of threads only where the results allow, as for grids.

Exit (proposed): on the 12-core machine on twelve threads, the rings with `exp` and `log` at least 2.5 times faster than NumPy's 0.524 s (0.21 s or less), and the daytime summary with the vapour pressure at least 2 times faster than its 0.181 s (0.09 s or less); NumPy's bits in both, and in every island test; a summary with a time key compiles; importing tidy3a loads no Numba.

Accepted (2026-10-28) by the plan's owner: all five decisions and the exit, as proposed.

Steps: 1. Islands in the compiler and planner (`Island` sources, evaluated per block by the driver before the kernel). 2. The exact functions, and `//` and `%`. 3. Time keys. 4. Sizing of islands. 5. Measured, and the path by threads where it pays. 6. README and CHANGELOG.

Step 1 status (2026-10-28): done, staged for review. `fused.expressions.Compiler` takes a step it cannot match to NumPy's bits but that is elementwise, a function of `ISLANDS` (`exp`, `log`, `log2`, `log10`, the trigonometric and hyperbolic functions and their inverses, `arctan2`, `ceil`, `sign`, `real`, `imag`, `angle`, `is_in`), `//`, `%`, or a power NumPy does not compute exactly, as an `Island`: an input the kernels read, the same island once however often it is read. Where its operands compile, a small kernel computes them for each block (`driver.operand_kernels`), with NumPy's bits, as IEEE arithmetic rounds the same everywhere and the kernels fuse no multiply and add, and NumPy applies only the function (`APPLIED`: `np.exp`, `np.floor_divide`, `np.power`, ...), a Python number staying one beside an array as NumPy takes it; else NumPy evaluates the island whole in the block's frame after the `mutate` steps it reads, run again by NumPy (`Island.steps`, from the definitions, which now hold their step and place). A first build evaluated every island whole: the rings with `exp` and `log` took 0.226 s at best (eight threads) and the daytime summary 0.174 s, NumPy making again the distance and the temperature in Celsius and a temporary array for each operation; with the operands compiled, 0.159 s and 0.075 s. What an island cannot be stays on NumPy, with its reason: a result of half precision, complex values, times, or text; `conj`, `dot`, and the rest. The planner names the islands (`Fusion.islands`; "planned run: a compiled kernel a block, with NumPy for exp(-distance / 20), log(abs(intensity) + 1)"). Tests: the compiler's (`tests/test_fused_expressions.py`): eight island expressions among every operation over 11 pairs of types and special values, NumPy's bits; the islands of a cube, `exp`, `//`, `%`, and `exp(x + y)`; an integer to the power 0.5; `conj` refused. The planner's: `exp`, a cube, `//`, `%`, and `exp` of a variable an earlier `mutate` made, as islands. The driver's: `exp`, `sin`, `log`, a cube, `//`, and `%` in filters, a mutate, and reductions, on one thread and three, NumPy's bits. The suite: 3,806 passed.

Measured (2026-10-28) on the 12-core machine, against NumPy's 0.524 s and 0.181 s on twelve threads: the rings with `exp` and `log` 0.838 s on one thread, 0.233 s on four, 0.159 s on eight, 0.163 s on twelve (3.2 times; exit 2.5); the daytime summary with the vapour pressure 0.215 s on one, 0.147 s on four, 0.078 s on eight, 0.075 s on twelve (2.4 times; exit 2). Islands' memory is not yet counted (step 4).

## 11. Verification and release strategy

Prioritize meaningful checks rather than copying tidy3's entire test matrix:

- Raw import round-trips and precise layout/label validation.
- Results matching direct NumPy/xarray calculations for independent numerical checks.
- Axis permutation and coordinate-label tests, including exact alignment failures.
- Point association surviving filtering and arithmetic; untouched intensity/ring values.
- A synthetic known rotation/translation with points below, at, and above the 50 m boundary.
- Grouped temporal summaries matching explicit expected values.
- Complex operator application without accidental conjugation or real-only coercion.
- Formula/native-expression agreement and clear index-binding failures.
- Installed-package smoke tests and notebook/script behavior.

Run fast unit and synthetic integration tests on pull requests. Keep large-data benchmarks, optional-format checks, and downloaded dataset exercises in separate jobs. Cache dependencies and record timings. Use one publication gate for the tested commit/artifacts, a focused installation smoke check, and trusted publishing where configured. Do not rerun unrelated tidy3, plot3, or stats3 suites for every tidy3a release.

The current approximately 20-minute tidy3 publication time is user-reported context, not a diagnosed bottleneck. Measure tidy3a's build/test times before choosing performance targets. Separating packages isolates release cycles; it does not by itself fix slow jobs.

## 12. Questions for the reviewer to resolve first

Questions 1–7 are resolved; the decisions are in the sections cited and summarized in §2. Question 8 is open.

1. Resolved: selection uses the field-axis rule, with `variable()`/`label()` for collisions and tidy3's selection helpers (§5.1); milestone 0 still tests field-axis select against stars-style strict select.
2. Resolved: `group_by` axes are kept and other observation axes reduced; grouping by values and `bins()`; a variable lacking the grouping axis is an error (§5.4).
3. Resolved: exact matching with reorder by label; joins via `left_join`/`inner_join`/`full_join`; `over=` names summed axes, so batch axes are kept (§5.5, §6.1–6.2).
4. Resolved: `read_array` returns the wrapper directly; `tidy()` wraps existing xarray objects (§4.1).
5. Resolved: plot3's conventions, subscripts as indices, `bind=`, `\sum_i` as the main form, `\mathrm{i}`, and required show-back (§6.3).
6. Resolved: tidy3a copies and adapts tidy3's `masking.py` and owns the copy; each transformer rewrites only calls it owns, which needs a tidy3 change (§5.6).
7. Resolved: first-release scope in milestone 7 (§10).
8. Open: may the Polars grouping engine (milestone 8) give results that differ from NumPy in the last digit, and if so, is it on by default when Polars is installed or only when asked for?

Parameter spelling, extra aliases, and additional domain helpers can wait until these examples are understood and pass their acceptance checks.

## 13. Prior art

Earlier attempts to make array programming friendlier fall into five groups. Items marked † were recalled rather than checked against a source during this review; verify them before citing.

| Group | Projects | Lesson for tidy3a |
|---|---|---|
| Tidy verbs on arrays | **stars** (R): dplyr verbs `filter`, `mutate`, `select`, `rename`, `pull`, `slice(along=)` on spatiotemporal arrays; `split`/`merge` between dimensions and variables†. **tbl_cube/cubelyr** (R): dplyr's own array type, moved out of dplyr at 1.0 for low use†. **tidyterra**† and **tidync**† (R) | Layers built for one data community (rasters) succeeded; the general-purpose one did not. Use stars as the reference design for remaining verb questions (slicing by position, grouping over dimensions) |
| dplyr ports in Python | dppd, pipda, pydplyr, siuba, dfply, plydata, dplython | All target pandas; no dplyr-style layer for xarray was found, so the gap is real. pipda's per-type verb registration is a pattern to consider |
| Named axes | **xarray**; **Haliax** (JAX, after Rush's *Tensor Considered Harmful*); PyTorch named tensors (remained a prototype†); DimensionalData.jl, AxisKeys.jl, NamedDims.jl†; **rray** (R broadcasting, stopped at 0.1.0) | Named axes are widely seen as the friendlier model; verb layers on top of them are rare |
| Index notation | **Tullio.jl** (`@tullio A[i,j] += B[i,k,l] * C[l,j]`), Einsum.jl, OMEinsum.jl; einops† (Python), TensorCast.jl† | Friendly array math names its indices explicitly; this supports the array layer's `over=` and formula index conventions. Tullio is the closest precedent for `formula()` |
| Array query languages | kdb+/q† (`select … by … from` in an array language), rasdaman/rasql†, SciDB†, ISO SQL/MDA†; pandas `Panel`, removed in favor of xarray† | Query verbs and array programming can coexist; forcing N-dimensional data into a table-shaped API did not last |

Implications carried into this plan: the two-layer split (§1.1), the `over=` convention (§6.1), stars as the reference for open verb questions, and choosing a home community, such as sensor and point-cloud data, for examples and outreach while keeping the core general.

Sources checked: [stars dplyr methods](https://search.r-project.org/CRAN/refmans/stars/html/dplyr.html), [stars tidyverse vignette](https://r-spatial.github.io/stars/articles/stars3.html), [stars arithmetic](https://r-spatial.github.io/stars/reference/ops_stars.html), [stars c()](https://r-spatial.github.io/stars/reference/c.stars.html), [dppd](https://dppd.readthedocs.io/), [pipda](https://pypi.org/project/pipda/), [pydplyr](https://pypi.org/project/pydplyr), [rray](https://rray.r-lib.org/), [Tullio.jl](https://docs.juliahub.com/Tullio/PIgzC/0.2.9), [Julia einsum comparison](https://github.com/JuliaPackageComparisons/JuliaPackageComparisons.github.io/blob/main/docs/comparisons/math/einsum.md), [Haliax](https://pypi.org/project/marin-haliax/0.2.5.dev202606020954/).

### 13.1 Backend review (2026-10-24)

Decided: keep xarray, over NumPy, as the array backend. The original plan took xarray as its starting point without comparing alternatives, so after the first-release preparation the candidates were reviewed for labels, dependencies, maintenance, and speed. Only xarray and Scipp provide what the tidy layer is built on: coordinate labels on axes, alignment by label, and grouping. xarray adds NetCDF/Zarr readers, Dask and Cubed, and the largest user base; on gridded data, every array library measured within noise of NumPy.

| Candidate | Labels on axes | Dependencies | Status (2026-10) | Conclusion |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **xarray** | Coordinates, alignment, groupby | NumPy, pandas, packaging | Very active | Keep as the backend |
| **Scipp** | Coordinates, plus physical units, uncertainties, binned event data | NumPy only (C++ core) | Active, 26.9.0 (2026-09-22) | Possible interop target (below) |
| **PyTorch** | Names only, no labels; the named tensor API is documented as experimental, and binary operations broadcast by position from the right, checking names rather than aligning by them (`align_as()` is explicit) | 526 MB installed, 0.56 s import | Array API tracker ([pytorch#58743](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/58743)) open since 2021; 2.14.1 tensors lack `__array_namespace__`, so xarray converts them to NumPy | Not a backend; revisit when xarray can wrap tensors |
| **JAX** (Haliax, Penzai) | Names and sizes only | About 240 MB plus SciPy | Haliax active; Penzai last released April 2025 | Not a backend; JAX arrays already work inside xarray |
| **named-arrays** (sun-data) | Names only: strict by-name broadcasting, reductions, and indexing; no coordinate labels | xarray, astropy, SciPy, matplotlib, numexpr, and smaller packages | Active, 2.16.0 (2026-10-21) | Not a backend: no labels, no group-by, no collection of variables with different axes; depends on xarray |
| **xarray `NamedArray`** | Names only, no labels | Inside xarray; not released separately | Unfinished as a public API | Not usable: no arithmetic operators and no indexing by name in xarray 2026.9.0 |
| **Awkward Array** | Field names, no coordinates | NumPy plus a C++ core | Active, 2.14.0 | For ragged records, not grids |
| **Iris** | Coordinates under strict CF conventions | Cartopy, Dask, netCDF4, SciPy, … | Active | Climate-specific; narrower than xarray |
| **Polars, DuckDB, pandas** | Tables, not axes | About 170 MB / small / 44 MB | Active | Right for tidy3's tables; gridded data needs one row per cell. Polars becomes an optional engine and a bridge (milestone 8) |

Measurements on an Apple M4 (synthetic data, best of several runs, milliseconds; ratios matter more than absolute values):

| | Rotate + 50 m filter, 10M points | Mean per ring, 10M points | Mean over time, 14.6M cells | Anomaly, 14.6M cells |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NumPy | 145 | 58 | 1.3 | 3.8 |
| xarray | 167 | 2,279 (no flox) | 1.4 | 4.2 |
| tidy3a | 198 | 147 | 1.6 | 4.4 |
| PyTorch CPU / MPS | 71 / 47 | 221 / 463 | 1.9 / 1.4 | 4.1 / 4.9 |
| JAX (jit) | 23 | 1,028 | 1.1 | 3.1 |
| Scipp | 58 (float64) | 9 with binning; 6,496 with `groupby` | 1.2 | 3.3 |
| named-arrays | 68 (one array per component) | 219 (one mask per ring; no group-by) | 0.9 | 2.9 |
| Polars | 72 | 7 | 161 (long table) | 212 (long table) |
| DuckDB | 72 | 14 | 42 (long table) | 338 (long table) |
| pandas | 133 | 50 | 242 (long table) | 539 (long table) |

A 64 × 1024 × 1024 by 1024 × 1024 contraction took 95–100 ms in NumPy and xarray, 91 ms with PyTorch on the CPU, 59 ms with PyTorch on the GPU (MPS), and 133 ms with JAX. Tables need about 7 times the memory of the array for gridded data. The tidy3a value-grouping time above includes the 2026-10-24 speedup (milestone 6).

Consequences:

- Speed comes from optional pieces that xarray already accepts, not from changing backend: flox or numbagg for grouping, Dask or Cubed for parallel and larger-than-memory work, and JAX arrays inside xarray for compiled or GPU computation. Checked with xarray 2026.9.0 and JAX 0.12: selection, arithmetic, reductions, boolean indexing, `dot`, and groupby keep JAX arrays, and most tidy3a verbs run on them unchanged; field write-back and `dot` with a NumPy matrix return NumPy. xarray's documentation does not yet list JAX among supported array types, so this is not yet a promise.
- PyTorch becomes an option in the same way once its tensors implement the Array API protocol; follow [pytorch#58743](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/58743).
- Scipp is a possible interop target rather than a replacement: `tidy()` could accept a Scipp `DataArray` or `Dataset` if physical units or binned point-cloud data become central, mapping dimensions and coordinates and carrying units into metadata. Its binning API, which grouped 10M points in 9 ms, is a reference for faster value grouping. Scipp itself describes xarray as the more mature choice for many applications, and its homepage does not cover lazy or larger-than-memory data.
- Strict named arrays were checked separately, since the first search covered only machine-learning named-axis libraries. named-arrays, tested at 2.16.0, enforces names strictly: shapes are dictionaries, broadcasting is by name only, and mismatched lengths on one name are an error. It supports astropy units, uncertainties that propagate, 3-D vector types, complex and `uint8` values, and keeps Dask arrays lazy; JAX arrays were converted to NumPy. It has no coordinate labels, no group-by, and no container for variables with different axes, which the field axis, `filter`'s kept positions, exact alignment, joins, and grouping all need, and it depends on xarray. Its strictness matches what tidy3a's array layer already enforces with `over=` and formula indices; its units and uncertainties are ideas shared with Scipp. xarray's own `NamedArray` (2026.9.0) cannot yet add two arrays or multiply one by a number, so it is a building block inside xarray rather than an option.
- Performance item, not yet scheduled: the rotate-and-filter example takes 82–85 ms in tidy3a at 10M points in the project environment, while the same computation on separate contiguous float32 columns takes 44 ms in plain NumPy (named-arrays: 68 ms). The same tidy3a pipeline took 169–198 ms in the benchmark environment with identical xarray 2026.9.0 and NumPy 2.5.4 but many more packages installed. Explained in milestone 9 step 5: `opt_einsum`, which JAX installed there, made xarray's `dot` itself faster but return column-major results, which doubled the time of every later step; tidy3a now always uses NumPy's `einsum`. Likely tidy3a overheads, to confirm by profiling: `dot` over a strided `[x, y, z]` view of the stored `point × feature` array, and two `mutate` steps that store intermediate variables before the filter.
- A NumPy-only core was prototyped after this review (`prototypes/`, not imported by `src/`); it is under evaluation, not decided.
  - Design: an immutable `Variable` (data, dims, attrs) borrows any array with NumPy's interface; labels live once per axis on a `Dataset` and are shared by every variable on that axis; one dispatcher turns axis names into axis numbers, applies tidy3a's alignment rules (exact labels, reorder by label with a note, labelled never pairs with unlabelled, 1-based positions attached before cutting), calls NumPy, and builds the result's metadata. A `LabelIndex` per label array is built once and cached; labels are frozen when stored so the cache cannot go stale. It searches strictly increasing labels directly with `np.searchsorted` and others through one sort; kinds (booleans, numbers, times, durations, text, bytes) never match across each other; numbers and times match only when a lookup converts to the label's type exactly; one NaN or NaT label can be looked up; mixed-type labels are an error naming the operation. It agrees with pandas' `get_indexer` on 25 boundary cases and 56 randomised label sets, except two deliberate differences: repeated labels raise tidy3a's error, and integers beyond float64 precision compare exactly (pandas matches `2**53` to the label `2**53 + 1`). An experimental `NumpyTidyArray` runs tidy3a's own expressions, formula parser, and verb constructors on the core, through private hooks in `src/` (verbs record their operation and arguments; `TidyArray._apply_verb`; formula compilation asks the context instead of xarray).
  - Results (Apple M4, same inputs, milliseconds): §7 rotate-and-filter at 10M points 66 against tidy3a's 84; at 34,688 points 0.30 against 1.26; mean per ring at 10M points 112 against 135; gridded mean and anomaly level with NumPy; import 47 against 224 ms. The demo's whole pipeline (rotate, formula distance, filter, grouped mutate, summarise) at 10M points: 135 against tidy3a's 128, with bit-identical results. The core's gains came from memory layout (views for consecutive labels, a contiguous `dot` result, scanning a mask once), which no backend requires. Finding groups needs pandas' hashing to be fast (110 ms; 545 ms with NumPy's sort), and indexing unsorted labels takes 839 ms at 10M against pandas' 343 ms; sorted labels index in 5.5 ms and cached lookups take 0.012 ms, as in pandas.
  - Coverage: run with only its reader swapped in, the pipeline passes 267 of tidy3a's 379 tests. Of the 112 failures, about 55 are out of its scope (lazy and Dask input, joins, `bins()`, selection helpers, `bind_cols`); about 25 are error messages without tidy3a's hints; about 14 are the inspection printout; about 10 are the milestone 6 metadata rules; a few are behaviour: grouped float means differ in the last digit when the grouped axis is not first, a notebook example with a NumPy rotation matrix fails, and two checks (`rename_axes` misuse, `dot` without `over=`) differ.
  - Lessons: the pipeline implements every verb a second time, which is where most differences come from; dependencies did not shrink, since fast grouping and unsorted indexes want pandas and lazy input and file formats want xarray; and each correctness detail (time units, NaN, mixed types, layouts) was found only by comparing against an independent reference. Milestone 9 takes these into account.
- No Python package offers dplyr-style verbs on labeled N-D arrays; the tidy3a gap identified in §13 remains.

Sources checked: [PyTorch 2.10 named tensor operator coverage](https://docs.pytorch.org/docs/2.10/name_inference.html), [pytorch#54581](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/54581), [pytorch#58743](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/issues/58743), [Reviving Named Tensors (2026)](https://dev-discuss.pytorch.org/t/reviving-named-tensors-strategy-for-addressing-the-experimental-backlog/3289), [Scipp](https://scipp.github.io/), [Scipp paper](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2010.00257), [xarray duck arrays](https://docs.xarray.dev/en/stable/user-guide/duckarrays.html), [xarray NamedArray design](https://github.com/pydata/xarray/blob/main/design_notes/named_array_design_doc.md), [array-api#698](https://github.com/data-apis/array-api/issues/698), [Penzai named axes](https://penzai.readthedocs.io/en/stable/notebooks/named_axes.html), [Levanter 1.0 and Haliax](https://levanter.readthedocs.io/en/latest/Levanter-1.0-Release/), [Awkward Array](https://awkward-array.readthedocs.io/en/main/getting-started/what-is-an-awkward-array.html), [Iris and xarray](https://scitools-iris.readthedocs.io/en/latest/community/iris_xarray.html), [named-arrays](https://github.com/sun-data/named-arrays), PyPI metadata for each package.
